## p.3 — Page 3

[pdf-2e2d435b755a4090ca | p.3] azInternational Centre for Island Technology, School of Energy, Geoscience, Infrastructures and 86
[pdf-7f233804bc3531eb6f | p.3] Society, Heriot-Watt University, Stromness, Orkney, KW16 3AN, United Kingdom 87
[pdf-c81757b2c48ad39bad | p.3] Abstract 88
[pdf-af081db0db54694ef5 | p.3] The ocean plays a central role in modulating the Earth’s carbon cycle. Monitoring how the ocean carbon cycle is changing is fundamental to managing climate change. Satellite remote sensing is currently our best tool for viewing the ocean
[pdf-e019ecbf0abd807a53 | p.3] surface globally and systematically, at high spatial and temporal resolutions, and
[pdf-25a38cbe4fdb203e9f | p.3] the past few decades have seen an exponential growth in studies utilising satellite data for ocean carbon research. Satellite-based observations must be combined with in-situ observations and models, to obtain a comprehensive view of ocean
[pdf-d098050fbfad0e9101 | p.3] carbon pools and ﬂuxes. To help prioritise future research in this area, a workshop
[pdf-992c90a19e46605aaa | p.3] was organised that assembled leading experts working on the topic, from around the world, including remote-sensing scientists, ﬁeld scientists and modellers, with the goal to articulate a collective view of the current status of ocean carbon research, identify gaps in knowledge, and formulate a scientiﬁc roadmap for the
[pdf-08ea6af4fc4c2bebf4 | p.3] next decade, with an emphasis on evaluating where satellite remote sensing may
[pdf-53bd04543dee82de17 | p.3] contribute. A total of 449 scientists and stakeholders participated (with balanced gender representation), from North and South America, Europe, Asia, Africa,
[pdf-83d47b92c852d724e8 | p.3] and Oceania. Sessions targeted both inorganic and organic pools of carbon in the ocean, in both dissolved and particulate form, as well as major ﬂuxes of carbon between reservoirs (e.g., primary production) and at interfaces (e.g., air-sea and
[pdf-ed067a097c139d19b9 | p.3] land-ocean). Extreme events, blue carbon and carbon budgeting were also key top-
[pdf-35bcb08cce4c780634 | p.3] ics discussed. Emerging priorities identiﬁed include: expanding the networks and
[pdf-15c785f54a4870e9a1 | p.3] quality of in-situ observations; improved satellite retrievals; improved uncertainty
[pdf-b295bc77f311925858 | p.3] quantiﬁcation; improved understanding of vertical distributions; integration with
[pdf-434d42f0ace51f98ec | p.3] models; improved techniques to bridge spatial and temporal scales of the diﬀerent
[pdf-b95612319fc86c7cfc | p.3] data sources; and improved fundamental understanding of the ocean carbon cycle,
[pdf-93ef82b1c2c71a3e64 | p.3] and of the interactions among pools of carbon and light. We also report on priori- ties for the speciﬁc pools and ﬂuxes studied, and highlight issues and concerns
[pdf-9d2b88b16b26c02aa0 | p.3] 3

## p.4 — Page 4

[pdf-4648d1f950ca6fb121 | p.4] that arose during discussions, such as the need to consider the environmental impact of satellites or space activities; the role satellites can play in monitoring ocean carbon dioxide removal approaches; economic valuation of the satellite
[pdf-d790be6333e741021e | p.4] based information; to consider how satellites can contribute to monitoring cycles of other important climatically-relevant compounds and elements; to promote
[pdf-c765e7e76821a3176d | p.4] diversity and inclusivity in ocean carbon research; to bring together communities working on diﬀerent aspects of planetary carbon; to follow an open science ap- proach; to explore new and innovative ways to remotely monitor ocean carbon;
[pdf-1e61d9545d7569d623 | p.4] and to harness quantum computing. Overall, this paper provides a comprehensive scientiﬁc roadmap for the next decade on how satellite remote sensing could help monitor the ocean carbon cycle, and its links to the other domains, such as terrestrial and atmosphere.

## p.7 — Page 7

[pdf-fc709c86562b0118bf | p.7] 1. Introduction 157
[pdf-78dff5322e2fd0fde2 | p.7] The element carbon plays a fundamental role in life on Earth. Owing to its 158
[pdf-221ce46e19b9b95972 | p.7] ability to bond with other atoms, carbon allows for variability in the conﬁguration 159
[pdf-4119705f03810f30a9 | p.7] and function of biomolecules such as deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) and ribonu- 160
[pdf-90534f27c520fcc91d | p.7] cleic acid (RNA) that control the growth and replication of organisms. Carbon is 161
[pdf-e88fe0264598aff9a6 | p.7] constantly ﬂowing through every sphere on the planet, the geosphere, atmosphere, 162
[pdf-2af5be4f3285f7c678 | p.7] biosphere, cryosphere, and hydrosphere, in liquid, solid or gaseous form. This 163
[pdf-3e0af5fd0eb19e4193 | p.7] ﬂow of carbon is referred to as the Earth’s carbon cycle. It comprises of diverse 164
[pdf-76ef3618d253497b4e | p.7] chemical species, organic and inorganic, and many processes responsible for 165
[pdf-e529a83f433e409f91 | p.7] transformations and ﬂow of carbon among the diﬀerent reservoirs. Although 166
[pdf-69542eca59fe220b51 | p.7] the total amount of carbon on Earth is relatively constant over geological time, 167
[pdf-acf31c97c36856ddae | p.7] the carbon content of the component spheres and reservoirs can change, with 168
[pdf-093821cfa4c9885d87 | p.7] profound consequences for the climate of the planet. Since the establishment 169
[pdf-373d0dd05bf91bf8be | p.7] of the industrial revolution at the start of the 19th century, humans have been 170
[pdf-8405887171cf583562 | p.7] increasing the carbon content of the atmosphere through the burning of fossil 171
[pdf-7526adf09f553a7cf0 | p.7] fuels and land use changes, trapping outgoing long-wave radiation in the lower 172
[pdf-8903ea518b58cc6792 | p.7] atmosphere and increasing the temperature of the planet. 173
[pdf-2eb982229948c7430c | p.7] This anthropogenic increase in atmospheric carbon (in the gaseous form of 174
[pdf-616a705026b9a99314 | p.7] CO2) has three principal fates: it can remain in the atmosphere, be absorbed 175
[pdf-587b00bbc07d9b60b5 | p.7] by the ocean, or be absorbed by vegetation on land. Estimates for the year 176
[pdf-65cb605636d56c9278 | p.7] 2020 suggest that just under half of the anthropogenic CO2 emissions currently 177
[pdf-f045d091f00d6bf3cc | p.7] released (10.2±0.8 Gt C yr−1) remain in the atmosphere (5.0±0.2 Gt C yr−1), with 178
[pdf-07ab2c6bd388824dfe | p.7] just over a quarter being absorbed by the land (2.9±1.0 Gt C yr−1) and by the 179
[pdf-17d27c7525aa486259 | p.7] ocean (3.0±0.4 Gt C yr−1) (Hauck et al., 2020; Friedlingstein et al., 2022). Our 180
[pdf-9c1e264a4c80a48a4a | p.7] ocean therefore plays a major role in regulating climate change. Understanding 181
[pdf-aad6e2b55047aa1a45 | p.7] what controls the trends and variability in the ocean carbon sink is consequently a 182
[pdf-da5f8831fa76f80b7f | p.7] major question in Earth Science. Recent work from the Global Carbon project 183
[pdf-2a9becb3d64a3f68e9 | p.7] suggests estimates of this sink from models (by which we mean to be 3-D, 184
[pdf-a135cb839e016d3608 | p.7] prognostic, process-based models) are not in good agreement with observational- 185
[pdf-fc87ae44d967147247 | p.7] based evidence (Friedlingstein et al., 2022). Never has it been so urgent to improve 186
[pdf-d92651ab3bc3800894 | p.7] our understanding of the ocean carbon cycle. 187
[pdf-4a94c770989a17d812 | p.7] 7

## p.8 — Page 8

[pdf-a48f6713359f2383aa | p.8] Monitoring the ocean carbon cycle is key to improved understanding. His- 188
[pdf-de55b8eed464677403 | p.8] torically, ocean carbon cycle reservoirs and ﬂuxes were monitored using in-situ 189
[pdf-20efffa6996e86c47c | p.8] methods, collecting data from ship-based platforms (dedicated research cruises 190
[pdf-ba4b2a85c800728175 | p.8] and ships of opportunity), moorings and time-series stations (Karl and Winn, 191
[pdf-19fae7b667feb283f2 | p.8] 1991; Raitsos et al., 2014; Bakker et al., 2016; Olsen et al., 2016). Since the 192
[pdf-45049ecd776c786028 | p.8] 1970’s satellite observations have been used (Gordon et al., 1980; Shutler et al., 193
[pdf-4f871ec8778403a901 | p.8] 2019; Brewin et al., 2021) and recent years have seen the expansion of ocean 194
[pdf-da6c5b06d0a25580b4 | p.8] robotic platforms for monitoring ocean carbon cycles (Williams et al., 2015, 2017; 195
[pdf-2a9a0052f038f741fb | p.8] Gray et al., 2018; Chai et al., 2020; Claustre et al., 2020, 2021), both aiding the ex- 196
[pdf-03104ef12b83425e54 | p.8] trapolation of local in-situ measurements to global scale. Each of these platforms 197
[pdf-54b53fbb2b55e42fa7 | p.8] have advantages and disadvantages, and it is commonly accepted that an approach 198
[pdf-728c94855d1518f913 | p.8] integrating data from all platforms is required. There is also a need to use coupled 199
[pdf-8a10eb47dbc7ba4756 | p.8] physical and biogeochemical modelling, with the in-situ and satellite data, to 200
[pdf-8b35c83ac9faaad6e9 | p.8] estimate the pools and ﬂuxes of carbon that are diﬃcult to measure otherwise, at 201
[pdf-976afc36c0430429d2 | p.8] the required temporal and spatial scales. 202
[pdf-b27735cb7814f620d9 | p.8] Satellites play a major role in our global carbon monitoring system. They are 203
[pdf-f4f0bf5291f6cb305f | p.8] the only platforms capable of viewing our entire surface ocean and the air-sea 204
[pdf-bf1161d5b7cb14e480 | p.8] boundary layer synoptically, at high temporal resolution. Consequently, the use 205
[pdf-2b98f7bb48d3c7a4b7 | p.8] of satellites in ocean carbon research has been expanding exponentially over the 206
[pdf-6d1134fdcf13dc1364 | p.8] past 50 years (Figure 1a). However, satellite instrumentation can only view the 207
[pdf-63a72d29ca8ba2609d | p.8] surface of the ocean (the actual depth the signal represents varies with wavelength 208
[pdf-7d32a345ef26731053 | p.8] and water composition), are constrained to operate in certain conditions (e.g., 209
[pdf-d7c200bb7f91973f28 | p.8] passive visible systems are limited to cloud-free conditions and low to moderate 210
[pdf-86d7fa256fee794da6 | p.8] sun-zenith angles) and at certain spatial and temporal scales, and are limited to 211
[pdf-d061a6491b78095bd9 | p.8] collecting information that can be contained in electromagnetic radiation. To 212
[pdf-11c8c2b1dbf60a6a56 | p.8] make full use of satellite observations for ocean carbon monitoring the remote- 213
[pdf-28d6eba2bcedf94fb6 | p.8] sensing community needs to work closely with in-situ data experts, physical and 214
[pdf-8a7429dda63d4aa14b | p.8] biogeochemical modellers, Earth system scientists, climate scientists and marine 215
[pdf-d50b9b577bf3c3c40e | p.8] policy experts. 216
[pdf-f1007c7fe9ef612fd2 | p.8] With this in mind, the European Space Agency (ESA) with support from 217
[pdf-c2ed900190c49c67ed | p.8] the US National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA), organised a 218
[pdf-a0eb053cd53285affd | p.8] 8

## p.9 — Page 9

[pdf-2f2c8c17bc276fe4e7 | p.9] virtual workshop called "Ocean Carbon from Space" in February 2022, building 219
[pdf-0b88ecb1daedd40bd8 | p.9] on a successful workshop organised in 2016 (Colour and Light in the ocean from 220
[pdf-985d470a48c8901261 | p.9] Earth Observation; Sathyendranath et al., 2017a; Martinez-Vicente et al., 2020), 221
[pdf-6fda5f4604a41034eb | p.9] and ﬁndings from a wide range of international initiatives (e.g., NASA EXport 222
[pdf-dd44cbf4ffc3ea4bb9 | p.9] Processes in the Ocean from Remote Sensing (EXPORTS), ESA Ocean Science 223
[pdf-b43ad39ce02604d099 | p.9] Cluster, ESA Climate Change Initiative (CCI), various European Commission 224
[pdf-08a471d5debc90d5c9 | p.9] Carbon Initiatives (e.g. Copernicus, such as the Ocean Colour Thematic Assembly 225
[pdf-56deb1694faa3b8e08 | p.9] Center (OC TAC) and the Multi Observations Thematic Assembly Center (MOB 226
[pdf-f1e9513b431495d625 | p.9] TAC), the Surface Ocean Lower Atmosphere Study (SOLAS), the Blue Carbon 227
[pdf-de0961da2598ba219c | p.9] Initiative, the Global Carbon Project, International Carbon Observing System1). 228
[pdf-443b5703f93eb79203 | p.9] The workshop was also part of the Committee on Earth Observation Satellites 229
[pdf-2b562ef3cbb3dca4d2 | p.9] (CEOS) workplan on Aquatic Carbon (CEOS, 2021). The theme of the workshop 230
[pdf-57be4a8bf4e16acaf0 | p.9] was on ocean carbon, its pools and ﬂuxes, its variability in space and time, and the 231
[pdf-bde83767def17f1515 | p.9] understanding of its processes and interactions with the Earth system. The goal 232
[pdf-21af0341b44bbf0e59 | p.9] of the workshop was to bring leading experts together, including remote-sensing 233
[pdf-3e67affa0cbe1df771 | p.9] scientists, ﬁeld scientists and modellers, to describe the current status of the ﬁeld, 234
[pdf-bebba733a515956af5 | p.9] and identify gaps in knowledge and priorities for research. In this paper, we 235
[pdf-242c8aecd611a578d9 | p.9] synthesize and consolidate these discussions and produce a scientiﬁc roadmap 236
[pdf-e24546b38fc7094357 | p.9] for the next decade, with an emphasis on evaluating where and how satellite 237
[pdf-6311148fde0ce3eded | p.9] remote sensing can contribute to the monitoring of the ocean carbon cycle. With a 238
[pdf-11798cbaba56bad914 | p.9] growing human population that is dependent on the blue economy sectors (OECD, 239
[pdf-137a1423da3fa784ed | p.9] 2016), as well as climate, we envisage this roadmap will help guide future eﬀorts 240
[pdf-75da12b5bf8f393721 | p.9] to monitor ocean carbon from space. 241

[pdf-95ae3569e4729cddca | p.9] 9

## p.10 — Page 10

[pdf-975fe06c77f8972dab | p.10] 2. Workshop details and approach to capture collective view of the status of 242
[pdf-40c2a7c9b7d7acf77d | p.10] the ﬁeld 243
[pdf-b5d9e69ec3590109ab | p.10] 2.1. Ocean Carbon from Space Workshop 244
[pdf-b0fd1de27a9927c6cb | p.10] The "Ocean Carbon from Space Workshop" (https://oceancarbonfromspace2022. 245
[pdf-1668ff4004242de279 | p.10] esa.int/) was organised by a committee of 15 international scientists, led by ESA 246
[pdf-c5d97ca7dfc23a3739 | p.10] within the framework of the Biological Pump and Carbon Exchange Processes 247
[pdf-10cc427b5faba7d9df | p.10] (BICEP) project (https://bicep-project.org) with support from NASA. In addition 248
[pdf-a5a6757d5577e50d3c | p.10] to this organising committee, a scientiﬁc committee of 31 international experts on 249
[pdf-ae5d23a99b150c6223 | p.10] the topic of ocean carbon were assembled, who helped structure the sessions and 250
[pdf-948f783a087c379320 | p.10] review abstracts. These committees initially proposed a series of sessions, target- 251
[pdf-559ea820e3ad892424 | p.10] ing 16 themes, covering: the pools of carbon in the ocean (including particulate 252
[pdf-bbcfd48756e4cbd83a | p.10] organic carbon, phytoplankton carbon, particulate inorganic carbon, dissolved 253
[pdf-47fec7b1ad2da64cb8 | p.10] organic carbon, and carbon chemistry, including dissolved inorganic carbon); 254
[pdf-529873a28c20eb8310 | p.10] the main processes (including marine primary production, export production, 255
[pdf-76c06ce55226f90465 | p.10] air-sea exchanges, and land-sea exchanges); and crosscutting themes (including 256
[pdf-ddbf591854c061c0e3 | p.10] the underwater light ﬁeld, uncertainty estimates, freshwater carbon, blue carbon, 257
[pdf-398b6b2bfe0785d68b | p.10] extreme events, tipping points and impacts on carbon, climate variability and 258
[pdf-2a4936c2a80de811d6 | p.10] change, and the ocean carbon budget). 259
[pdf-8db8d835bebb8eaa70 | p.10] The workshop was widely advertised, through a variety of means, including: 260
[pdf-14f31a10a395610196 | p.10] email distribution lists; through international bodies like the International Ocean 261
[pdf-4d7025a023d8308761 | p.10] Colour Coordinating Group (IOCCG) and SOLAS networks; space agencies; 262
[pdf-a24ed3bb5d81b5dcbd | p.10] and through social media platforms. Scientists and stakeholders working in the 263
[pdf-ceebc8801dfa9d13af | p.10] ﬁeld of ocean carbon were invited to submit abstracts to the 16 themes and to 264
[pdf-156c63ca965288fc17 | p.10] participate in the workshop. The organising committee also identiﬁed key experts 265
[pdf-235080c11384c9346f | p.10] in the ﬁeld who were invited to give keynote presentations. 266
[pdf-27c914a40c27e9f4dc | p.10] A total of 98 abstracts were submitted to the workshop, and based on the 267
[pdf-eea57ae3ce44eb355a | p.10] topics of these abstracts, the workshop was organised into six sessions combining 268
[pdf-4282e2026dc337b340 | p.10] various themes as needed, and covering: 269
[pdf-5a29541869a414bb36 | p.10] • Primary Production (PP), 270
[pdf-fedaec6bea609f2211 | p.10] • Particulate Organic Carbon (POC), 271
[pdf-73f05679afb920afc4 | p.10] 10

## p.11 — Page 11

[pdf-15cf1dd8c027186c56 | p.11] • Phytoplankton Carbon (C-phyto), 272
[pdf-b228ce49a2bc57a783 | p.11] • Dissolved Organic Carbon (DOC), 273
[pdf-8916b3831bb25cfc50 | p.11] • Inorganic Carbon and ﬂuxes at the ocean interface (IC), 274
[pdf-0c69e7a17be0749df7 | p.11] • Cross-cutting themes with three sessions; 275
[pdf-01569512f61ead59cb | p.11] – Blue Carbon (BC), 276
[pdf-a6227b944c2bf5e955 | p.11] – Extreme Events (EEs), 277
[pdf-4c8085f81fffafad8b | p.11] – Carbon Budget Closure (CBC). 278
[pdf-8e94273e88457a2b30 | p.11] The organisation committee identiﬁed chairs for each session, and abstracts were 279
[pdf-42df83ecf20ade0580 | p.11] reviewed by the organisation and scientiﬁc committees and assigned to oral or 280
[pdf-8f2d53234570ca3553 | p.11] e-poster presentations. E-poster presentations were delivered through breakout 281
[pdf-c1406f26645f60d50b | p.11] rooms to help promote discussions. Each session included keynote speakers, oral 282
[pdf-c02b5d75fbdc9af578 | p.11] presentations and importantly, time for discussing gaps in knowledge, priorities, 283
[pdf-74700663ed88c602df | p.11] and challenges. There were four poster sessions covering the six themes of the 284
[pdf-ec55a829035e478772 | p.11] workshop. Participants were encouraged to upload their presentations or e-poster 285
[pdf-e7b68fa380392c790f | p.11] (under the form of a 1-3 slides presentation) prior to the conference start to 286
[pdf-15f15883bf59d5c005 | p.11] facilitate knowledge exchange and prepare for workshop discussions. 287
[pdf-79784fb5f06170b661 | p.11] The workshop took place from 14th to 18th February 2022, following the 288
[pdf-c712245317604da86a | p.11] international day of women and girls in science. Due to COVID restrictions, 289
[pdf-9f52ac64b03747bccb | p.11] an online format was preferred (using the webex video conferencing software; 290
[pdf-86a300f27113e0b726 | p.11] https://www.webex.com), which resulted in a ﬂexible schedule and programme 291
[pdf-b0240cabecd6b54c16 | p.11] designed to accommodate participants from diﬀerent regions and time zones, 292
[pdf-bf1343cb1a87748051 | p.11] and ﬂexible working (e.g., child care responsibilities). A total of 449 people 293
[pdf-974acb0d9a593af086 | p.11] from a wide geographical spread (Figure 1b) participated. Gender was not asked 294
[pdf-be713ee89ef6c46acd | p.11] at registration for privacy concerns, but interpretation of registered participants 295
[pdf-97ee3b5098e42e5a18 | p.11] suggested around 47 % were female and 53 % male (Figure 1c; acknowledging 296
[pdf-526580151177b3ea8c | p.11] not everyone identiﬁes as female or male), reﬂecting an increasing participa- 297
[pdf-234891a55771ae04a7 | p.11] tion of female scientists in ocean carbon science. Gender balance is important, 298
[pdf-7d8452e295de788036 | p.11] as it has been shown that scientiﬁc research is more accurate when gender is 299
[pdf-34cd0cddd5b611bcca | p.11] 11

## p.12 — Page 12

[pdf-4e6e1b790b4b657452 | p.12] considered, that research teams are more likely to come up with new ideas and 300
[pdf-b5a2d328db3c2ead8d | p.12] perspectives, and that at present, men signiﬁcantly outnumber women in the sci- 301
[pdf-403cb4fcc7cee9038b | p.12] ence, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) workforce (Bert, 2018). 302
[pdf-847756220fa223fb85 | p.12] Orcutt and Cetini´c (2019) discuss gender balance in oceanography and provide 303
[pdf-0d192db8bef1498378 | p.12] ten useful recommendations on how we can progress towards better gender bal- 304
[pdf-9015087e0b9c11a270 | p.12] ance. More broadly, increased diversity promotes innovation, productivity, critical 305
[pdf-b5810eab4bdc5d068c | p.12] thinking, creativity, communication, social justice and sustainability (Phillips, 306
[pdf-369fe4007f338c0275 | p.12] 2014; Johri et al., 2021). Given the importance of improving diversity in Earth 307
[pdf-73e224386ace119618 | p.12] Sciences, particularly in oceanography where problems have persisted (Garza, 308
[pdf-66f27ae8322f2578ea | p.12] 2021), more members of under-represented groups are needed in the study of the 309
[pdf-48de24c34ff1ed3fa9 | p.12] ocean carbon cycle. Eﬀorts such as Unlearning Racism in Geosciences (URGE; 310
[pdf-666aade3c42b5d9d43 | p.12] https://urgeoscience.medium.com/) and public celebrations of diversity (e.g., 311
[pdf-6048799fe9819b1aae | p.12] Royal Society celebration of Black science, see https://royalsociety.org/topics- 312
[pdf-4f3a89f958e9408a0c | p.12] policy/diversity-in-science/a-celebration-of-black-science/) will help in this re- 313
[pdf-f35e3d49d7672fdc40 | p.12] gard, but more eﬀort is needed. 314
[pdf-dffb167d35ea3f519f | p.12] 2.2. Tools and approaches to capture collective view 315
[pdf-ef92354fdc06993fc7 | p.12] A series of tools and approaches were used to capture the collective view of 316
[pdf-37a36bf0e5d6df9e8f | p.12] the community and identify the major gaps, challenges, and priorities, that fed 317
[pdf-00f7a1978b2bd5e9be | p.12] into this scientiﬁc roadmap. 318
[pdf-b21375380d57ec2313 | p.12] Firstly, session chairs were asked to prepare statements on the main scientiﬁc 319
[pdf-d849c7961a5e77bb88 | p.12] challenges, gaps, and opportunities of their session theme, prior to the start of 320
[pdf-efe7c1ffda1d215200 | p.12] the conference. All presenters (e-poster and oral) were also asked to include one 321
[pdf-30de2fb2a27dfdd762 | p.12] slide about knowledge gaps and priorities for next steps on their work over the 322
[pdf-51daf07d47924bde8b | p.12] next decade. These statements were then used by session chairs to help structure 323
[pdf-b616916a2f44d4194b | p.12] the discussion slot organised at the end of each session. A ﬁnal discussion session 324
[pdf-65df0280581e12ea01 | p.12] was held at the end of the workshop, whereby all session chairs were asked to 325
[pdf-f99a86cd2c7e4e8372 | p.12] join a panel to identify overarching themes. 326
[pdf-da01107b9836f86310 | p.12] All sessions were recorded through Webex. Throughout the workshop, we 327
[pdf-620db5e7bcce472fa2 | p.12] used Padlet software (https://en-gb.padlet.com), a cloud-based, real-time collabo- 328
[pdf-631d4fb5c0fb351594 | p.12] rative web platform which allowed participants to interact and upload thoughts 329
[pdf-b3f03fa8dae3211f92 | p.12] they had on the scientiﬁc challenges, gaps, and opportunities for each session, 330
[pdf-1b324c28b484f16206 | p.12] 12

## p.13 — Page 13

[pdf-d982547cc5f5e8088a | p.13] comment on those suggested by the chairs and other participants, all within virtual 331
[pdf-e773ade1c28479e5d2 | p.13] bulletin boards called "padlets". Following the closure of the workshop, session 332
[pdf-11e78dca8d06a9ded7 | p.13] chairs were asked to provide a written synthesis of the main outcome of their 333
[pdf-c2c26b11a01b1dbd2e | p.13] sessions. 334
[pdf-5e7327ccc79ba99c76 | p.13] All scientiﬁc priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities identiﬁed and 335
[pdf-4bbc943520b60cfd44 | p.13] discussed during the workshop, were organised into: 336
[pdf-3d280d007a5064513a | p.13] • Session-speciﬁc themes, 337
[pdf-1e8e50f3e0e201e578 | p.13] • Common themes, 338
[pdf-12b1c4503d18541a5a | p.13] • Emerging concerns and broader thoughts. 339
[pdf-760d3c6b2b7ca74313 | p.13] For the reader wanting to focus on recommendations for the entire subject, we 340
[pdf-f91e615bf05b4347b4 | p.13] suggest you go to Section 5 and 6 of the paper. Table 1 provides an overview of 341
[pdf-6aa653b4ec910de858 | p.13] the session-speciﬁc themes of the paper and a guide to navigate this scientiﬁc 342
[pdf-91697d6328dae81a7a | p.13] roadmap, and Table 2 provides a selection of recently launched and upcoming 343
[pdf-5bdf791deebd90e541 | p.13] satellite sensors with applications in ocean carbon research and monitoring. 344
[pdf-9627f124f6e6f66cb2 | p.13] 3. Session-speciﬁc theme outcomes 345
[pdf-52b253d3935d5c9ae7 | p.13] In the following sections, we begin by providing a brief description of each 346
[pdf-b4ecc3195e1555a7d5 | p.13] session-speciﬁc theme, then brieﬂy highlight the current state of the art, and ﬁnally 347
[pdf-e458d2124dc49aa53a | p.13] focus on the identiﬁed priorities, scientiﬁc challenges, gaps, and opportunities, to 348
[pdf-7dd4a3b7c91116b17c | p.13] be targeted over the next decade. We deﬁne these terms according to: 349
[pdf-7588bdfb497f0d624a | p.13] • Priority: Something that is considered very important and must be dealt 350
[pdf-1b305a3db2b25d88e6 | p.13] with before other things, 351
[pdf-8f94fc9bb47a941996 | p.13] • Challenge: Something that requires great eﬀort to be achieved, 352
[pdf-412def676e3f33eba2 | p.13] • Gap: Something lacking or missing and required to make progress, 353
[pdf-bd677d01865bd2d589 | p.13] • Opportunity: A situation that makes it possible to make progress. 354
[pdf-d2973a797e45640ab2 | p.13] 13

## p.14 — Page 14

[pdf-adfda1c4d556ddd1cd | p.14] 3.1. Primary production (PP) 355
[pdf-f1a7dad275b7fa14bb | p.14] Primary production (photosynthesis) channels energy from sunlight into ocean 356
[pdf-a733c86f6c75ef75e9 | p.14] life, converting DIC, in the form of CO2, into phytoplankton tissue (e.g., C-phyto) 357
[pdf-3a5646df54ed40637f | p.14] that then fuels ocean food webs. In discussions about the role of phytoplankton in 358
[pdf-cd46184053dcc5abdb | p.14] the carbon cycle, it is useful to consider the diﬀerent components of PP. Carbon 359
[pdf-7b374ace3d07e51c75 | p.14] ﬁxed through photosynthesis, before any loss terms are detected, is referred to as 360
[pdf-1652a3e103d7430b6c | p.14] gross PP. When phytoplankton respiratory losses are subtracted from gross PP, we 361
[pdf-2fdf5f8647ed6eb963 | p.14] get net PP. When all the losses to PP required to meet the metabolic requirements 362
[pdf-ca96e36126d3e29c73 | p.14] of the entire community are taken away, then we are left with net community 363
[pdf-456aa7c42d7f241e01 | p.14] production. It is also common practice to partition PP into new production (i.e., 364
[pdf-74a7f5c7c29d0ab006 | p.14] PP driven by allochthonous nutrient input), and regenerated production (i.e., PP 365
[pdf-7df4c978633805095d | p.14] sustained by locally available nutrients), with the sum of the two yielding gross 366
[pdf-145957aa28dfa0c021 | p.14] PP. It is often diﬃcult, if not impossible, to match these exact theoretical and 367
[pdf-cc4df477b9d7bf36ad | p.14] conceptual deﬁnitions with practical observations, because of the limitations 368
[pdf-38769a8f24e6781573 | p.14] of the tools available. But, when dealing with estimates of PP from carbon 369
[pdf-6450bd7d9a6a7f72b9 | p.14] incubation techniques, it is generally accepted that short incubations of about 370
[pdf-c8280cbc3c6491af52 | p.14] one hour are close to gross PP, whereas longer incubations of one day are close 371
[pdf-59815754daf9ea22eb | p.14] to net PP. If we adopt this operational deﬁnition, then PP calculations that are 372
[pdf-0588c7c82ef95525d9 | p.14] based on photosynthesis-irradiance experiments carried out over periods of one 373
[pdf-ae52db8d6fc5c87484 | p.14] or two hours, are treated as gross PP (especially since these measurements are 374
[pdf-0132703da4163b1fbc | p.14] typically corrected for dark respiration measured during the experiment), and PP 375
[pdf-30f5eea59a11d19e12 | p.14] measurements that extend over a whole day (24 hours) approach net PP. 376
[pdf-f5a0a81f34df545562 | p.14] On the other hand, PP estimated, often indirectly, over seasonal time scales 377
[pdf-82751044437a8d0a30 | p.14] are close to new production. It is also common in the literature to discuss export 378
[pdf-2896938e529801bc2a | p.14] production, which is that component of PP that is transported below a particular 379
[pdf-3a51e1f02653f8b944 | p.14] depth horizon deep in the water column, and thereby removed from the oceanic 380
[pdf-7ac5e14c84c23ca8a5 | p.14] mixed layer, and hence isolated from interactions with the atmosphere. Export 381
[pdf-0102fe9cb6ff78d21c | p.14] production and new production are sometimes treated as being equivalent to 382
[pdf-78e19fa1a97b649386 | p.14] each other, but in reality, the depth horizon used for computations of export 383
[pdf-d4a5acb9648de0e871 | p.14] production is relevant to discussions of time scales that are applicable, before the 384
[pdf-087260ecc67ddf7c41 | p.14] exported production, or the regenerated carbon and nutrients associated with that 385
[pdf-2aed52e48f5e087025 | p.14] 14

## p.15 — Page 15

[pdf-47739955d39e73d01d | p.15] production, reappears at the surface. The deeper the depth horizon, the longer the 386
[pdf-cc8897034b240d730a | p.15] time scale of isolation. The time scale associated with that component of export 387
[pdf-ae0b4b38c4a8858501 | p.15] production that reaches the bottom of the water column and gets buried there, is 388
[pdf-3cd22082882275ad26 | p.15] of the order of millions of years. 389
[pdf-03300ac90fb68c958a | p.15] Total net PP is approximately the same on land and in the ocean (∼ 390
[pdf-059bc29c7a5f5e11c1 | p.15] 50 Gt C yr−1; Longhurst et al., 1995; Field et al., 1998; Bar-On et al., 2018). 391
[pdf-621890e5d32548cf96 | p.15] By removing CO2 from surrounding waters, PP lowers the ambient CO2 concen- 392
[pdf-f49c6785ab00ef9cac | p.15] tration in surface waters, which can potentially lead to a drawdown of CO2 from 393
[pdf-ca93bdda68f3aa1b1e | p.15] the atmosphere. In doing so, PP can inﬂuence climate. The magnitude of any 394
[pdf-662e01d0a3035954d6 | p.15] climate eﬀect of PP depends, however, on the fate of the phytoplankton produced 395
[pdf-d405d95ebffab75049 | p.15] through PP. Only when the reduction in surface ocean pCO2 is maintained over 396
[pdf-ecd03469150379c7db | p.15] time can it lead to a lasting drawdown of CO2. In practice, PP can only have a 397
[pdf-6b56222239b87c11b0 | p.15] long-term impact on climate when its products are removed from surface waters 398
[pdf-14be1253877641b9a8 | p.15] through the ocean’s organic carbon “pumps” (Volk and Hoﬀert, 1985; Boyd et al., 399
[pdf-9d0c9e6c27410b22b6 | p.15] 2019). The “biological pump”, whereby organic material is transported to below 400
[pdf-9915168942c3d91b29 | p.15] the permanent thermocline is largely driven by “new” production (Dugdale and 401
[pdf-7ce000c0d24944efdb | p.15] Goering, 1967), i.e., PP driven by allochthonous nutrient input (which is sensitive 402
[pdf-a9aed87c2196bf741c | p.15] to stoichiometry and nutrient availability). To quantify the eﬀect of ocean PP in 403
[pdf-6a4873227b619244d3 | p.15] global carbon cycling and, thereby, climate development, there is therefore a need 404
[pdf-63c002b772b8d9c7b9 | p.15] to develop mechanisms to diﬀerentiate between total (gross) and new PP in the 405
[pdf-58fed9bfd593c0016a | p.15] ocean (Brewin et al., 2021). 406
[pdf-9f42a1738e74028c1a | p.15] 3.1.1. State of the art in PP 407
[pdf-c7132ef51abeea5ac8 | p.15] Satellite algorithms of PP have a long-established history, dating back over 408
[pdf-28f9b59e550ebceab5 | p.15] 40-years, to the time when the ﬁrst ocean-colour satellite (the Coastal Zone Color 409
[pdf-4e790557e0d8e70a85 | p.15] Scanner, CZCS) became available (Smith et al., 1982; Platt and Herman, 1983). 410
[pdf-00218c7679ce1ef7fe | p.15] Some initial attempts were made to convert ﬁelds of chlorophyll-a directly into 411
[pdf-7f8056ad1e9d4b7675 | p.15] PP (Smith et al., 1982; Brown et al., 1985; Eppley et al., 1985; Lohrenz et al., 412
[pdf-2add097b0bbc2038b8 | p.15] 1988), before approaches based on ﬁrst principles were established, utilising 413
[pdf-03959c768185797c45 | p.15] in addition to information on chlorophyll-a concentration, information on bulk 414
[pdf-15fcac0a9e4a99aa31 | p.15] and spectral light availability (now available through satellite Photosynthetically 415
[pdf-8497f9ee9c45fb2b55 | p.15] Available Radiation (PAR) products), on the response of the phytoplankton to 416
[pdf-93c8ed13eea6c287be | p.15] 15

## p.16 — Page 16

[pdf-16c08cd2a67fc191ca | p.16] the available light (parameters of the photosynthesis-irradiance curve), and en- 417
[pdf-622c8e93bbf39f2203 | p.16] vironmental data such as day length (e.g., Platt et al., 1980; Platt and Herman, 418
[pdf-a03b410dbbe764f14e | p.16] 1983; Platt et al., 1990; Platt and Sathyendranath, 1988; Sathyendranath and Platt, 419
[pdf-b726f94d8ed13d361b | p.16] 1989). The ﬁrst global estimates were computed in the mid-1990’s (Longhurst 420
[pdf-baecabbbfae08be47f | p.16] et al., 1995; Antoine et al., 1996; Behrenfeld and Falkowski, 1997a), arriving at 421
[pdf-ea63c359f8c204025b | p.16] values of around 50 Gt C y−1, consistent with current estimates (Carr et al., 2006; 422
[pdf-1c3f3f3d1a87cdb7e9 | p.16] Buitenhuis et al., 2013; Kulk et al., 2020, 2021). Whereas many of the modern 423
[pdf-82e54f039a476d905a | p.16] techniques can diﬀer in implementation, they have been shown to conform to the 424
[pdf-4f9e725c434a4b7026 | p.16] same basic formulation, with the same set of parameters (Sathyendranath and 425
[pdf-0c8354d1c9b68065e5 | p.16] Platt, 2007), with some going beyond total PP, and partitioning it into diﬀerent 426
[pdf-7de101770a269e3619 | p.16] phytoplankton size-classes (e.g., Uitz et al., 2010, 2012; Brewin et al., 2017b). 427
[pdf-57774a796a6c07e1ab | p.16] For a review of these approaches, the reader is referred to the classical works 428
[pdf-4632c9f2ab0eb8a049 | p.16] of Platt and Sathyendranath (1993), that of Behrenfeld and Falkowski (1997b), 429
[pdf-59353f5fe4e763e9ff | p.16] Sathyendranath and Platt (2007), Sathyendranath et al. (2020), Section 4.2.1. of 430
[pdf-08a67a72c88c851142 | p.16] Brewin et al. (2021), and the recent review of Westberry et al. (2023). For a review 431
[pdf-293b0286c0a5d98e54 | p.16] of operational satellite radiation products for ocean biology and biogeochemistry 432
[pdf-ac7bdf929bb5acebe6 | p.16] and a roadmap for improving existing products and developing new products, 433
[pdf-48e7dc14e95de5bec6 | p.16] see Frouin et al. (2018). The reader is also referred to the huge eﬀorts made by 434
[pdf-2c4f57819b1b8bdd02 | p.16] NASA over the past 20 years to evaluate and improve these satellite algorithms 435
[pdf-7d205c2aa9c9e75f0c | p.16] (Campbell et al., 2002; Carr et al., 2006; Friedrichs et al., 2009; Saba et al., 2010, 436
[pdf-bd0342b270026aeb73 | p.16] 2011; Lee et al., 2015). The process of evaluating remote sensing algorithms 437
[pdf-02af4bf64a27fdb9fd | p.16] with in-situ data is frequently referred to as "validation" in the remote sensing 438
[pdf-8cc4a9adbb0a7c8f5d | p.16] community. NASA PP validation activities have highlighted variations in model 439
[pdf-8957f5ec55ba6f07d5 | p.16] performance with region and season (root mean square deviations of between 0.2 440
[pdf-7401e7c01fcf05228c | p.16] to 0.5 in log10 space, when compared with in-situ data), illustrating the importance 441
[pdf-7e19b99c936cf69966 | p.16] of minimising the uncertainties in model inputs and parameters, and in knowing 442
[pdf-493705b45261fd05bc | p.16] the uncertainties in the in-situ measurements used for validation. 443
[pdf-8d637e9caa513007f8 | p.16] Following presentations and discussions on PP at the workshop, ﬁve key 444
[pdf-4dc1a484b2f69320b3 | p.16] priorities were identiﬁed. These are summarised in Table 3 and include: 1) 445
[pdf-4c5c8dc2d8074289ca | p.16] parametrisation of satellite algorithms using in-situ data; 2) uncertainty estimation 446
[pdf-ba543dfe2e7526ac10 | p.16] of satellite algorithms and validation; 3) linking surface satellite measurements to 447
[pdf-4b045e2c1dd3b85a4d | p.16] 16

## p.17 — Page 17

[pdf-8406d58d3f008f9639 | p.17] the vertical distribution; 4) trends; and 5) fundamental understanding. 448
[pdf-3dfaead51da2acd38e | p.17] 3.1.2. PP priority 1: Parametrisation of satellite algorithms using in-situ data 449
[pdf-d559c41712699edf59 | p.17] Challenges: Considering that most satellite PP models conform to the same 450
[pdf-6acd4e756d5a814276 | p.17] principles (Sathyendranath and Platt, 2007), a major challenge to the research 451
[pdf-c7ad23982d7e34c412 | p.17] community is to improve our understanding of the spatial and temporal variability 452
[pdf-ddcf90850d53ef8ca3 | p.17] in the model parameters, which will be key to improving accuracy of satellite PP 453
[pdf-8a71e555e85c1a0eaf | p.17] models (Platt et al., 1992). The continuation of existing sampling campaigns and 454
[pdf-0576cb03eb90eb7c2c | p.17] expansion to under-represented regions, is subject to ﬁnancial support for in-situ 455
[pdf-c9b083cdb40380db7a | p.17] observations, particularly ship-based research cruises, considering that many PP 456
[pdf-f1bc371f69278fd3c8 | p.17] measurements require specialised equipment, not suitable for automation. Given 457
[pdf-ececa9921857f11ed2 | p.17] the declining ﬂeet of research vessels in many regions (e.g., Kintisch, 2013), new 458
[pdf-0e926eef45e84b83d3 | p.17] solutions are needed, with sustained funding. 459
[pdf-18d7bfedba0688a333 | p.17] Another challenge is that in-situ data on PP and model parameters are often 460
[pdf-12be99834895dbea87 | p.17] collected in a non-standardised way, with diﬀering conversion factors and proto- 461
[pdf-284f03d2a7bf81ee18 | p.17] cols, and diﬀering ancillary measurements, with limited information on the light 462
[pdf-2f94799a17f9c23add | p.17] environment, for both the experimental set-ups as well as the in-situ data (Platt 463
[pdf-f2893dc18246804e27 | p.17] et al., 2017). There are many ways PP can be measured (see Sathyendranath et al., 464
[pdf-815b66e32e0fb5cd60 | p.17] 2019b; Church et al., 2019; IOCCG Protocol Series, 2022), and to convert among 465
[pdf-6e55bc3f16045beff7 | p.17] methods is not straight-forward, especially considering methods measure diﬀerent 466
[pdf-6ff08beb5a9aba3a3d | p.17] types of PP (gross, net and new), though some studies have shown promise in this 467
[pdf-ef9f8ef4e8cc87a886 | p.17] regard (e.g., Regaudie-de Gioux et al., 2014; Kovaˇc et al., 2016, 2017; Mattei and 468
[pdf-2c17760b5cf4be05dd | p.17] Scardi, 2021). There is a clear challenge to develop better protocols and standards 469
[pdf-f59a6901d888faa7e7 | p.17] for PP data collection. Recent eﬀorts by the IOCCG have made some progress 470
[pdf-e95e8097fe9adc16fd | p.17] (IOCCG Protocol Series, 2022). 471
[pdf-38398608866beb91b0 | p.17] A further challenge with developing and validating satellite algorithms stems 472
[pdf-5bf08db4620a9d036f | p.17] from the fact that PP (a time varying rate) is estimated from an instant satellite 473
[pdf-081565979ad527b1a5 | p.17] snapshot in time. The time variability of PAR, biomass and the possible variability 474
[pdf-2d92545ca2fc79b602 | p.17] in photosynthetic parameters must be modelled. Meanwhile these all have diurnal 475
[pdf-635e90036102a7a9dc | p.17] variability. As a result of many of these challenges, satellite PP algorithms do not 476
[pdf-5089c0ef9c4779f616 | p.17] always agree with one another (Siegel et al., 2023). 477
[pdf-3dabc8212568f2f821 | p.17] Gaps: Although large eﬀorts have been made in recent years to compile 478
[pdf-5743d51906cc69ead4 | p.17] 17

## p.18 — Page 18

[pdf-c43a41e62bd8819da5 | p.18] global in-situ datasets of the parameters of the photosynthesis-irradiance curve 479
[pdf-4a03f205a078a884bb | p.18] (e.g., Richardson et al., 2016; Bouman et al., 2018), relatively few measurements 480
[pdf-2af3f627f4b94e217d | p.18] of photosynthesis-irradiance curve parameters exists globally, with many regions 481
[pdf-c4c9bb682ac44bc8f1 | p.18] (e.g., Indian Ocean, Southern Ocean and central Paciﬁc) being under-represented 482
[pdf-2433f2324f07b4e2f8 | p.18] (Kulk et al., 2020), and some hard to reach (e.g., Polar seas). Challenges to in-situ 483
[pdf-62f55a19379874fc81 | p.18] data collection (e.g. lack of adequate funding) and compilation have meant there 484
[pdf-6bf78dcf99d94465a5 | p.18] are very few stations with continuous in-situ measurements of PP and related 485
[pdf-ac6e0e10518746f82f | p.18] parameters. As the ocean colour time-series approaches a length needed for 486
[pdf-7cb61c347b277e298c | p.18] climate change studies (∼40 years; Henson et al., 2010; Sathyendranath et al., 487
[pdf-c98a36c635d3d60e5f | p.18] 2019a), this may impact our ability to verify climate trends in PP detected from 488
[pdf-90a89fd0db3fc2443c | p.18] space (see PP priority 5). There are gaps in coordination at the international 489
[pdf-8cc2a610b6e7e2d470 | p.18] level that if ﬁlled, would greatly beneﬁt the systematic and sustained collection 490
[pdf-8c8e962cefd6074f2e | p.18] of in-situ measurements on PP. Many remote sensing algorithms of PP rely on 491
[pdf-4ed343cb149dc21376 | p.18] a knowledge of photosynthesis-irradiance curve parameters. Consequently, the 492
[pdf-5ff8e1a8662087ff82 | p.18] algorithms are only as accurate as the coverage (both spatial and temporal) of 493
[pdf-7f691355f924eba8a0 | p.18] these in-situ parameters. They are also likely to be sensitive to climate change, so 494
[pdf-7391a57e862d04e034 | p.18] it is important to keep updating the in-situ databases. There is also a strong spatial 495
[pdf-516d5b4060cc92d67c | p.18] bias (North America and Europe) in existing estuarine in-situ PP measurements 496
[pdf-fa235f1a31840c8223 | p.18] (Cloern et al., 2014). 497
[pdf-077cbc03be4cb77691 | p.18] Opportunities: By capitalising on an expanding network of novel and au- 498
[pdf-1e2c5759364059c5a5 | p.18] tonomous in-situ platforms, there are opportunities to improve the quantity of 499
[pdf-1470efb527255cf777 | p.18] measurements of PP, by harnessing active ﬂuorescence-based methods (IOCCG 500
[pdf-60cd0fc4ae19776d9b | p.18] Protocol Series, 2022), such as Fast Repetition Rate (FRR) ﬂuorometry (Kolber 501
[pdf-bc6196f0798181f3b1 | p.18] and Falkowski, 1993; Kolber et al., 1998; Gorbunov et al., 2000) and Fluorescence 502
[pdf-1697c2e44274c6e2f0 | p.18] Induction and Relaxation (FIRe) techniques (Gorbunov et al., 2020). In fact, vari- 503
[pdf-9f67421ba400bcebc7 | p.18] able ﬂuorescence techniques are increasingly being used to assess phytoplankton 504
[pdf-97c8d65e1610cdbb0f | p.18] photosynthesis (see Gorbunov and Falkowski, 2020). There are challenges in 505
[pdf-a029f4113f7bdc7df2 | p.18] interpreting these data (Gorbunov and Falkowski, 2020), and diﬀerences between 506
[pdf-4ae47cc590ce1be506 | p.18] FRR and 14C PP can be large (Corno et al., 2006). However, as these are op- 507
[pdf-695b0a8099ff88cf3b | p.18] tical measurements that can be collected in real time, they are well suited to 508
[pdf-24a1b01fc5b726d034 | p.18] autonomous platforms (Carvalho et al., 2020). For a recent review on the topic see 509
[pdf-21efd02af580de54bf | p.18] 18

## p.19 — Page 19

[pdf-00657c5d06f6c8f96f | p.19] Schuback et al. (2021). Dissolved oxygen measurements, derived from oxygen 510
[pdf-8aa874442dc5fb1b2a | p.19] optode sensors on autonomous platforms, can be used to estimate and quantify 511
[pdf-4b0460576cfbe3a4ad | p.19] photosynthesis and respiration rates (Addey, 2022), as well as to quantify gross 512
[pdf-76dda2e50d93c0842b | p.19] oxygen production that can be used to constrain net PP estimates (Odum, 1956; 513
[pdf-4c23df785d68069119 | p.19] Barone et al., 2019; Johnson and Bif, 2021). Johnson and Bif (2021) used diurnal 514
[pdf-1a0cd80b03fdc35e18 | p.19] oxygen cycles from BGC-Argo ﬂoats to estimate global net PP at 53 Gt C yr−1, by 515
[pdf-b020f902c8f6860e58 | p.19] assuming a ﬁxed ratio of net to gross PP (as many net PP methods do). As high- 516
[pdf-3339e71e0aa09f8464 | p.19] lighted by the authors, the ratio of net to gross PP, however, varies considerably, 517
[pdf-4f6e78b8fb74681a18 | p.19] in ways that are poorly understood. The diurnal oxygen method has also seen 518
[pdf-b9485a38b398792e41 | p.19] extensive application in estuarine and other coastal waters (e.g., Caﬀrey, 2004). 519
[pdf-fea9251ac67ab8d2c8 | p.19] Such estimates require high temporal resolution sampling, to observe the entire 520
[pdf-ded25ab19bdfb3f025 | p.19] daily cycle (both night and day). Open data policies are key to maximising use of 521
[pdf-644c658902e8161761 | p.19] these datasets. 522
[pdf-ad1f6aa2916158f0d8 | p.19] A multi-platform approach to combining discrete in-situ measurements, with 523
[pdf-227898a4575d78ac1d | p.19] those from autonomous in-situ platforms and satellite data, could oﬀer synergistic 524
[pdf-102f69ae3fa482aab9 | p.19] beneﬁts, providing the diﬀerent scales of the observations, and diﬀerences in 525
[pdf-82a939e4579774be0a | p.19] measurement techniques can be bridged (Cronin et al., 2022). There are also op- 526
[pdf-c2064e2e1dbe749677 | p.19] portunities to encourage and support existing time-series stations (e.g., Bermuda 527
[pdf-86680a315a9df58a3d | p.19] Atlantic Time-series Study (BATS), Hawaii Ocean Time-series (HOT), Western 528
[pdf-30c780254f393a5743 | p.19] Channel Observatory (WCO) Station L4, Carbon Retention in a Colored Ocean 529
[pdf-557b6a06139432c926 | p.19] Time-Series (CARIACO), Line P, Porcupine Abyssal Plain, Blanes Bay Microbial 530
[pdf-ba8e0d7c7f1e90397d | p.19] Observatory, Long Term Ecological Research (LTER) sites, and Stonˇcica) to 531
[pdf-e128eac55d81c32525 | p.19] continue to make high-quality in-situ measurements of PP as well as the model 532
[pdf-03c6739ae81d166042 | p.19] parameters necessary for implementation of PP and photoacclimation models. 533
[pdf-8c76214c537db87c35 | p.19] There are opportunities to use artiﬁcial intelligence, such as machine learning, 534
[pdf-04134243f993e27813 | p.19] to help in this regard (e.g., see Huang et al., 2021), which has proven useful for 535
[pdf-3894f916278df77673 | p.19] estimating net PP from space in estuaries (Xu et al., 2022). There are oppor- 536
[pdf-10a4d97b1d49a6c201 | p.19] tunities to encourage pathways to commercial partnerships and technological 537
[pdf-27fba13913eb6d6124 | p.19] innovation as science questions call for operational in-situ sensors and platforms, 538
[pdf-3e917cfc3c8f4e4be8 | p.19] to target hard to access or currently unattainable ocean carbon properties and key 539
[pdf-6ce27c98fa3e1e8fd3 | p.19] PP parameters. 540
[pdf-0bf24c25aadde6673c | p.19] 19

## p.20 — Page 20

[pdf-86b14167be133ae0fb | p.20] There are opportunities to exploit the ability of geostationary platforms 541
[pdf-7565c87ba8d2c4b3bb | p.20] (e.g.Geostationary Ocean Color Imager (GOCI) and Geosynchronous Littoral 542
[pdf-8f573db763b1b303f9 | p.20] Imaging and Monitoring Radiometer (GLIMR)), to resolve diurnal variability 543
[pdf-f2322b1d6085c9839b | p.20] in light (PAR) and biomass. Such sensors are also able to gather considerably 544
[pdf-091b39a110dde43f22 | p.20] more data for a given region than polar orbiting satellites (Feng et al., 2017). By 545
[pdf-7348d7228032057b91 | p.20] building on the international community engagement of the "Ocean Carbon from 546
[pdf-ed13f767ccf786320c | p.20] Space" workshop, and that of other international initiatives (e.g., IOCCG), there 547
[pdf-98a264e0fa69ae6465 | p.20] are opportunities to formulate priorities for funding, and to create the necessary 548
[pdf-31b26449ecbbef4514 | p.20] coordinating bodies, to address the challenges and gaps identiﬁed above. 549
[pdf-4e4421f1454db16d8e | p.20] 3.1.3. PP priority 2: Uncertainty estimation of satellite algorithms and validation 550
[pdf-43ffaef7e08a93cc12 | p.20] Challenges: Assessment of satellite-based PP estimates is currently challeng- 551
[pdf-c6b2d09ea718e9f79b | p.20] ing, owing to the sparsity of in-situ data on PP and model parameters (limited in 552
[pdf-8896be08a34bb21a57 | p.20] spatial and temporal coverage and by costs), diﬀerences in the methods used for 553
[pdf-fb8fe5deaad1f3936c | p.20] in-situ data collection, diﬀerences in scales of in situ and satellite observations, 554
[pdf-97a46290e2e0cf1de3 | p.20] and a lack of availability of independent in-situ data to those used for model 555
[pdf-22e4958814afbf1171 | p.20] tuning. Standard oceanographic cruises can be aﬀected by extreme weather 556
[pdf-e02d6721fc06708be2 | p.20] conditions, particularly during fall and winter seasons. As a result, ship-based 557
[pdf-294d28bf7684713db0 | p.20] observations are sparse and often biased towards the summer-season. 558
[pdf-fec3a7e47a16dce33a | p.20] Gaps: Validation-based uncertainty estimates of satellite-derived PP products 559
[pdf-20c3a50b520141654c | p.20] are often not readily provided, and it is diﬃcult to quantify model-based error 560
[pdf-1f53a50b9e3415b2e1 | p.20] propagation methods (e.g., Brewin et al., 2017c). There are gaps in our under- 561
[pdf-a11b1f5a9f95daab39 | p.20] standing of the uncertainty in key parameters and variables used for input to PP 562
[pdf-1cc726fd1c87cee0f0 | p.20] models. Other gaps exist relating to the nature of passive ocean-colour, such as 563
[pdf-b400885596f49a3e54 | p.20] data gaps in satellite observations (e.g., cloud covered pixels, and coverage in 564
[pdf-af7a7ec9820609af19 | p.20] polar regions; Stock et al., 2020). 565
[pdf-64e7237765b78805d3 | p.20] Opportunities: We are now at a point where the computational demand 566
[pdf-c138343ba85cecaf39 | p.20] of formal error propagation methods (going from errors in top-of-atmosphere 567
[pdf-90b8a4d558e2cc6c01 | p.20] reﬂectance through to errors in PP model parameters) can be met, such that per- 568
[pdf-802bd71a6659a14515 | p.20] pixel uncertainty estimates in satellite PP products could be computed (McKinna 569
[pdf-bb96e9924c65eb74f3 | p.20] et al., 2019). There are also opportunities to constrain PP estimates and reduce 570
[pdf-80a9aa785630251afc | p.20] uncertainties through harnessing emerging hyperspectral, lidar (with improved 571
[pdf-36d227e5f1f941c4ca | p.20] 20

## p.21 — Page 21

[pdf-cc09556121f379258d | p.21] vertical resolution over passive ocean colour) and geostationary sensors, that may 572
[pdf-dfc2f236d7cc294bd9 | p.21] provide more information on the community composition of the phytoplankton 573
[pdf-2d39115e117df55d6c | p.21] and their diel cycles (day-night cycles, a requirement being increased temporal 574
[pdf-104dfdb89700e0028c | p.21] resolution), as well as information on the spectral attenuation of underwater 575
[pdf-0c7e44e912b71133d3 | p.21] light, crucial for deriving PP. The synergistic usage of multiple satellites can be 576
[pdf-58aed7d13961da2c94 | p.21] an opportunity to improve input irradiance products to PP models. There are 577
[pdf-319904372e95943058 | p.21] also opportunities to use satellite sensors measuring light in the ultraviolet (UV) 578
[pdf-7c6789e54879e63f1a | p.21] to improve satellite PP estimates (Cullen et al., 2012; Oelker et al., 2022). For 579
[pdf-ad5b265292d7cf9904 | p.21] improved uncertainty estimation, continuous validation is crucial, as is quantifying 580
[pdf-e098772b8bbda62494 | p.21] uncertainties in model parameters. Autonomous platforms and active ocean colour 581
[pdf-0e7ae06dab9d1b8056 | p.21] remote sensing (lidar) may oﬀer opportunities to help in this regard. 582
[pdf-b4741010f6c4434d3e | p.21] 3.1.4. PP priority 3: Linking surface satellite measurements to the vertical 583
[pdf-577f8dc81474141efe | p.21] distribution 584
[pdf-a7469b608ffd72cb18 | p.21] Challenges: Considering passive ocean-colour satellites only view a portion 585
[pdf-73fcae55aa49d81ad9 | p.21] of the euphotic zone (the ﬁrst penetration depth), resolving the vertical structure 586
[pdf-dfa818448e95793e43 | p.21] of all satellite-based carbon pools and ﬂuxes is challenging, but none more so than 587
[pdf-a6ae3414c2fcf72d3b | p.21] that of PP. There are challenges in the requirements to know vertical variations 588
[pdf-7a9300934c5a5a4a54 | p.21] in the phytoplankton biomass (e.g., Chlorophyll-a, hereafter denoted Chl-a), the 589
[pdf-aed3251917f7a47357 | p.21] physiological status (e.g., photoacclimation) of the phytoplankton (e.g., through 590
[pdf-69cfe7391f5728ac05 | p.21] the parameters of the photosynthesis-irradiance curve), and the magnitude, angular 591
[pdf-84929defe296a5d76a | p.21] structure, and spectral nature of the underwater light ﬁeld. For example, due to 592
[pdf-08f1715c53ec02cd62 | p.21] wind-depending wave-induced light focusing, there can be extreme short-term 593
[pdf-15841407f01059ab13 | p.21] variability in PAR near the surface, with irradiance peaks > 15 times the average 594
[pdf-e00d18db4761624c9d | p.21] (Hieronymi and Macke, 2012) in visible, UV-A and UV-B spectral ranges, with 595
[pdf-5768677ae7a02a2926 | p.21] implications for phytoplankton photosynthesis. 596
[pdf-8411e937ff9674e915 | p.21] Gaps: Our understanding of this vertical variability is impeded by the sparsity 597
[pdf-22b5101de845233e5a | p.21] of in-situ observations on vertical structure. Ideally, we require observations at the 598
[pdf-3016eb797992e442f6 | p.21] equivalent spatial and temporal scale to that of the satellite data, for successfully 599
[pdf-1a890130bc8687f21d | p.21] extrapolating the surface ﬁelds to depth. There are also gaps in vertical physical 600
[pdf-a586deaf64a2a6978b | p.21] data, and in their uncertainties, at equivalent scales to the satellite observations, 601
[pdf-a869337142fd743a3f | p.21] such as the mixed-layer depth. 602
[pdf-1b03ef6eeacb87d861 | p.21] 21

## p.22 — Page 22

[pdf-15083f5845fee9e7ef | p.22] Opportunities: There are future opportunities to improve our basic under- 603
[pdf-05e11e2981a115b93a | p.22] standing of vertical structure by tapping into existing and planned arrays of 604
[pdf-e3d6b67a0200a15c21 | p.22] autonomous in-situ platforms, such as the global array of Biogeochemical (BGC) 605
[pdf-60de9201b58e8bbfd3 | p.22] - Argo ﬂoats (Johnson et al., 2009; Claustre et al., 2020; Cornec et al., 2021; 606
[pdf-75d8557e07c25754dc | p.22] Addey, 2022) and also the physical Argo array for ﬁelds of mixed-layer depth 607
[pdf-f830dce5c2ce9b1c0d | p.22] and sub-surface temperature, with the help of statistical modelling (e.g., Foster 608
[pdf-6ace082d6ab3b6c3ef | p.22] et al., 2021). Other technologies are also expected to improve understanding of 609
[pdf-448879a1ea2d923153 | p.22] vertical structure, such as moorings and ice tethered and towed undulating plat- 610
[pdf-6b12b34e683b80bff3 | p.22] forms (Laney et al., 2014; Bracher et al., 2020; Stedmon et al., 2021; Von Appen 611
[pdf-6cb927b6fd923fd7bf | p.22] et al., 2021). These platforms may help us improve our understanding of the 612
[pdf-6bfc10f1a94595c5c2 | p.22] vertical distribution of parameters and variables relevant for PP modelling, such 613
[pdf-48d9e21c060f901753 | p.22] as chlorophyll (acknowledging potential vertical changes in ﬂuorescence quantum 614
[pdf-29760bcdcba9122bc1 | p.22] yield eﬃciency), backscattering and light. Future satellite lidar systems will be 615
[pdf-621429fb2315a70415 | p.22] capable of viewing the ocean surface up to three optical depths, improving the 616
[pdf-0483b2968a575f7187 | p.22] vertical resolution of ocean colour products. 617
[pdf-8b1a86d82d01a94bf7 | p.22] 3.1.5. PP priority 4: Trends 618
[pdf-0afbbbf6968aa1f05c | p.22] Challenges: Detecting trends in PP is a major challenge to our research 619
[pdf-6ece7cd9b6e954c65c | p.22] community. A recent report by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change 620
[pdf-46734ea8e7ed0b75c5 | p.22] (IPCC, 2019) expressed low conﬁdence in satellite-based trends in marine PP. 621
[pdf-c48269d84f75d45d5d | p.22] Gaps: The reasons the IPCC report cited this low conﬁdence were related 622
[pdf-c0a435433f54cf2a49 | p.22] to the fact that the length of satellite ocean colour record is not suﬃcient yet for 623
[pdf-5bf7accd65009b8043 | p.22] climate change studies, and the lack of corroborating trends in in-situ data (see 624
[pdf-03ffdf1f298eca4f6d | p.22] PP priority 1) (IPCC, 2019). Additionally, there are gaps in uncertainty estimates 625
[pdf-7aa79ac06c00c68378 | p.22] for satellite-based products (see PP priority 3), needed to quantity the signiﬁcance 626
[pdf-21e4a17aadba794fe4 | p.22] of any such trends. 627
[pdf-bfda1b73952301ee7b | p.22] Opportunities: To meet these challenges, and ﬁll these gaps, there has been 628
[pdf-236bb9dc47f91ee1e5 | p.22] signiﬁcant work over the past decade to create consistent and continuous satellite 629
[pdf-3e6a7f8acb7699a83a | p.22] records for climate research (e.g., Sathyendranath et al., 2019a). As we approach 630
[pdf-6b98cab8d4af4f9486 | p.22] the point at which the length of satellite ocean colour record will be suﬃcient for 631
[pdf-cdf0b8c88543ca9e36 | p.22] climate change studies, we can build on this work and harness these systems that 632
[pdf-7c2c003b4d0ead4659 | p.22] have been put in place, merging future ocean colour sensors with current and past 633
[pdf-66d298029bed74f987 | p.22] 22

## p.23 — Page 23

[pdf-c73db6134b07a6ad50 | p.23] sensors (e.g., Yang et al., 2022a). There are also opportunities to bring satellite 634
[pdf-44a290de1cafb61201 | p.23] data and models together, for example, using data assimilation, to improve our 635
[pdf-637ce03ec9817d2dd8 | p.23] conﬁdence and understanding of PP trends (e.g., Gregg and Rousseaux, 2019) and 636
[pdf-853464714c4da4f88f | p.23] understand variability in PP and photoacclimation. There are also opportunities 637
[pdf-510dba2ae0bb93496f | p.23] to gain insight into the impacts of climate change on PP, by studying short-term 638
[pdf-7e85f8fd476e680b2e | p.23] extreme events (see Section 4.2 and Le Grix et al., 2021). 639
[pdf-46845bc33c30043856 | p.23] 3.1.6. PP priority 5: Fundamental understanding 640
[pdf-4a24dbcbd219c22065 | p.23] Challenges: At the workshop, participants also identiﬁed some major chal- 641
[pdf-00826fc848aa8905ed | p.23] lenges relating to our fundamental understanding of marine PP. These included: 642
[pdf-d39eaf0116b0fd6039 | p.23] the need to understand better the relationships among PP, phytoplankton com- 643
[pdf-f467d9f5da559c472d | p.23] munity structure and physical-chemical environment (e.g. nutrient availability); 644
[pdf-5102d3c4dd60c8a899 | p.23] understand better feedbacks between physics and biology and how biology aﬀects 645
[pdf-cea35afb645bd0df60 | p.23] the carbon cycle; understand better the fate of PP (e.g., secondary and export pro- 646
[pdf-40a535f3abc2900376 | p.23] duction); and understand better the interactions among the diﬀerent components 647
[pdf-5d2ff336f7b1cd1a61 | p.23] of the Earth System and how they inﬂuence marine primary productivity. As 648
[pdf-0ae3422ad35b905cc1 | p.23] stated earlier, for carbon cycle studies, there is a clear requirement to go beyond 649
[pdf-fee6485e0d228f8033 | p.23] PP and strive to quantify new production and net community production (e.g., 650
[pdf-62d57035f23b6b2c15 | p.23] Tilstone et al., 2015; Ford et al., 2021, 2022a,b). 651
[pdf-e42ca330b5139982b0 | p.23] Gaps: There are gaps in in-situ observations that if ﬁlled could help meet 652
[pdf-5310d89e4b81dea24c | p.23] some of these challenges (see PP priority 1). Additionally, meeting some of 653
[pdf-3ea46503a2f704d39d | p.23] these challenges may require higher spatial and temporal resolution products 654
[pdf-b2fb4dfb0de25ee595 | p.23] than currently available, for example, to study diurnal variability. The need for 655
[pdf-a1f25336d2caea4496 | p.23] higher spatial and temporal resolution data also limits our ability to estimate PP 656
[pdf-ebe5213a013d23a830 | p.23] in coastal and inland waters, impeding our understanding of land-sea interactions 657
[pdf-414bd343d76abfc60e | p.23] (Regnier et al., 2022) (see Section 4.1 for links to Blue Carbon). 658
[pdf-5c3ff46fe26af8ae9d | p.23] There are also gaps in satellite information on datasets relevant to photochem- 659
[pdf-6be74f186a32cff6c1 | p.23] ical reactions, mostly activated by UV light, impacting PP through photodegra- 660
[pdf-571d7992e00a652018 | p.23] dation of phytoplankton and the formation of UV absorbing compounds. High 661
[pdf-71d86e62c66d948804 | p.23] spectral resolution data from satellite are also needed to improve PP modelling 662
[pdf-dae4c42a4bfcf41826 | p.23] (Antoine and Morel, 1996). Should such datasets become available, they will 663
[pdf-2592c3e21210c42264 | p.23] require validation. Equipping autonomous platforms with hyperspectral sensors 664
[pdf-7428b0abba1ca87db1 | p.23] 23

## p.24 — Page 24

[pdf-52077ea3b9ff0eddc4 | p.24] could provide help in this regard (see priority 3). There are gaps in our under- 665
[pdf-5e675850755041add4 | p.24] standing of controls on PP in the ocean by viruses and other microbes (Suttle 666
[pdf-4f8a0b6cdc7d7f204c | p.24] et al., 1990). 667
[pdf-6a1d00dd2aaf35bb4b | p.24] Opportunities: With greater emphasis placed on an Earth system approach, 668
[pdf-25d0a024d75323bae2 | p.24] to meet the challenges of the United Nations (UN) Ocean Decade, there are now 669
[pdf-38f2c73d4521c84a00 | p.24] more opportunities for collaborative interdisciplinary research, which may help to 670
[pdf-52ad1ada71fd7b3b3b | p.24] unify the integration of PP across interfaces, bringing together PP on land and in 671
[pdf-9874edc4c648dafaf3 | p.24] the ocean. For example, there have been promising developments in tidal wetland 672
[pdf-b64a105ec09cac92ac | p.24] gross PP algorithms (Feagin et al., 2020). With increasing computation power, 673
[pdf-674f3859b7158136a6 | p.24] there are also opportunities to merge/nest regionally tuned models for larger scale 674
[pdf-589ab14292c9d9ab72 | p.24] estimates of PP. A shift from high performance computing to quantum computing 675
[pdf-9bdfd4c4a384152646 | p.24] could lead to signiﬁcant progress in this direction, as well as incorporation of 676
[pdf-54605d5268a06470ec | p.24] input data streams from molecular biology. 677
[pdf-da90540010fee42138 | p.24] There are opportunities to harness novel algorithms and satellites (e.g. 678
[pdf-8ee77c6f801317f5c1 | p.24] Sentinel-5P, Sentinel-5, Sentinel-4, Plankton, Aerosol, Cloud, ocean Ecosys- 679
[pdf-6c374b44af71387ea0 | p.24] tem (PACE), see Table 2) that can provide enhanced information on the spectral 680
[pdf-ca9a2932109560325c | p.24] composition of underwater light ﬁeld (e.g., for the retrieval of diﬀuse underwater 681
[pdf-8c20bb95d37ae79316 | p.24] attenuation (Kd) of UV and short blue light for Tropospheric Monitoring Instru- 682
[pdf-ed5efa345edc47cb86 | p.24] ment (TROPOMI) (Sentinel-5P) see Oelker et al., 2022). There is also potential 683
[pdf-94b31ae71ac313baf3 | p.24] to go beyond the one waveband (490 nm) Kd products, as currently provided 684
[pdf-cb5c07e18b45d6c648 | p.24] operationally, to multi and hyperspectral Kd products, building on the capabilities 685
[pdf-7ea38fdbad9fd58127 | p.24] of S3-OLCI next generation missions and older generation satellites like the 686
[pdf-9b842ba466f3744fef | p.24] Medium Resolution Imaging Spectrometer (MERIS), that have a suit of bands 687
[pdf-5bb14887a6f696569d | p.24] in the visible range. Especially considering improved data storage and transfer 688
[pdf-0c25c319bce6d3097b | p.24] capabilities. There are also opportunities to use satellite instruments covering 689
[pdf-8705089a9b0dbd5e42 | p.24] the UV spectral range to give insight on the presence of UV absorbing pigments 690
[pdf-bebb0fee79ccaf1736 | p.24] and types of coloured dissolved organic matter (CDOM), which may provide 691
[pdf-63a63154995e07e287 | p.24] important information on photodegradation processes. Active-based lidar systems, 692
[pdf-08c70977cf2b053e6f | p.24] capable of viewing further into the water column, at day and night and at low sun 693
[pdf-bc5a50c34c8f8758b6 | p.24] angles, and geostationary platforms, may oﬀer opportunities to ﬁll gaps in our 694
[pdf-15ca2547e1bd32ab89 | p.24] understanding of PP. 695
[pdf-61956e297410b499ac | p.24] 24

## p.25 — Page 25

[pdf-7d3ade62cec470c9a6 | p.25] 3.2. Particulate Organic Carbon (POC) 696
[pdf-f0db50eda0ca82933c | p.25] POC can be deﬁned functionally as the organic carbon in a water sample that 697
[pdf-a369e61cb04d97fea7 | p.25] is above 0.2 µm in diameter (taken as the formal boundary between dissolved 698
[pdf-234e983aee3334a152 | p.25] and particulate substances). Globally, it is thought to be in the region of 2.3 - 699
[pdf-f95e3ae0913bbc0cd0 | p.25] 4.0 Gt C in size (Stramska, 2009; CEOS, 2014; Galí et al., 2022), with around 700
[pdf-9f1d59231cd71fa5ea | p.25] 0.58 - 1.3 Gt C in the upper mixed layer (Evers-King et al., 2017; Galí et al., 2022). 701
[pdf-ff772239ec4225bc3f | p.25] It is among the most dynamic pools of carbon in the ocean, and turns over at a 702
[pdf-f3ac5acc032703eae3 | p.25] higher rate than any organic carbon pool on Earth (Sarmiento and Gruber, 2006). 703
[pdf-438578b9a1d7564214 | p.25] It can be separated into living (e.g., phytoplankton, zooplankton, bacteria) and 704
[pdf-23e4aae4d7e037bcfb | p.25] non-living (e.g., detritus) organic carbon material. 705
[pdf-4d4eb48d4894945a6c | p.25] 3.2.1. State of the art in POC 706
[pdf-98c60f7b596b94c2ec | p.25] Satellite remote-sensing of POC focuses typically on the use of ocean colour 707
[pdf-b93ddb7c6ca380ed9e | p.25] data, and is among the more mature satellite ocean carbon products, with the 708
[pdf-83bf85f9a3aac67023 | p.25] ﬁrst satellite-based algorithm developed in the late 90’s (Stramski et al., 1999). 709
[pdf-9bd005ab854db614a9 | p.25] Current algorithms include those that are: based on empirical band ratio or band- 710
[pdf-69ee2fa78c7aa71924 | p.25] diﬀerences in remote-sensing reﬂectance wavelengths; backscattering based; 711
[pdf-654dd1f44b1da55ad9 | p.25] backscattering and chlorophyll based; based on estimates of diﬀuse attenuation 712
[pdf-b5eea5cec4fe2583ab | p.25] (Kd); and based on a two-step relationship between diﬀuse attenuation and beam 713
[pdf-bbdc516deb0a5b9864 | p.25] attenuation. It is worth acknowledging the inherent optical property (IOP)-, 714
[pdf-f31cbae23620bc62b3 | p.25] chlorophyll-, and Kd-based algorithms involve ﬁrst deriving these inputs from 715
[pdf-75a018a78aff94300d | p.25] remote-sensing reﬂectance. For a recent review of these algorithms the reader 716
[pdf-c3fa92a4158270b648 | p.25] is referred to Section 4.1.3.1. of Brewin et al. (2021). The empirical algorithm 717
[pdf-2145caa0696860f0bf | p.25] that links POC in the near-surface ocean to the blue-to-green reﬂectance band 718
[pdf-02df6efbc1a549186f | p.25] ratio described in Stramski et al. (2008) has been used by NASA to generate the 719
[pdf-fe3b4537b704e66e1a | p.25] standard global POC product from multiple satellite ocean colour missions, and in 720
[pdf-5f1c438153675c7d4f | p.25] some ESA POC initiatives (Evers-King et al., 2017). These standard algorithms 721
[pdf-77a0b0f65466d8788e | p.25] provided a tool for estimation of global and basin-scale reservoirs of POC in 722
[pdf-730dd495f63381a7e3 | p.25] the upper ocean layer (e.g., Stramska and Cieszy´nska, 2015). Recently, a new 723
[pdf-c220cd3718c7620703 | p.25] suite of ocean colour sensor-speciﬁc empirical algorithms intended for global 724
[pdf-1776db582df4f8b992 | p.25] applications was proposed by Stramski et al. (2022) with a main goal to improve 725
[pdf-63cba99d73c9f0f025 | p.25] 25

## p.26 — Page 26

[pdf-11f599c191ebafd20a | p.26] POC estimates compared to current standard algorithms in waters with very 726
[pdf-8ecd4f09aade7cf25a | p.26] low POC (ultraoligotrophic environments, < 0.04 mg m−3) and relatively high 727
[pdf-b2ad70ba97cd216597 | p.26] POC (above a few hundred mg m−3). Intercomparison and validation exercises 728
[pdf-fab3a02b636e26b62c | p.26] have suggested the performance of satellite POC algorithms is comparable to, or 729
[pdf-3ba7a9af03febf0920 | p.26] even better than, satellite estimates of chlorophyll-a (Evers-King et al., 2017), 730
[pdf-90e1a6b6b8490eeaa2 | p.26] among the more widely used ocean colour products. The high performance in 731
[pdf-04e212ec19c5170efb | p.26] satellite POC is perhaps related to POC representing the entire pool of organic 732
[pdf-d1297f0cb0398298fc | p.26] particles (rather than just phytoplankton, as with Chl-a). However, a recent study 733
[pdf-c274e5050213256f44 | p.26] highlighted signiﬁcant inconsistencies between satellite-retrieved POC and that 734
[pdf-9c23fc01debd43f7f5 | p.26] estimated from BGC-Argo ﬂoat data at high-latitudes during the winter season 735
[pdf-2fe2cdf0d5332d4d4e | p.26] (Galí et al., 2022). 736
[pdf-d75a4222a10629e0cb | p.26] Six priority areas of POC were identiﬁed, that will be discussed separately 737
[pdf-668e74bd59956a1e49 | p.26] in this section, including: 1) in-situ measurement methodology; 2) in-situ data 738
[pdf-49c50ef822ab414cba | p.26] compilation; 3) satellite algorithm retrievals; 4) partitioning into size; 5) vertical 739
[pdf-6866029354affb58f1 | p.26] proﬁles; and 6) biogeochemical processes and the biological carbon pump. Table 740
[pdf-387531f744fc534af3 | p.26] 4 summarises these priorities, and their challenges, gaps and opportunities. 741
[pdf-f41e0b8f24723d05b1 | p.26] 3.2.2. POC priority 1: In-situ measurement methodology 742
[pdf-86798c1866ec665367 | p.26] Challenges: The current ﬁltration-based methodology that uses glass-ﬁber 743
[pdf-ce8a2c8391fdd56660 | p.26] ﬁlters (nominal porosity typically around 0.7 µm, through the eﬀective pore size 744
[pdf-6ceaa1a00d0f2d5d9c | p.26] of glass-ﬁbre ﬁlters is thought to be substantially smaller; Sheldon, 1972) for 745
[pdf-756974e4273b4348b8 | p.26] retaining particles and measuring POC does not include all POC-bearing particles, 746
[pdf-1506adbbaeb6e285c7 | p.26] and hence does not determine the total POC. In particular, some fraction of 747
[pdf-fa96942aa45227f663 | p.26] submicrometer POC-bearing particles is missed by this method (e.g., Nagata, 748
[pdf-d63032ab3e4b1805cb | p.26] 1986; Taguchi and Laws, 1988; Stramski, 1990; Lee et al., 1995), and these 749
[pdf-6e0a16113d8b517da9 | p.26] small-sized particles can make signiﬁcant contribution to total POC (e.g., Sharp, 750
[pdf-b06df304e026834cee | p.26] 1973; Fuhrman et al., 1989; Cho and Azam, 1990). Glass-ﬁbre ﬁlters are also 751
[pdf-841b60ba6b373c35c7 | p.26] subject to cell leakage and can cause breakage of cells due to the combined eﬀects 752
[pdf-78cd5668fa395cb0db | p.26] of pressure sample loading, and needle-like microﬁber ends (IOCCG Protocol 753
[pdf-5fa0d92d7ac95dd94f | p.26] Series, 2021). Other sources of possible underestimation of total POC include 754
[pdf-d287ba59ed15dce4fc | p.26] the loss of POC due to the impact of pressure diﬀerential across the ﬁlters (but 755
[pdf-573f5aa70d0efb62ca | p.26] see Liu et al., 2005) and an underrepresentation of the contribution of relatively 756
[pdf-62f25b5bdaeb846fc9 | p.26] 26

## p.27 — Page 27

[pdf-38f919b4a0afc6bcea | p.27] rare large particles associated with a limited ﬁltration volume (e.g., Goldman and 757
[pdf-028c1c5bfafa521b17 | p.27] Dennett, 1985; Bishop, 1999; Gardner et al., 2003; Collos et al., 2014; McDonnell 758
[pdf-cb75bb0e355671a880 | p.27] et al., 2015). Thus, it is very important to report volumes ﬁltered together with 759
[pdf-bb7281bbad842dedee | p.27] POC concentrations. Diﬀerences in ﬁlter type, particle settling in bottles, and 760
[pdf-a0865fd71b0fe8678a | p.27] breakage or leakage of phytoplankton and other cells, are other issues that can 761
[pdf-6f7afead7fcca05904 | p.27] cause errors in ﬁltration-based methods. 762
[pdf-1fa5e5e3dbaad0276d | p.27] Optical remote sensing (including ocean colour measurements from space) is 763
[pdf-27d0caaccce932f25f | p.27] driven by all particles suspended in water, including particles which are missed 764
[pdf-13c592d0a2ff10e3ae | p.27] and/or underrepresented by the current ﬁltration-based POC methodology (Davies 765
[pdf-4979a99be7da11c4d3 | p.27] et al., 2021). Thus, there is a mismatch between in-situ POC measurements 766
[pdf-6fd0febfee89807c64 | p.27] through ﬁltration and optical measurements that serve as a proxy of POC. The 767
[pdf-27e2e87ea3a3048b07 | p.27] missing portion of POC unaccounted for by the current ﬁltration based POC 768
[pdf-ca1931c6cec8380db3 | p.27] methodology is important to both the ocean biogeochemistry and ocean optics 769
[pdf-e2f13b69332173ecad | p.27] that underlies ocean colour measurements from space. 770
[pdf-79ba756f2de5d2ec28 | p.27] While standardisation of POC methodology is generally desirable, there are 771
[pdf-ea172e7f7e4a02c7a8 | p.27] important interpretive challenges that must be recognized during the standardis- 772
[pdf-d2b7ca72d2e5041c06 | p.27] ation process. In particular, while the recommendation to use DOC-absorption 773
[pdf-b93d29b2dca63c02b2 | p.27] correction to the standard ﬁltration-based method will result in correction for 774
[pdf-71897fb2a65d7dd9b2 | p.27] one known source of overestimation of the fraction of total POC that is strictly 775
[pdf-d55ced6070d1a2b915 | p.27] retainable on the ﬁlters (Moran et al., 1999; Gardner et al., 2003; Cetini´c et al., 776
[pdf-9b044fbc944993f836 | p.27] 2012; Novak et al., 2018; IOCCG Protocol Series, 2021), the issue of known 777
[pdf-947d3a9ef7410559e7 | p.27] sources of underestimation of total POC remains unresolved. 778
[pdf-bbc076eab502080152 | p.27] The fractional contributions to POC associated with diﬀerently-sized particles 779
[pdf-7e95895ca398e88ed2 | p.27] and/or diﬀerent types of particles (e.g., diﬀerent groups or species of microorgan- 780
[pdf-a6cc2aaa60144ed3ae | p.27] isms) are diﬃcult to quantify and remain poorly known for natural polydisperse 781
[pdf-b6d7c168681cd9135b | p.27] and heterogenous assemblages of suspended particles. 782
[pdf-26e331fc96975cf4f9 | p.27] Gaps: The current POC standard method does not account for both the artiﬁ- 783
[pdf-8b00ef4dbc26d80224 | p.27] cial gains and losses of POC during collection of particles by ﬁltration (Gardner 784
[pdf-c7049add713a5c323c | p.27] et al., 2003; Turnewitsch et al., 2007; IOCCG Protocol Series, 2021). With the 785
[pdf-e86ffcf6a6acad140c | p.27] exception of size-based ﬁltration (which has known limitations), no experimental 786
[pdf-630ef755c2c6b7e144 | p.27] capabilities exist to partition total POC of natural particulate assemblages into 787
[pdf-7a76cd449e14cec06f | p.27] 27

## p.28 — Page 28

[pdf-abaee528c18af7759f | p.28] contributions by diﬀerent size fractions and/or diﬀerent types of particles which 788
[pdf-8f9ba0c373d29282af | p.28] play diﬀerent roles in ocean biogeochemistry and carbon cycling. Another im- 789
[pdf-821d24fbc597a21562 | p.28] portant gap is the lack of a certiﬁed reference material (CRM) for POC. A CRM 790
[pdf-4ebcf19871cfb16faf | p.28] allows to estimate the accuracy of POC estimated by diﬀerent laboratories and by 791
[pdf-9592d3b80c523a3f5c | p.28] the same laboratory in diﬀerent times and locations. Consequently, a CRM for 792
[pdf-91f20facd7677442f1 | p.28] POC, if used by the community, would allow to reduce uncertainties in POC. 793
[pdf-75b7b0a80aadae714a | p.28] Opportunities: There are opportunities to advance and standardise the mea- 794
[pdf-0f6a24bc7207f88a4a | p.28] surement methodology of total POC to provide improved estimates. These 795
[pdf-54b7d90aff1239005e | p.28] advancements can be brought about by including the portion of POC that is 796
[pdf-1c3bbe0c97bfef24b9 | p.28] unaccounted for by the current standard ﬁltration-based method. This would 797
[pdf-77b7a23194550ce1ef | p.28] likely involve developing measurement capabilities aiming at quantiﬁcation of 798
[pdf-bfec6d306483f32c5f | p.28] POC contributions associated with diﬀerently-sized particles and diﬀerent particle 799
[pdf-6e68ec530a50213c19 | p.28] types based on combination of single-particle measurement techniques for particle 800
[pdf-d0fea47b9195bf8164 | p.28] sizing, particle identiﬁcation, and particle optical properties. 801
[pdf-3352f57a1d48be64d0 | p.28] 3.2.3. POC priority 2: In-situ data compilation 802
[pdf-8a5ad739ae95236720 | p.28] Challenges: There have been signiﬁcant investments, at regional, federal and 803
[pdf-85ea42a80ce6c01b10 | p.28] international scale, into POC data collection (see Figure 1 of Evers-King et al. 804
[pdf-e2dc774459b130f71c | p.28] (2017) for a map of global sampling coverage of in-situ POC data), which has 805
[pdf-e7da4d16ccc39e80bb | p.28] transformed our understanding of POC in the ocean. But there are challenges in 806
[pdf-104b07eb6c489f6395 | p.28] using these data for POC algorithm development and validation. The ﬁeld-based 807
[pdf-be6a48508d3a5501ae | p.28] datasets are commonly compiled from data collected by diﬀerent investigators on 808
[pdf-8ebb87b56a660551d6 | p.28] many oceanographic expeditions covering a long period of time. The information 809
[pdf-0bb4f25e60e42a0717 | p.28] content available in documentation of various individual datasets is non-uniform 810
[pdf-f99c8c446830addfd6 | p.28] and does not always contain suﬃcient details about data acquisition and process- 811
[pdf-0738fa6d55bff39b99 | p.28] ing methodology. This creates a risk that the compiled datasets are aﬀected by 812
[pdf-7cf2c5b61f2721c539 | p.28] methodological inconsistencies across diverse subsets of data, including the poten- 813
[pdf-3e9b47d465d35bf1af | p.28] tial presence of methodological bias in some data. The presence of methodological 814
[pdf-d8b82f3ad9c9bb544e | p.28] bias is generally diﬃcult to identify given the range of environmental variability, 815
[pdf-b4f7c3ddd4b1bcd2e0 | p.28] especially when available details on data acquisition methods are limited and/or 816
[pdf-50ca3230c5dfdb0a29 | p.28] there is a lack of replicate measurements (a CRM would help in this regard, see 817
[pdf-afd8a9b07e347f9e71 | p.28] POC priority 1). Thus, indiscriminate use of data for the algorithm development 818
[pdf-51d182db0d80cb7d06 | p.28] 28

## p.29 — Page 29

[pdf-0e1ded8cd9ab6809b2 | p.29] and validation analyses is not advisable. These issues pose signiﬁcant challenges 819
[pdf-f58d7b057a4f7b9a7d | p.29] for assembling high-quality ﬁeld datasets that meet the standards and objectives of 820
[pdf-1702346b931e17eadc | p.29] algorithm development or validation analyses including, for example, the process 821
[pdf-b37aba0987e5ebd964 | p.29] of data quality control based on predeﬁned set of inclusion and exclusion criteria 822
[pdf-0a3e6f5dd83e65499c | p.29] and assurance of environmental representativeness of datasets assembled for the 823
[pdf-57b6e0be11f82959cf | p.29] analysis of speciﬁc algorithms (e.g., global vs. regional; Stramski et al., 2022). 824
[pdf-07e1cd5575d8c027c6 | p.29] Best practices for data quality control have been improved signiﬁcantly following 825
[pdf-9f35d8aef5756a37d0 | p.29] the recent publication of the IOCCG Particulate Organic Matter (POM) protocol 826
[pdf-abcb44a2f93bb10ac2 | p.29] (IOCCG Protocol Series, 2021) 827
[pdf-eae7c69327bbc5fbae | p.29] The common validation strategy that relies on comparisons of ﬁeld-satellite 828
[pdf-12fa89cbbad38afd12 | p.29] data matchups is not by itself suﬃcient to ensure rigorous assessment and under- 829
[pdf-1a6e0f9bb8ecf3527a | p.29] standing of various sources of uncertainties in satellite-derived POC products. 830
[pdf-168236d134ce038b3e | p.29] The deviations between ﬁeld and satellite data matchups can occur for various 831
