[pdf-4bde6433482e60451a | p.29] reasons such as spatial-temporal mismatch of data, uncertainties in both satellite 832
[pdf-b9ec343ab764d7286b | p.29] and in-situ measurements, atmospheric correction, and performance skills of the 833
[pdf-95d0ee7ff3085bc2e4 | p.29] in-water algorithm itself. In addition, the number of available data matchups is 834
[pdf-092c0e493a60060cdc | p.29] often limited in various environments. 835
[pdf-462df7786620832122 | p.29] Gaps: While the documentation of data acquisition and processing methods 836
[pdf-298d42a36bfded5569 | p.29] is often limited, especially in historical datasets, there are no standardised best- 837
[pdf-7a9c0f29819a9f8f2c | p.29] practice guidelines to ensure consistency in data quality control and synthesis 838
[pdf-439fc25e4eb231baa8 | p.29] eﬀorts when larger datasets are compiled from various individual subsets of 839
[pdf-103f5fc6a6fec3a079 | p.29] data. There are also regions within the world’s oceans, such as polar regions and 840
[pdf-a3fb1b938609265dc5 | p.29] the Indian Ocean, where concurrently collected ﬁeld data of POC and optical 841
[pdf-f9120fc5b9b56d3cb6 | p.29] properties are scarce, including the lack of temporal coverage over the entire 842
[pdf-3e5a5d73404cca0c42 | p.29] seasonal cycle. 843
[pdf-f5ec990bd9fcefb115 | p.29] Opportunities: Further eﬀorts related to POC algorithm development and 844
[pdf-7d79aed34ae6787580 | p.29] validation can beneﬁt from careful scrutiny of historical and future data to min- 845
[pdf-a1c63128fc7452db56 | p.29] imize the risk of using biased data and ensure that the analyses are conducted 846
[pdf-c14cf2880d02b9d2d7 | p.29] using data with consistently high quality and are accompanied with suﬃciently 847
[pdf-4f8022651d2a880d5c | p.29] detailed documentation on data acquisition and processing methods. These ef- 848
[pdf-4ba69326ac2d7e2009 | p.29] forts can be facilitated through further improvements and standardisation of best 849
[pdf-2a7fa19ceae3275ff5 | p.29] 29

## p.30 — Page 30

[pdf-ea5a6c0ebd205fed38 | p.30] practices for documentation, quality control, sharing, and submission of data into 850
[pdf-1ac9e0a7d0ba106af3 | p.30] database archives. Such practices are expected to lead to better data quality, data 851
[pdf-cf9eabcd7fc7d2f443 | p.30] interpretation, and uncertainty assessments (IOCCG Protocol Series, 2021). 852
[pdf-32f1d64130db5bea09 | p.30] There is a need to continue ﬁeld programs in which concurrent POC and 853
[pdf-dcf786ad82c05b4ccc | p.30] optical data are acquired across diverse environments including those that have 854
[pdf-1939d7e7dba8c2ee4f | p.30] been severely under-sampled in the past. 855
[pdf-43c048fee7a3de792e | p.30] 3.2.4. POC priority 3: Satellite algorithm retrievals 856
[pdf-cd4f97ef407f99f813 | p.30] Challenges: There can be a high level of complexity and variability of water 857
[pdf-8beda21b606433851e | p.30] optical properties and water constituent composition including POC-bearing 858
[pdf-657e12deef59f12102 | p.30] particles, especially in coastal regions and inland waters (where non-algal particles 859
[pdf-529ea09df181ec0aa1 | p.30] are more prevalent), which are highly susceptible to land eﬀects and re-suspension 860
[pdf-ef6b84d6a2a3a7b3b0 | p.30] of sediments from shallow bottom. This makes it very diﬃcult to develop a uniﬁed 861
[pdf-4c152df0e3d43fee08 | p.30] approach to provide reliable POC retrievals from optical remote sensing along 862
[pdf-00566f3c74f82ea4a5 | p.30] the continuum of diverse optical/biogeochemical environments from open ocean 863
[pdf-bcc9d39379f35a8815 | p.30] to coastal and inland water bodies. 864
[pdf-79dcfe4ad798734779 | p.30] Standard global POC products are generated indiscriminately with respect to 865
[pdf-b8a39bedd8cebd9ccb | p.30] optical water types or the optical composition of water. Hence, this product is 866
[pdf-2e8dd3d2aadddfd943 | p.30] generated for a wide range of environmental situations, including the conditions 867
[pdf-8cd9ba8bbec1690f2a | p.30] outside the intended scope of global algorithms, which implies unknown and po- 868
[pdf-f5b5817c93bd18f83d | p.30] tentially large uncertainties. An inter-mission consistency of POC satellite-based 869
[pdf-acc10215c627aebcd2 | p.30] products is required to support long-term climate data records. To successfully 870
[pdf-7e57606439da995b14 | p.30] harness new satellite geostationary and hyperspectral data (e.g., GLIMR, PRe- 871
[pdf-ac230902638be12bf0 | p.30] cursore IperSpettrale della Missione Applicativa (PRISMA), PACE), there are 872
[pdf-00ec213654f990e614 | p.30] challenges associated with appropriate atmospheric correction schemes, that can 873
[pdf-747cbe25e5c36dea8b | p.30] deal with large solar zenith and viewing angles for geostationary sensors, and 874
[pdf-da54a10f0434a3b5ef | p.30] spectral consistency for hyperspectral sensors. 875
[pdf-5f0f5e946542b6eef6 | p.30] Gaps: The current routine process of generating standard global POC products 876
[pdf-47f65a11fe9ed2df89 | p.30] from global empirical algorithms either lack the mechanistically-based ﬂags 877
[pdf-3d24b795d7f0dde64d | p.30] associated with ocean properties or optical water types to prevent the application 878
[pdf-bb79cd8b8b4ee4f87f | p.30] of algorithms beyond their intended use, or where ﬂags do exist, their usage is 879
[pdf-6fdda0c2f99db0bab0 | p.30] often not clariﬁed and they are often not accurate. Clear and accurate ﬂags are 880
[pdf-3c9aad636ff2aa5c7c | p.30] 30

## p.31 — Page 31

[pdf-9e566a02be41fa8477 | p.31] needed to guide users on product uncertainties and applications. The need for 881
[pdf-29af4d8da8b6fc4e7e | p.31] appropriate ﬂags to prevent the use of algorithms outside their scope is broadly 882
[pdf-7bd4333b8bb33cdef3 | p.31] relevant, for example, it applies also to regional algorithms (McKinna et al., 883
[pdf-8da387d13fcf7bfee3 | p.31] 2019). 884
[pdf-405b4a3f992b7db3f3 | p.31] There is a lack of advanced algorithms based on adaptive approaches that in- 885
[pdf-d9777e9384d028fec4 | p.31] corporate mechanistic principles on the interaction of light with water constituents 886
[pdf-4e41ad316b0350f2a6 | p.31] and associated optical water typologies, but the workshop saw the emergence 887
[pdf-1bdd3cb805b346ca32 | p.31] of such methods, which is a promising sign. For example, algorithms that dis- 888
[pdf-6deab9e2d6dcbe329a | p.31] criminate the water bodies based on varying composition of organic and mineral 889
[pdf-8bf317e2246b89e4ed | p.31] particles are required to enable reliable POC retrievals across diverse environ- 890
[pdf-0c77aee070a9dd9a03 | p.31] ments including the optically-complex coastal water bodies (Loisel et al., 2007; 891
[pdf-f7cd7b48f2610f77e6 | p.31] Wo´zniak et al., 2010; Reynolds et al., 2016). 892
[pdf-ff18d8ad2605af8d24 | p.31] Opportunities: Recent development of a new suite of empirical satellite 893
[pdf-0c611f468c82a4ad8d | p.31] sensor-speciﬁc global POC algorithms provide the opportunity for further testing, 894
[pdf-bff8c39be862532356 | p.31] validation, analysis of inter-mission consistency, and ultimately an implementation 895
[pdf-103e92d50b91ad4097 | p.31] of next-generation algorithms for routine production of a reﬁned global POC 896
[pdf-5e9281e1dc29767a81 | p.31] product (Stramski et al., 2022). 897
[pdf-3654ebbef4b4923906 | p.31] The analysis of POC reservoir and its spatial-temporal dynamics is expected to 898
[pdf-b21cbff0e064a2f36b | p.31] be enhanced by increased availability and use of geostationary and hyperspectral 899
[pdf-0ddc0a381e6ada419b | p.31] satellite data (e.g., GLIMR, PRISMA, PACE) along with in-situ data. 900
[pdf-b7471d96c877e1587e | p.31] 3.2.5. POC priority 4: Partitioning into size 901
[pdf-6b8be108cc23c984fe | p.31] Challenges: The particle size distribution (PSD) is an important link between 902
[pdf-71fe27a09c505e05d4 | p.31] ecosystem structure and function on the one hand, and optical properties on 903
[pdf-3b496ac5bae91df920 | p.31] the other, as it aﬀects both. Phytoplankton cell size is a key trait, and size 904
[pdf-9481df876f4b931f31 | p.31] fractions are closely related to functional types (Le Quéré et al., 2005; Marañón, 905
[pdf-1aa923bd7d6f5a194c | p.31] 2015). Monitoring the size distribution of particles in the ocean can provide 906
[pdf-72bf128d37d89a8de6 | p.31] information on how carbon ﬂows through the marine food-web, and how much 907
[pdf-3b4e290375963d8c96 | p.31] carbon is exported out of the euphotic zone, both useful for carbon management 908
[pdf-4b50f2135ae28b7b95 | p.31] strategies. One of the most challenging, yet important tasks moving forward 909
[pdf-153b0d8a0ffb32578c | p.31] is to develop understanding of the diﬀerent functional and/or size partitions of 910
[pdf-cf9916f87110e830fc | p.31] POC. Bulk POC does not give a full picture of the ecosystem or its role in 911
[pdf-9244faf9017481b15f | p.31] 31

## p.32 — Page 32

[pdf-008b96ca296a84125c | p.32] biogeochemical cycles. In addition, empirical POC satellite algorithms assume 912
[pdf-68cfdcb007e3b45ba8 | p.32] certain relationships between POC and optical properties. These relationships 913
[pdf-e3776e50bdd08c6542 | p.32] can change if basic characteristics of the POC change, such as its particle size 914
[pdf-68c3eb48ca5130f693 | p.32] distribution (PSD) or the fraction of total POC due to living phytoplankton. For 915
[pdf-c37c62b2e3668a4f29 | p.32] example, the POC-speciﬁc backscattering coeﬃcient can change if the PSD of 916
[pdf-aaf6affc86072352f5 | p.32] POC changes, and the POC-speciﬁc absorption spectra can change if the living 917
[pdf-898ed34d4c9d5b3f47 | p.32] carbon:POC ratio changes (e.g., Stramski et al., 1999; Loisel et al., 2001; Balch 918
[pdf-c24e8b883837002991 | p.32] et al., 2010; Wo´zniak et al., 2010; Cetini´c et al., 2012; Reynolds et al., 2016; 919
[pdf-249292ad1a1dc10d4a | p.32] Kostadinov et al., 2016; Johnson et al., 2017; Koestner et al., 2021; Kostadinov 920
[pdf-5423cc38ff20d6e235 | p.32] et al., 2022). 921
[pdf-173b1ce89bea6f53be | p.32] Notwithstanding the operational limitations of what constitutes POC and dis- 922
[pdf-2575f75acabe269d8a | p.32] solved substances within the submicrometer size range, the particle assemblages 923
[pdf-bd66397773c1c1e6ec | p.32] in the near surface ocean are exceedingly complex, which makes this challenge 924
[pdf-e786fe559f1e9a2193 | p.32] particularly diﬃcult to address. In addition, both forward and inverse modelling 925
[pdf-53a6d83c53b8bd078a | p.32] of the optical properties of the ocean entirely from ﬁrst principles are not feasible 926
[pdf-c74c24870b28d065d0 | p.32] currently. The range from truly dissolved substances to particles such as large 927
[pdf-586772504e8ae85229 | p.32] zooplankton and beyond span many orders of magnitude in size and are governed 928
[pdf-2bb3ac56ce5eb68239 | p.32] by diﬀerent optical regimes, which makes it diﬃcult, for example, to identify, 929
[pdf-6dd2aa61a125a2685f | p.32] quantify, and separate the various sources of optical backscattering in the ocean 930
[pdf-1ad55d88050eb07e48 | p.32] (Stramski et al., 2004; Clavano et al., 2007; Stemmann and Boss, 2012). 931
[pdf-48c7f86dafa0e23cb0 | p.32] In terms of functional fractions, POC can be considered to consist of phyto- 932
[pdf-a70fdba0d6cc16c40f | p.32] plankton, heterotrophic bacteria, zooplankton, and organic detritus (of marine 933
[pdf-df9a213eace50aac7b | p.32] or terrestrial origin). In terms of size fractions, ideally the PSD of POC and its 934
[pdf-3a9da66fccf587ac46 | p.32] various functional components should be measured in situ. There are theoretical 935
[pdf-b8321bb208e2d6000f | p.32] considerations indicating that the marine bulk PSD, spanning several orders of 936
[pdf-44ffc7e8228a9729b6 | p.32] magnitude in size, can follow, to ﬁrst approximation, a power-law with a certain 937
[pdf-9308f52ecb468159e1 | p.32] slope (e.g., Kerr, 1974; Kiefer and Berwald, 1992; Jackson, 1995; Rinaldo et al., 938
[pdf-41202d1e38d0eb988e | p.32] 2002; Brown et al., 2004; Hatton et al., 2021). The power-law approximation of 939
[pdf-df7f67c37491474ec3 | p.32] marine PSD was used in numerous studies involving experimental data of PSD 940
[pdf-119cefdc53323656a9 | p.32] (e.g., Bader, 1970; Sheldon et al., 1972; Jackson et al., 1997; Jonasz and Fournier, 941
[pdf-81ed18d9e3aa632f12 | p.32] 2007; Buonassissi and Dierssen, 2010; Clements et al., 2022) and satellite-based 942
[pdf-b5db688ac6ccb95df2 | p.32] 32

## p.33 — Page 33

[pdf-518d44cba19ae11ff1 | p.33] estimation of PSD (Kostadinov et al., 2009, 2010, 2016, 2022). However, there 943
[pdf-a3ab2b36c0b5384ea8 | p.33] is a challenge associated with the use of power-law approximation because ma- 944
[pdf-ecfbf3f7d67ba3112a | p.33] rine PSDs commonly exhibit some features across diﬀerent size ranges, such as 945
[pdf-388f0050ceb75ef8a1 | p.33] distinct peaks, shoulders, valleys, and changes in slope, which can result in signif- 946
[pdf-4e9ed7f00fe59f8946 | p.33] icant deviations of PSD from a single-slope power function. Such deviations were 947
[pdf-06dd910bc936ffb2bd | p.33] demonstrated in many measurements of PSD in diﬀerent oceanic environments 948
[pdf-7639fbafdf33b655f1 | p.33] (e.g., Jonasz, 1983; Risovi´c, 1993; Bernard et al., 2007; Reynolds et al., 2010; 949
[pdf-594f0ecb52e3740ccf | p.33] White et al., 2015; Organelli et al., 2020; Reynolds and Stramski, 2021). 950
[pdf-6525d492b15198d150 | p.33] Finally, optically complex coastal waters present an additional challenge in 951
[pdf-fac09b24f37084fefb | p.33] that allochthonous and autochthonous sources of POC may be mixed, for example, 952
[pdf-0b94b7cb3e0c43872c | p.33] due to riverine input, making the task of separating POC by functional fractions 953
[pdf-6b267ff7751c95608b | p.33] with known or assumed optical properties or PSD more challenging. 954
[pdf-9045ad1ba8204006f6 | p.33] Gaps: There is a dearth of concurrent data on POC, PSD and carbon data for 955
[pdf-20ca84d63c8f90ae20 | p.33] the components that make up the POC (e.g., phytoplankton carbon). This is a 956
[pdf-2838afb3f61b5e0a40 | p.33] major limiting factor for satellite algorithm development. 957
[pdf-5629258c782e7b75a3 | p.33] Opportunities: There is an opportunity to exploit upcoming hyperspectral 958
[pdf-c689189602cac8a826 | p.33] and polarization remote-sensing data. For example, the degree of linear polariza- 959
[pdf-83fd455ee3a330fdd0 | p.33] tion may provide information on the bulk refractive index of particles (Zhai and 960
[pdf-391de12aa813a8ff5f | p.33] Twardowski, 2021). However, to do so requires eﬀorts directed toward progress 961
[pdf-39ed2aec3a4377a048 | p.33] in basic research into how POC is partitioned into its various components. It 962
[pdf-870d08d52f26024ee0 | p.33] is important to include measurements of PSD in future POC ﬁeld campaigns 963
[pdf-7d1f7a4521f26fa7e3 | p.33] globally, and in the compilation of global, quality-controlled datasets for algo- 964
[pdf-6f4a0953f53d97dca8 | p.33] rithm development. Further studies of non-parametric descriptors of PSD are 965
[pdf-ecd806cb687dc7a00d | p.33] desirable because they oﬀer superior performance compared with the power law 966
[pdf-f7a1a0df50bac72acb | p.33] approximation for representing the contributions of diﬀerent size fractions to PSD 967
[pdf-fa73fa761cab0f736f | p.33] across a wide diversity of marine environments (Reynolds and Stramski, 2021). 968
[pdf-95bdf17d30a51cd33d | p.33] Satellite-based approaches to monitoring zooplankton (e.g. Strömberg et al., 2009; 969
[pdf-86034aeb9d6ddb3d83 | p.33] Basedow et al., 2019; Behrenfeld et al., 2019; Druon et al., 2019) could further aid 970
[pdf-966c538d3e61b30e05 | p.33] in partitioning out the contribution of zooplankton to POC. Additionally, there are 971
[pdf-d7bb05a08f5f1a103f | p.33] opportunities to harness multi-scale observational approaches (e.g., combining 972
[pdf-943e4a01bc5f5db269 | p.33] satellites with ocean robotics) for improved monitoring of POC size fractions 973
[pdf-4d32f9c0ad327de0ae | p.33] 33

## p.34 — Page 34

[pdf-16f17c173a7f1181ce | p.34] (Sauzède et al., 2015, 2016; Claustre et al., 2020). 974
[pdf-f2cab701008a57fdb5 | p.34] 3.2.6. POC priority 5: Vertical proﬁles 975
[pdf-73789af33df4791e52 | p.34] Challenges: Whereas vertical proﬁles of POC can be estimated from in-situ 976
[pdf-0df9028faeb297f4aa | p.34] optical sensors (in particular, backscattering sensors and transmissometers) de- 977
[pdf-e50523bb3b96e1220c | p.34] ployed on autonomous in-situ platforms, the performance of present optical-based 978
[pdf-2aa1df146139501aaa | p.34] POC algorithms is hampered by limited understanding and predictability of varia- 979
[pdf-7d8ae928434b926f11 | p.34] tions in the characteristics of particulate assemblages and their relationships with 980
[pdf-9857867864eb1eb083 | p.34] optical properties throughout the water column. There is a strong requirement to 981
[pdf-efa13f6bd6cf0eb201 | p.34] promote fundamental research to better quantify and understand the relationships 982
[pdf-757a47101f17cf9575 | p.34] between variable vertical proﬁles of POC (and characteristics of the POC such 983
[pdf-676a513fcedd7f64fd | p.34] as PSD, functional and size fractions) and the optical signal detectable from 984
[pdf-c31f3f7cf9e134fdf5 | p.34] satellites. 985
[pdf-60f6dca9a75301d06b | p.34] Gaps: One of the most frequently asked questions posed by users of ocean 986
[pdf-780f61d0714a48075f | p.34] colour remote sensing data (e.g., modellers) is what the satellite sensor actually 987
[pdf-258ee10f74f85f0a61 | p.34] “sees”, in particular how deep the satellite sensor probes the water column in 988
[pdf-35ddc358a92d4766fc | p.34] terms of variable near-surface vertical proﬁles of retrieved data products such as 989
[pdf-e1e1f8b48d2192c636 | p.34] POC. For passive ocean colour, due to the double trip light must take through 990
[pdf-52ad71fb3886915bce | p.34] the water column between the ocean surface and a given depth (downwelling 991
[pdf-e80e5e6d7a25c4a093 | p.34] radiance and then upwelling radiance), the source of the water-leaving optical 992
[pdf-f02b88aef785ab212b | p.34] signal reaching the satellite is heavily weighted to the near-surface layers of the 993
[pdf-747bf8e031c8367c9e | p.34] ocean. Early research from the 1970s demonstrated that ∼90 % of the water- 994
[pdf-83b4da6fe63c227839 | p.34] leaving signal comes from one e-folding attenuation depth, i.e., the layer deﬁned 995
[pdf-c693d843daad463cc4 | p.34] by 1/Kd, where Kd is the wavelength-dependent diﬀuse attenuation coeﬃcient 996
[pdf-f560f959d11f3ad9e8 | p.34] for downwelling irradiance (Gordon and McCluney, 1975). There is a need 997
[pdf-58176fb9bdc31591b4 | p.34] to expand on this research and develop POC-speciﬁc understanding, including 998
[pdf-6672067844db6a8fe7 | p.34] the eﬀects of vertical proﬁles of variables going beyond just bulk POC, namely 999
[pdf-7d51d757a739d48c06 | p.34] POC partitioned by functional and/or size fractions (see POC priority 4). The 1000
[pdf-f2d137fc279a399adb | p.34] diurnal evolution of the characteristics of POC vertical proﬁles also needs careful 1001
[pdf-b529d44c7dec050e21 | p.34] consideration. At present, there is an uneven distribution of vertical in-situ proﬁles 1002
[pdf-6380d49fad7b84a16c | p.34] of POC globally, with the southern hemisphere poorly covered compared with 1003
[pdf-6fcb37ec8cb8b81b8a | p.34] the northern hemisphere. 1004
[pdf-e2dae0d6df8462aa13 | p.34] 34

## p.35 — Page 35

[pdf-a2f9f663ee1f211886 | p.35] Opportunities: There are opportunities to advance basic research into improv- 1005
[pdf-94aefd59d72462ca34 | p.35] ing our understanding of the relationships between POC and optical properties, 1006
[pdf-c34e713f1fae8186b9 | p.35] such as the particulate backscattering coeﬃcient, that are potentially amenable 1007
[pdf-30c750e4f01e0b6a79 | p.35] to measurements from autonomous in-situ platforms such as BGC-Argo ﬂoats. 1008
[pdf-78a8399bb144854c6d | p.35] Artiﬁcial Intelligence (AI) may help in this regard (Claustre et al., 2020). Such 1009
[pdf-a8efbbcd1c8b35dc7d | p.35] research is expected to guide development of new sensors and algorithms (e.g., 1010
[pdf-e46ef6059f17e9b3d5 | p.35] scattering sensors that include polarization) which will ultimately provide more 1011
[pdf-f0d2b20cb284587b67 | p.35] reliable estimations of POC throughout the water column from autonomous 1012
[pdf-f5f1fceba62ffb3915 | p.35] systems. There are opportunities for synergy among satellite, models and au- 1013
[pdf-4207aece4b08d4b7fc | p.35] tonomous platforms to create 3D and 4D ﬁelds of POC (Claustre et al., 2020). 1014
[pdf-f8baf16403bfdf19d8 | p.35] Future active-based satellite lidar systems will penetrate further into the water col- 1015
[pdf-7216f8db9c324c1b44 | p.35] umn improving vertical resolution of variables like the backscattering coeﬃcient, 1016
[pdf-879b2c0d1c1d4eba94 | p.35] a proxy for POC (Jamet et al., 2019). 1017
[pdf-d587453e2c5a0c6d7f | p.35] 3.2.7. POC priority 6: Biogeochemical processes and the biological carbon 1018
[pdf-dc5377f108eedf13e4 | p.35] pump 1019
[pdf-e2e1bc1833ee102e1e | p.35] Challenges: It is estimated that around 80 % of the carbon that is exported 1020
[pdf-4920f722233ab2ab2c | p.35] through the ocean biological carbon pump (BCP) is in the form of POC, and the 1021
[pdf-b5a1f70a8ef92b3503 | p.35] remainder is transported downward as DOC via vertical mixing and advection 1022
[pdf-84cf2750a0f460a2a1 | p.35] (Passow and Carlson, 2012; Legendre et al., 2015; Boyd et al., 2019). The vertical 1023
[pdf-8731dca1f4884d1aeb | p.35] export of POC is challenging to quantify, and believed to result from several 1024
[pdf-9e765ad0a8638ec64a | p.35] biological and physical processes, of which gravitational POC sinking is thought 1025
[pdf-69e82187555713a5d7 | p.35] to be the largest component (Boyd et al., 2019). For a ﬁxed ﬂuid viscosity and 1026
[pdf-ec623218823bfd2542 | p.35] density, gravitational sinking speed is a function of particle size, composition, 1027
[pdf-637e6e803aed689283 | p.35] and structure (Laurenceau-Cornec et al., 2020; Cael et al., 2021). The distribution 1028
[pdf-6c1b69e90662e7a196 | p.35] of these properties in the particle population results to a large extent from the 1029
[pdf-e415ba147e8aa257a3 | p.35] functioning of the upper-ocean ecosystem. Therefore, overcoming the challenges 1030
[pdf-372e7e8e5bbb694bc4 | p.35] related to the satellite retrieval of POC mass (POC priority 3), size distribution 1031
[pdf-ae5af3df4067a6dc70 | p.35] (POC priority 4), and vertical distribution (POC priority 5), as well as particle 1032
[pdf-db4ec7b00ca79dbef9 | p.35] properties (e.g., composition), is key to improved understanding and prediction 1033
[pdf-ba045c33071a849f3f | p.35] of the BCP. 1034
[pdf-4f9a432c53a78c0e8b | p.35] Quantifying the global vertical POC export ﬂux is a major challenge, as the 1035
[pdf-3323e9f163e2726188 | p.35] 35

## p.36 — Page 36

[pdf-20f60891dd29567f2e | p.36] range of current estimates (ca. 5-15 Gt C yr−1; Boyd et al., 2019) remains similar 1036
[pdf-d66e66fd90e258f5c2 | p.36] to the ranges quoted in the 1980’s (Martin et al., 1987; Henson et al., 2022). 1037
[pdf-99899508061128959c | p.36] Improved ability to estimate the concentration and ﬂuxes of POC (gravitational 1038
[pdf-bd2a52785f7c066ab7 | p.36] sinking, but also other pathways like the migrant pumps and physical pumps) 1039
[pdf-3678544edf0e4fc48c | p.36] would also beneﬁt the study of trace element cycling (Conway et al., 2021) and 1040
[pdf-9d5b522ee8a8296205 | p.36] deep-ocean ecosystems that rely on POC export. Current methods to measure 1041
[pdf-0df76e2d6020d29fdd | p.36] gravitational POC export are work-intensive and do not allow for high spatial- 1042
[pdf-a345b3e25fa3db5a8c | p.36] temporal coverage, nor do they cover other pathways of carbon export, such 1043
[pdf-9a339f05cbb007372e | p.36] as the migrant and mixing pumps, that contribute to a large portion of carbon 1044
[pdf-b266b7570c0a374a0d | p.36] export (Boyd et al., 2019) and change the sequestration times of exported carbon. 1045
[pdf-d0459611db5c2ab1ca | p.36] Moreover, they often rely on simpliﬁed assumptions (steady-state vertical proﬁles, 1046
[pdf-b4021e874d183ff042 | p.36] negligible eﬀects of horizontal advection, to name just a few) whose validity 1047
[pdf-f3245af15950e6e424 | p.36] is not always tested or subjected to sensitivity analyses (Buesseler et al., 2020). 1048
[pdf-6a8fc4acef90cbdbe6 | p.36] Therefore, empirical (e.g., remote-sensing based) and prognostic models of gravi- 1049
[pdf-c35286ea698c4b9b55 | p.36] tational POC export rely on in-situ measurements that are inherently uncertain 1050
[pdf-479ae77adf72b14476 | p.36] and have sparse spatial-temporal coverage. 1051
[pdf-7e13bc1b6eb6692ce7 | p.36] Gaps: There is a sparsity of in-situ data on vertical ﬂuxes of POC, meaning our 1052
[pdf-9f4ede418ba8b9e0a3 | p.36] understanding of the relationship between upper-ocean biogeochemical properties 1053
[pdf-8ea77467fd67c3849a | p.36] and vertical POC ﬂuxes is very uncertain. This impedes our ability to represent 1054
[pdf-86ee96276dc7233e83 | p.36] POC ﬂux in empirical and mechanistic models of the BCP. Large-scale estimates 1055
[pdf-12096fee1da9f12fcc | p.36] of vertical POC export usually focus on the average (climatological) state of 1056
[pdf-d3b9be84c82ee2ac18 | p.36] the ocean, but interannual variations and their drivers (e.g., the role of physical 1057
[pdf-63a668ab98e40975a6 | p.36] forcing) remain poorly known (Lomas et al., 2022), and because of data sparseness 1058
[pdf-89a737f43bf5aa3c1a | p.36] there is a risk of confounding spatial and temporal variability. 1059
[pdf-a0553392268e0214a8 | p.36] Although shallow seas and continental slope areas are thought to play an 1060
[pdf-9124846989fb1bde1e | p.36] important role in the global POC cycle, there are large gaps in understanding, 1061
[pdf-3799cfec1cd47108fc | p.36] as the sources and fate of POC in these areas remain diﬃcult to monitor and 1062
[pdf-ae66a28928243ed178 | p.36] quantify owing to the presence of optically complex environments, the higher 1063
[pdf-a5b413b396ac923d67 | p.36] abundance of inorganic particulate materials and the potentially larger role of 1064
[pdf-807f6c819d67d0d639 | p.36] lateral advection (Arístegui et al., 2020). Finally, gaps in understanding of the 1065
[pdf-0b3345b4574481c51b | p.36] role of zooplankton diel vertical migration (DVM) (e.g., Bianchi et al., 2013a,b; 1066
[pdf-2e1edace484ed5ca04 | p.36] 36

## p.37 — Page 37

[pdf-2b45254a5629c31286 | p.37] Boyd et al., 2019) and the associated biogenic hydrodynamic transport (BHT) 1067
[pdf-06438097a0837011ae | p.37] (e.g., Wilhelmus et al., 2019), mean these processes are rarely incorporated into 1068
[pdf-4ccc3789f4c14af29c | p.37] ocean biogeochemical models. 1069
[pdf-aa6832d8fd4eca40f9 | p.37] Opportunities: Sampling from autonomous platforms (BGC-Argo, gliders, 1070
[pdf-2dceb638dc2da96c90 | p.37] moorings, etc.) can provide the spatial-temporal resolution needed to reﬁne our 1071
[pdf-8e868b61b07deed08b | p.37] understanding of the BCP, complementing more detailed shipborne observations 1072
[pdf-bfd1d0d5884a0b4a4d | p.37] and the synoptic surface view obtained from satellites. For example, "optical 1073
[pdf-4843f79187f0f4f744 | p.37] sediment traps” mounted on BGC-Argo ﬂoats (Bishop et al., 2004; Estapa et al., 1074
[pdf-b3040b843774d91e69 | p.37] 2017) can record a nearly-continuous proxy of vertical POC ﬂuxes in the ocean 1075
[pdf-a5f95cead4b9a6787e | p.37] interior. 1076
[pdf-f06d563375226c8652 | p.37] Merging of these various data streams using statistical techniques (e.g., ma- 1077
[pdf-2e60c0e950c37f5175 | p.37] chine learning; Sauzéde et al., 2020) can allow for reﬁned estimates of the BCP, 1078
[pdf-c148bdebc110df98f6 | p.37] reducing the sampling bias associated with shipborne measurements. These com- 1079
[pdf-fa7cd400fb2bc785b4 | p.37] plementary data streams can be further used to constrain mechanistic models 1080
[pdf-6278d4f1400499d787 | p.37] of the BCP, for example, through data assimilation and parameter optimization 1081
[pdf-489512ec55a2bc8831 | p.37] (Nowicki et al., 2022). These approaches will improve quantiﬁcation of the ﬂuxes 1082
[pdf-cfc69d023b299d5736 | p.37] that form the BCP, help identify knowledge gaps and eventually spur progress 1083
[pdf-fb1d212eb0d8755075 | p.37] in process-level understanding. Ongoing eﬀorts are aimed at improving under- 1084
[pdf-3fea8106eeb9ae9a1a | p.37] standing of the eﬀects of DVM and BHT on the biological pump, through a 1085
[pdf-42b09593a1c98a94dd | p.37] synergy of remote-sensing (e.g., Behrenfeld et al., 2019), laboratory studies, and 1086
[pdf-501ea840af2f5a34d7 | p.37] biogeochemical modelling. 1087
[pdf-43c4e593911b51718a | p.37] Although the framework drafted above is conceptually valid for the study of 1088
[pdf-3f0253b1aa13797c20 | p.37] continental shelves, these areas require higher-resolution observations and models 1089
[pdf-dd433c1355615e5798 | p.37] that can resolve their larger heterogeneity and a wider array of transport and 1090
[pdf-f09e29eb293a72bd36 | p.37] transformation processes. Therefore, such areas would beneﬁt from dedicated 1091
[pdf-178e2beb7c6578ba70 | p.37] regional process studies and monitoring from geostationary satellites and other 1092
[pdf-98ad94039723aa4f51 | p.37] airborne sensors. 1093
[pdf-02e300a536e7f249a5 | p.37] 3.3. Phytoplankton Carbon (C-phyto) 1094
[pdf-2efafa6a974c6d8c31 | p.37] The living pool of POC can be partitioned into components associated with 1095
[pdf-c05e3c8631c7fba34e | p.37] living phytoplankton cells and other types of carbon (e.g., zooplankton, detritus, 1096
[pdf-3685405d97c08aa26c | p.37] 37

## p.38 — Page 38

[pdf-17215935bddce8afde | p.38] fecal pellets). C-phyto is a particularly important pool of POC owing to its role 1097
[pdf-213c8f03ceec5e2f53 | p.38] in marine PP and providing food to the majority of the marine ecosystem. It has 1098
[pdf-4d8803349718ad0709 | p.38] been estimated that the pool is around 0.78 - 1.0 Gt C in size (Falkowski et al., 1099
[pdf-334032f97134a31380 | p.38] 1998; Le Quéré et al., 2005), but despite its small size (relative to terrestrial plants, 1100
[pdf-a7b2280278f7158735 | p.38] which is in the order to 450 Gt C, see Bar-On et al., 2018) it contributes around 1101
[pdf-e2f4ac3609c3dd315f | p.38] 50 Gt C yr−1 in PP (equivalent to terrestrial plants, see Section 3.1). 1102
[pdf-366e875e8c0fe11d9c | p.38] C-phyto is key to establishing the carbon-to-chlorophyll ratio (important for 1103
[pdf-d9fb91b7253b335a6b | p.38] understanding phytoplankton physiology and their adaptation to light, nutrient 1104
[pdf-907a65962b64de9414 | p.38] and temperature changes), to compute PP using carbon-based models (Behren- 1105
[pdf-9ef9c3ee5d0373f12a | p.38] feld et al., 2005; Sathyendranath et al., 2009), and to assess the contribution of 1106
[pdf-7fe3187d469540a0d1 | p.38] photophysiology to the phytoplankton seasonal cycle (Bellacicco et al., 2016). 1107
[pdf-31663ed97a0faf7c70 | p.38] High temporal C-phyto data allows for determination of carbon-based growth and 1108
[pdf-dfe55b2aefe67198de | p.38] loss rates in phytoplankton (e.g., Sathyendranath et al., 2009; Zhai et al., 2010; 1109
[pdf-ba8dff90e623aa1fec | p.38] Behrenfeld and Boss, 2014). C-phyto has also been innovatively used to assess, 1110
[pdf-31ec3870e33a7b354d | p.38] at the sea-air interface, the export of organic matter towards the atmosphere in the 1111
[pdf-c2defe526b4ab4de6a | p.38] form of aerosols (O’Dowd et al., 2004; Fossum et al., 2018). 1112
[pdf-49841f5390d0309d2e | p.38] 3.3.1. State of the art in Phytoplankton Carbon 1113
[pdf-831d946618f3f5fc1a | p.38] A number of algorithms have been developed to derive C-phyto from ocean 1114
[pdf-8d462972b27b7db914 | p.38] colour observations (see Bellacicco et al. (2020) and reference therein, and Section 1115
[pdf-7819a4bb8d88f6f0d7 | p.38] 4.1.3.2. of Brewin et al. (2021)). The approaches used can be grouped broadly 1116
[pdf-23eba0631d6cfa43f3 | p.38] into: i) backscattering-based approaches (e.g., Behrenfeld et al., 2005; Martínez- 1117
[pdf-a278c5219b6c8ebd64 | p.38] Vicente et al., 2013; Graﬀet al., 2015); ii) chlorophyll-based approaches(e.g. 1118
[pdf-cda1dc9e1baebba2c8 | p.38] Sathyendranath et al., 2009) some with use of models of photoacclimation and 1119
[pdf-af3898159de0a8d7f4 | p.38] physiology parameters (e.g., Jackson et al., 2017; Sathyendranath et al., 2020); 1120
[pdf-8c83010bf019a27290 | p.38] and iii) size-class-based approaches (e.g., Kostadinov et al., 2016, 2022; Roy 1121
[pdf-d3f513096ad328cc13 | p.38] et al., 2017). These approaches can also be grouped according to their product 1122
[pdf-a2e4f370694a615918 | p.38] (PSD, size class or taxonomic class) or the optical properties used to derive 1123
[pdf-f16ef931cca2953a73 | p.38] them (Chl-abundance based, backscatter, absorption, radiance) (Mouw et al., 1124
[pdf-3c6f9feaa8986b7221 | p.38] 2017). Each approach relies on the covariation between optical properties or POC, 1125
[pdf-db8a31c967a74a2a49 | p.38] and a proxy of phytoplankton concentration such as Chl-a, phytoplankton light 1126
[pdf-b69bc6b8a06e93db07 | p.38] absorption or size distribution. Satellite environmental data, such as light or sea- 1127
[pdf-9a79d4af5b7b4638e4 | p.38] 38

## p.39 — Page 39

[pdf-b81730770a3cdc1377 | p.39] surface temperature (SST), have been shown to help improve satellite retrievals of 1128
[pdf-bb8e1850a0250c3bb5 | p.39] the chlorophyll-a concentration of diﬀerent phytoplankton groups (Ward, 2015; 1129
[pdf-7cbbf68069c134b71b | p.39] Brewin et al., 2015a, 2017a; Moore and Brown, 2020; Xi et al., 2021; Sun et al., 1130
[pdf-a2e73eddd1b676d005 | p.39] 2023), and recently also for retrievals of diatom carbon concentration (Chase 1131
[pdf-93f525cd95c8f0c45e | p.39] et al., 2022). 1132
[pdf-155d4d37473f513a31 | p.39] One of the biggest challenges in retrieving C-phyto from ocean colour obser- 1133
[pdf-0b8569ae2e6fcda342 | p.39] vations is separating the contributions of organic detritus, or non-algal particles 1134
[pdf-4d4d0f48dc26ae03b7 | p.39] (NAP), and living phytoplankton cells to the optical properties, such as the par- 1135
[pdf-dd9fa92f50b3ec57c7 | p.39] ticle backscattering, and to the particle size distributions, particularly in turbid 1136
[pdf-bd5cd04dfdd29da895 | p.39] or coastal waters. It is assumed that phytoplankton (and co-varying material) 1137
[pdf-b003ad7332a48f9e64 | p.39] control the backscattering signal in the open ocean (Dall’Olmo et al., 2009; Or- 1138
[pdf-a5d8866baf4cbf4b25 | p.39] ganelli et al., 2018), an assumption used in Case-1 water models (e.g., Morel and 1139
[pdf-6ad8e70075c908fc8a | p.39] Maritorena, 2001). However, the variation of NAP horizontally, vertically, and 1140
[pdf-531df1b860689b4178 | p.39] temporally is considerable in many parts of the ocean (Bellacicco et al., 2019, 1141
[pdf-e914150107890c947b | p.39] 2020) in size and concentration (Organelli et al., 2020). Recent eﬀorts have been 1142
[pdf-7645df4c1bb8257b27 | p.39] made to improve C-phyto estimates from satellite-based particle backscattering 1143
[pdf-8c55fc0ecbce816399 | p.39] by accounting for variability in NAP (e.g., Bellacicco et al., 2020). 1144
[pdf-afe7b5281d4e3f05f9 | p.39] Each of the proposed approaches have advantages and disadvantages, and 1145
[pdf-193c0481829425621d | p.39] can be improved with knowledge on the optics-to-carbon conversion factors (that 1146
[pdf-f9866663a10836d834 | p.39] can inform the Chl-a to C ratio), using in-situ C-phyto datasets (e.g., Martínez- 1147
[pdf-22c6e67d805ad52e00 | p.39] Vicente et al., 2017), and through reduced uncertainties in satellite-derived inputs 1148
[pdf-311a151ded0141aa2a | p.39] of relevant quantities (i.e., backscattering, Chl-a, and particle size distribution). 1149
[pdf-3f61c9999c0593a962 | p.39] Currently, no method has extended the global estimation of C-phyto to below the 1150
[pdf-32d17b9d155a94d27b | p.39] ocean surface where many biogeochemical interactions occur. 1151
[pdf-148c60d5cd0cb8f044 | p.39] During the workshop, three key priority areas of C-phyto were identiﬁed, that 1152
[pdf-60fb6fa3a0b9bfb590 | p.39] will be discussed separately in this section, and include: 1) in-situ data; 2) satellite 1153
[pdf-e70bd76208452b85d7 | p.39] algorithm retrievals; and 3) vertical structure. Table 5 summarises these priorities, 1154
[pdf-1f52564667e425f085 | p.39] and their challenges, gaps and opportunities. 1155
[pdf-cb929b0cc1e1ed31e0 | p.39] 3.3.2. C-phyto priority 1: In-situ data 1156
[pdf-45c66d0fd36764d7a0 | p.39] Challenges: Measuring C-phyto in-situ is notoriously diﬃcult and no stan- 1157
[pdf-16de5bf96d354d4a5f | p.39] dard method exists and any measurements are likely to have high uncertainties. 1158
[pdf-b43b62ab567c32f4c9 | p.39] 39

## p.40 — Page 40

[pdf-44cfb2c4ed996140c8 | p.40] A major challenge for communities working in this ﬁeld is to improve in-situ 1159
[pdf-6201300be356bca946 | p.40] methodologies for quantifying C-phyto and to measure or estimate photoacclima- 1160
[pdf-ca9c3f1752c674f067 | p.40] tion model parameters. A couple of methods exist to directly measure C-phyto. 1161
[pdf-06e96afef85b1dd90d | p.40] One of them entails the separation of living phytoplankton particles from non- 1162
[pdf-e50a7a973737930cbc | p.40] living (detrital) particles and the subsequent elemental measurement of those 1163
[pdf-ff6f44f20ed27e2156 | p.40] particles (Graﬀet al., 2012, 2015). Another, older method (Redalje and Laws, 1164
[pdf-e4a53ff9682fe692a2 | p.40] 1981), requires incubation experiments in which the sample cells are labelled with 1165
[pdf-128d1fe65a48624a20 | p.40] 14C, and the speciﬁc activity of Chl-a is measured at the end of the experiment as 1166
[pdf-8655edb6c4fcd7c276 | p.40] well as the total particulate 14C activity. The direct measurement methodology 1167
[pdf-3c298bf0d4e4bc8641 | p.40] of Graﬀet al. (2012, 2015) is largely biased towards nano and pico-sized phyto- 1168
[pdf-a3b735a19623531fd0 | p.40] plankton particles detected by ﬂow cytometry, whereas the method of Redalje 1169
[pdf-12a8b32fd7957ad481 | p.40] and Laws (1981) depends on Chl-a being suﬃciently high for the incubation 1170
[pdf-730358fe06dac12436 | p.40] experiments. It is important that these direct methods are incorporated into exist- 1171
[pdf-5729abd029c0c76aa4 | p.40] ing programs. C-phyto may also be indirectly measured by applying empirical 1172
[pdf-e68979cff980c0fb6f | p.40] relationships that relate cell biovolume to C-phyto (Menden-Deuer and Lessard, 1173
[pdf-3f7d9d4972250b02e7 | p.40] 2000; Lomas et al., 2019). These empirical relationships are largely attributed to 1174
[pdf-483ab4847e1f0eac69 | p.40] micro-sized phytoplankton (diatoms and dinoﬂagellates) and are limited to either 1175
[pdf-d764d191180d31e436 | p.40] a select number of laboratory cultures or a speciﬁc region in the global ocean. 1176
[pdf-30fe5e40e45dc81d3e | p.40] Standardization of phytoplankton carbon data submission using emerging in-situ 1177
[pdf-588375ae8b0d6451ec | p.40] techniques (such as the Imaging FlowCytobot) is also challenging (Neeley et al., 1178
[pdf-24f81ae676ae2d1b30 | p.40] 2021). 1179
[pdf-e96e068295caff2eb7 | p.40] Gaps: As a direct result of this challenge, one of the largest gaps for de- 1180
[pdf-2a049c5ed292e2561e | p.40] riving C-phyto from space is the paucity of global in-situ C-phyto data (and 1181
[pdf-c3464ffae62d20b6c9 | p.40] C-phyto community composition), to develop and validate models and algorithms. 1182
[pdf-e7afb062be1c1b9978 | p.40] Coincident in-situ observations of both phytoplankton community composition, 1183
[pdf-eaed2e22de31123178 | p.40] by ﬂow cytometry, microscopy or the more recent method of imaging-in-ﬂow 1184
[pdf-f8213f257e02f5f905 | p.40] cytometry (e.g., Imaging Flow Cytobot (IFCB), FlowCam) with bio-optical and 1185
[pdf-8b8415192888bae68a | p.40] radiometric measurements are critical for establishing relationships among phy- 1186
[pdf-e3cda07af3d2ab5ca9 | p.40] toplankton type, size, pigments and optical signatures. Only limited number of 1187
[pdf-2bf9d30dc6a7200f40 | p.40] ﬁeld data sets (e.g., NASA’s EXPORTS campaign, and the Atlantic Meridional 1188
[pdf-582d5c2df10e62213d | p.40] Transect Programme (AMT)) contain these coincident measurements, leading to 1189
[pdf-fe88217ad42bc78d28 | p.40] 40

## p.41 — Page 41

[pdf-781a3663a62b345712 | p.41] a lack of understanding of their temporal or spatial variability. Moreover, few 1190
[pdf-25c6c6daa6ece6b7d7 | p.41] measurements are taken below the surface ocean (see C-phyto priority 3). 1191
[pdf-e1d71e08d040ebfa9f | p.41] Additionally, there are very few consistent C-phyto surface time-series data 1192
[pdf-15db9921514c6a1cd6 | p.41] sets available. Time series data sets with clear uncertainties are critical to 1193
[pdf-6fb87fa4cd11c158b3 | p.41] understanding of spatio-temporal variability in C-phyto, community composi- 1194
[pdf-31fc8536dc98ff11d4 | p.41] tion and coincident optical properties. Existing time-series studies that include 1195
[pdf-1116f07d85cf0c65a5 | p.41] these measurements are limited (e.g., Martha’s Vineyard Coastal observatory, 1196
[pdf-e513161c413932d200 | p.41] https://nes-lter.whoi.edu/). 1197
[pdf-b68f9f03adaf62997c | p.41] Opportunities: There is an opportunity to enlarge and explore data collected 1198
[pdf-d96d633d3aea0d35d0 | p.41] at so-called "in-situ supersites". In-situ supersites are sampling sites in which 1199
[pdf-3d417a6db220ca84cb | p.41] manual or automated, coincident measurements of bio-optical, biogeochemical, 1200
[pdf-63bd161e9b74c34fd4 | p.41] and/or biological measurements, are collected regularly as part of a time series 1201
[pdf-5e503bfa350f480999 | p.41] program. These sites are typically co-located with satellite measurements and can 1202
[pdf-270d1ba2cf7b701c41 | p.41] be used to improve and/or validate satellite algorithms. Such sites already exist 1203
[pdf-c0aa41457a2218e328 | p.41] and include, for example, the Martha’s Vineyard Coastal Observatory (MVCO), 1204
[pdf-a15c819941ebab4213 | p.41] located in Edgartown, Massachusetts, USA. At this observatory, hydrographic 1205
[pdf-eb8c7df5216010edaf | p.41] (salinity, temperature), meteorological and biological measurements are collected 1206
[pdf-d3dd4f2b7e3ced0ba9 | p.41] in real-time. What makes the data from this observatory particularly powerful 1207
[pdf-a2276b31935915420e | p.41] is the inclusion of an IFCB that collects particle and plankton images approxi- 1208
[pdf-c3f6b309e636cb76c0 | p.41] mately every 20-minutes. In conjunction with regular ship-based measurements 1209
[pdf-7139c268b7f6fa1f85 | p.41] through the Northeast Shelf LTER (NES-LTER) program as well as satellite-based 1210
[pdf-8b9e3b1925ebb31ad5 | p.41] observations, not only are these data instrumental to advancing algorithms to 1211
[pdf-3f8c0aebaf7cc1d767 | p.41] retrieve phytoplankton taxonomy, but they also advance our understanding of how 1212
[pdf-5c7a7ed54803348f74 | p.41] climate variability impacts phytoplankton communities and, ultimately the food 1213
[pdf-8b7d5281a9466ebae2 | p.41] web (Hunter-Cevera et al., 2021). Moreover, phytoplankton observations can be 1214
[pdf-3a77a6eb6ba0d641e3 | p.41] used to derive estimates of C-phyto, which are necessary for the development 1215
[pdf-b87d3ceeb3804e14cf | p.41] and validation of C-phyto algorithms by linking C-phyto to measured optical 1216
[pdf-8e5aec2cd68e0f00f9 | p.41] properties and considering the diversity and variation of phytoplankton and other 1217
[pdf-fb8450bafb04fec9d8 | p.41] optical constituents. Other sites, such as the Palmer Station Antarctic LTER and 1218
[pdf-5939c2236a6220381f | p.41] the BATS station have included regular observations of phytoplankton taxonomy 1219
[pdf-a55a1adf9fa9476b7f | p.41] and bio-optics as part of their sampling strategies and these data may also be 1220
[pdf-79ea7fad72f1d9fb6d | p.41] 41

## p.42 — Page 42

[pdf-fbbd3e9406f3d5b91c | p.42] used for C-phyto estimations and algorithm development (Casey et al., 2013; 1221
[pdf-32504fa7fcbe441815 | p.42] Nardelli et al., 2022). Similarly, at the Acqua Alta Oceanographic LTER site 1222
[pdf-b6c24f029661b573df | p.42] (AAOT; www.ismar.cnr.it), located in the Gulf of Venice (Mediterranean Sea), 1223
[pdf-d4b06f4a422961186f | p.42] several essential ocean variables (EOVs) including phytoplankton taxonomy have 1224
[pdf-ce56b3a17a2f7391a7 | p.42] been collected for decades (Acri et al., 2020) and these observations have been 1225
[pdf-c70b46170bfc8d249c | p.42] recently empowered with an IFCB for continuous measurements. AAOT is also 1226
[pdf-b08f6e876282e9e97a | p.42] an AERONET and HYPERNET site and used for CAL/VAL activities of OCR 1227
[pdf-a99d3ff36561e35998 | p.42] satellites (Concha et al., 2021). Moving forward, we must empower additional 1228
[pdf-d0788380a6f0aec24c | p.42] observatories, such as those used for water quality assessment, and expand the 1229
[pdf-54df02ca0e8f524545 | p.42] range of data they collect, to strive towards the collection of the entire size spec- 1230
[pdf-178b0f7c6e153f201f | p.42] trum of phytoplankton required for satellite C-phyto algorithms (e.g., microscopy, 1231
[pdf-885896e7f6c156d805 | p.42] imaging-in-ﬂow cytometry, ﬂow cytometry). Supersite measurements could even 1232
[pdf-2ce2e06afa652c2415 | p.42] be complemented by dedicated mesocosm experiments that will help to improve 1233
[pdf-165dc71c6d82e4daf3 | p.42] the mechanistic understanding of the relationship between C-phyto and optical 1234
[pdf-bf4d1f885f8b0fa1bd | p.42] properties. In addition, these data sets can be used to derive reliable uncertainties 1235
[pdf-bea2e33f63601a7f80 | p.42] in in-situ C-phyto data. A future network of these supersites could be established 1236
[pdf-a6b026efe86f128bfb | p.42] to be representative of global scales, and not only collect data at the surface but 1237
[pdf-592b01688b277fae37 | p.42] also throughout the euphotic zone and beyond. 1238
[pdf-5a698a4ab67fda243b | p.42] Another opportunity is to improve the global distribution of optical property 1239
[pdf-024f30fe1fc73d6cfc | p.42] measurements used as input of C-phyto algorithms by empowering validation 1240
[pdf-80439d296ee97624a5 | p.42] through continuous underway optical measurements (e.g. Slade et al., 2010; 1241
[pdf-1a9d50de902b53c468 | p.42] Brewin et al., 2016; Rasse et al., 2017; Burt et al., 2018) and autonomous mobile 1242
[pdf-fd614621bab5432b8b | p.42] platforms such as BGC-Argo proﬁling ﬂoats and Lagrangian drifters (e.g., Abbott 1243
[pdf-1886f054575c800b29 | p.42] et al., 1990; Boss et al., 2008; Sauzède et al., 2016; Bisson et al., 2019; Xing 1244
[pdf-db223b6f24d1ecb545 | p.42] et al., 2020). For the latter, these robotic platforms allow the acquisition of optical 1245
[pdf-f06376811dd8eb80a7 | p.42] data with limited spatial and temporal bias, as they also collect data in remote 1246
[pdf-026cd3d6075ffc31eb | p.42] regions, even during meteorological conditions that are unfavourable for ship- 1247
[pdf-d706fb841eb248b162 | p.42] based sampling (Organelli et al., 2017). Optical data from these platforms, or 1248
[pdf-dc58ab573f6a48223d | p.42] similar technologies, have been used to derive bulk properties, such as diﬀuse 1249
[pdf-49956a10df4a765382 | p.42] attenuation (Kd), Chl-a, CDOM and POC, and are a source of sub-surface data, 1250
[pdf-ca94645915cf47ad38 | p.42] complementary to the surface data from satellites. As hyperspectral data can 1251
[pdf-b5d650e478b76d7ef0 | p.42] 42

## p.43 — Page 43

[pdf-f5567f185728339629 | p.43] help resolve estimates on the composition (type and size) of phytoplankton 1252
[pdf-0d35f69a1adfaf7a42 | p.43] (Chase et al., 2013; Liu et al., 2019), integrating autonomous instrumentation with 1253
[pdf-a32283f0a8d15d9bdf | p.43] hyperspectral capabilities (Jemai et al., 2021; Organelli et al., 2021) can provide 1254
[pdf-b2edfd86cc0f0014cb | p.43] insight into phytoplankton composition in the illuminated part of the water column 1255
[pdf-960ad0e8aa91b3713c | p.43] (Bracher et al., 2020). Eﬀorts to enlarge the optical multi-platform data acquisition, 1256
[pdf-f31767380921e66d88 | p.43] and to develop protocols for the derivation of high-quality C-phyto data sets, must 1257
[pdf-4647438c10ad90b79a | p.43] be taken since these have the potential to ﬁll the gap of C-phyto information below 1258
[pdf-27992db439caf0fe3a | p.43] the ﬁrst optical depth and provide information on phytoplankton photoacclimation 1259
[pdf-d43de9670877381b1e | p.43] (see C-phyto priority 3). Additionally, there maybe future possibilities to connect 1260
[pdf-f8ab5cb6d16c692413 | p.43] genetic level information, and at the particle/organismal level, with phytoplankton 1261
[pdf-3e5c7b95541e159b0d | p.43] carbon properties (Braakman et al., 2017). 1262
[pdf-1d352f9ae89d588ba8 | p.43] 3.3.3. C-phyto priority 2: Satellite algorithm retrievals 1263
[pdf-7b8cd7d8457fcb2135 | p.43] Challenges: Backscattering is an optical property that has been linked to 1264
[pdf-1a420194cb001589bd | p.43] C-phyto. However, particle backscatter includes all particles, not just phytoplank- 1265
[pdf-9d8216a157b494d272 | p.43] ton and it is challenging to separate phytoplankton from non-living particles, 1266
[pdf-fe4e4bb4b79dfb9dfe | p.43] without complementary information such as microscopic or ﬂow cytometric data. 1267
[pdf-c7a29a62302456cde5 | p.43] Additionally, we should strive to increase the accuracy of backscattering retrievals 1268
[pdf-7656626d286136f85f | p.43] from space, itself a challenging task. Correcting the remote sensing reﬂectance 1269
[pdf-5b16ef9a10a0eb10f9 | p.43] for Raman scattering prior to semi-analytical retrievals has shown some promise 1270
[pdf-4238a5858e8b4b251a | p.43] for improving quality of back-scattering retrievals (Westberry et al., 2013; Lee 1271
[pdf-68905e18f96e882950 | p.43] et al., 2013; Pitarch et al., 2019). 1272
[pdf-7aad4d22f5af1b646c | p.43] Chl-a, both satellite-derived and in situ, is often used in models that relate 1273
[pdf-6ae46bea9a3ce8f16a | p.43] particle backscatter to C-phyto through empirical relationships. However, the 1274
[pdf-789b9657dcd9e37573 | p.43] uncertainties within these empirical relationships are increased by the inﬂuence of 1275
[pdf-c6c9c63d985e563259 | p.43] phytoplankton composition and the physiological state of phytoplankton driving 1276
[pdf-747172a898f9534e59 | p.43] photoacclimation, i.e., the adjustment of Chl-a in response to light, particularly in 1277
[pdf-44941b611b0d1cf177 | p.43] the surface ocean, and uncertainties in Chl-a measurements. In addition, in low 1278
[pdf-872cb52577cfa203a1 | p.43] phytoplankton biomass regions, such as in the subtropical gyres, uncertainties in 1279
[pdf-d7f161b636b77c279e | p.43] both satellite retrieved optical properties and Chl-a can be large. 1280
[pdf-78735ae2655f005ebe | p.43] Gaps: There is a gap in our mechanistic understanding of how optical proper- 1281
[pdf-6f66942c87c04352fa | p.43] ties link to C-phyto, considering the diversity of phytoplankton composition and 1282
[pdf-a4d9d86be5383dd643 | p.43] 43

## p.44 — Page 44

[pdf-153cbaf81b209ec28f | p.44] their physiological state, and the other optically signiﬁcant substances that can 1283
[pdf-5b8b1182c0d7698b9c | p.44] have an impact on the optical properties. 1284
[pdf-c17b61e82123e467a3 | p.44] Each of the methods, models and algorithms, have uncertainties, either in- 1285
[pdf-fd96015cff954b3350 | p.44] herent or owing to the input data, which are infrequently reported. As such, 1286
[pdf-10ee038b7b2619ef77 | p.44] there are gaps in our knowledge of the accuracy of our models and algorithms 1287
[pdf-ea7fa96b370d958485 | p.44] to derive C-phyto, which includes uncertainties associated with direct or indirect 1288
[pdf-678ebd3171be9be85a | p.44] measurements of in-situ C-phyto. 1289
[pdf-d40f4f2d115e1ce29e | p.44] Opportunities: There are opportunities to produce long time-series of C- 1290
[pdf-a419f3078996f1fa4f | p.44] phyto data using merged ocean-colour datasets (e.g., OC-CCI (https://www. 1291
[pdf-abda6167c80d58e000 | p.44] oceancolour.org), GlobColour (https://www.globcolour.info), and Copernicus 1292
[pdf-68c3c8fdf8afc59137 | p.44] Marine (https://marine.copernicus.eu); Maritorena et al., 2010; Sathyendranath 1293
[pdf-e5f9e9df445d7c1562 | p.44] et al., 2019a; Kostadinov et al., 2022), or by adapting algorithms to operate on 1294
[pdf-02d3a37a86b72fb8c2 | p.44] diﬀerent ocean colour sensors that cover diﬀerent time spans (e.g., since 1979 1295
[pdf-72d88131e5649209a1 | p.44] until today; Oziel et al., 2022). These products should include pixel-by-pixel 1296
[pdf-b50bed66340fc7a407 | p.44] uncertainties. C-phyto satellite algorithms may be improved by using synergistic 1297
[pdf-1942617761cf844201 | p.44] information on the abundance and composition of the diﬀerent optical components 1298
[pdf-d11d213c6d6d309b03 | p.44] (phytoplankton, NAP, CDOM), which may lower the uncertainties in C-phyto 1299
[pdf-4a8f3d918d41fdc82d | p.44] retrievals. 1300
[pdf-0d45f0ef1288d176ce | p.44] There are also opportunities to improve C-phyto products by exploring the 1301
[pdf-3ad083dad5067287d8 | p.44] combined use of satellite data with ecosystem modelling. Directly using satellite 1302
[pdf-a16dd72bbd378c89eb | p.44] Chl-a or phytoplankton community-speciﬁc Chl-a for evaluation or assimilation 1303
[pdf-4fbac46aeb370a5b54 | p.44] in (coupled-ocean-) biogeochemical models could be a promising avenue for 1304
[pdf-71e301e19133cbd3b5 | p.44] deriving C-phyto (IOCCG, 2020). Other exciting avenues of research include 1305
[pdf-bd744a619909d81860 | p.44] combining models of photoacclimation with size-based approaches (Sathyen- 1306
[pdf-bbd1d719ac088b304c | p.44] dranath et al., 2020), that can be reconciled with models of PP, meaning the 1307
[pdf-c51bd2d3ddc823140f | p.44] carbon pools and ﬂuxes are produced in a consistent manner. 1308
[pdf-4d6d424bfc8b7aa290 | p.44] 3.3.4. C-phyto priority 3: Vertical structure 1309
[pdf-bf321a14df348698e0 | p.44] Challenges: Considering the diﬃculties in measuring C-phyto in situ (see 1310
[pdf-464b3673df59bbc659 | p.44] C-phyto priority 1) is it very challenging to collect, aggregate and produce an 1311
[pdf-33c5b754f0ae76e594 | p.44] in-situ dataset that is representative of entire euphotic depth and beyond at global 1312
[pdf-85314399a0d2dfcc49 | p.44] scale, required for understanding distributions in C-phyto. 1313
[pdf-7c44856de6ff226f55 | p.44] 44

## p.45 — Page 45

[pdf-fe560fba6e4b8e87a9 | p.45] Gaps: Since current satellite ocean colour techniques are limited to passive 1314
[pdf-54cc6b2e9d3d69cd3f | p.45] radiometry which only delivers information from the ﬁrst optical depth, the 1315
[pdf-bb1299d2c52e2fdd6d | p.45] collection of in-situ C-phyto data for validation of satellite products has been 1316
[pdf-c5122c78e3062085bc | p.45] largely limited to discrete water sampling at surface depths. For a complete 1317
[pdf-aa8d5f15a33c8209ac | p.45] understanding of the role of C-phyto in the ocean carbon cycle, it is imperative that 1318
[pdf-6ca2eabb7bb829bf15 | p.45] we extend measurements deeper into the water column, encompassing the entire 1319
[pdf-42f0a2ceaa63ec9dff | p.45] euphotic zone. Parametrisations have been developed to extrapolate the satellite 1320
[pdf-ca7fc8b75040548ede | p.45] ocean colour ﬁelds on the ﬁrst optical depth to derive the chl-a concentration 1321
[pdf-42719764d8f7a0d099 | p.45] (Morel and Berthon, 1989) or the contribution of phytoplankton size classes (Uitz 1322
[pdf-84da5bbcf30216f68c | p.45] et al., 2006) for the entire euphotic depth. Similarly, approximations based on in 1323
[pdf-adf3e9a5851b9b6de6 | p.45] situ data sampling of the vertical proﬁle of phytoplankton carbon are needed. 1324
[pdf-05bddb629c6ac3ab07 | p.45] Opportunities: There are potential opportunities to use autonomous plat- 1325
[pdf-4585c4c0e94e7db363 | p.45] forms such as BGC-Argo ﬂoats (Claustre et al., 2020), undulating proﬁlers 1326
[pdf-3b045f660888afcb7e | p.45] (Bracher et al., 2020) and moorings (Von Appen et al., 2021), together with 1327
[pdf-126b3810826175fdf7 | p.45] satellite passive (ocean colour) and active (lidar) remote-sensing and modelling 1328
[pdf-a4c53cfe13f6d97943 | p.45] (e.g. through data assimilation), to help reconstruct, via techniques like artiﬁcial 1329
[pdf-cd114ee807e6261aeb | p.45] intelligence, the 4D view of C-phyto, to better observe phytoplankton biomass 1330
[pdf-cfb3604ebfe38c6ad3 | p.45] dynamics below the ocean surface (e.g., Brewin et al., 2022). Quantum computing 1331
[pdf-bbad581e43746be2aa | p.45] may help in this regard. 1332
[pdf-41e09d6ac1bc4a871f | p.45] 3.4. Dissolved Organic Carbon (DOC) 1333
[pdf-4c68b9c8b3003946ae | p.45] DOC is ubiquitous in the ocean and represents a considerable reservoir of 1334
[pdf-0d7e56604177a6be47 | p.45] carbon, at around 662 Gt C, approximately the size of the atmospheric CO2 pool 1335
[pdf-06877b6a14e922722c | p.45] (Hansell et al., 2009). Marine DOC is also a dynamic carbon component, that ful- 1336
[pdf-cfe7098001b4e778df | p.45] ﬁlls important biogeochemical and ecological functions, and connects terrestrial 1337
[pdf-1c449a30409f278889 | p.45] landscapes (Anderson et al., 2019), freshwater and marine ecosystems and the 1338
[pdf-ef70e75a0c1b4bb8ac | p.45] atmosphere (Carlson and Hansell, 2015; Anderson et al., 2019). Continuously 1339
[pdf-72d8ee4133414a2f9c | p.45] and accurately quantifying DOC stocks and ﬂuxes in the ocean is critical to our 1340
[pdf-e0ef75f9b384cfac35 | p.45] understanding of the global role of DOC and its susceptibility to change. 1341
[pdf-7b2a8a67b0a9ee48f5 | p.45] 45

## p.46 — Page 46

[pdf-9fb295fea1c820600e | p.46] 3.4.1. State of the art in DOC 1342
[pdf-0806d98a6fb39b22a9 | p.46] In recent years, synoptic monitoring of DOC has been attempted using optical 1343
[pdf-62bdefdd5ec2159f39 | p.46] techniques and Earth Observation. A wide range of methods have been trailed, 1344
[pdf-b7813db07a22c24aaa | p.46] mainly empirical, including linear regressions, artiﬁcial neural network algorithm, 1345
[pdf-af16518e25c8b9246d | p.46] random forest classiﬁcation, and gradient boosting. These approaches typically 1346
[pdf-0d6918731f2d0f4da2 | p.46] estimate DOC concentration using single or multiple variables, including: remote- 1347
[pdf-5c8c52850eed9baf82 | p.46] sensing reﬂectance, remotely-sensed CDOM absorption coeﬃcients, sea-surface 1348
[pdf-ebbffd6267de67cc02 | p.46] salinity, SST, chlorophyll-a concentration, and modelled mixed layer depths. For 1349
[pdf-5e6457818dea64d5df | p.46] an in-depth review of the status of DOC monitoring, the reader is referred Section 1350
[pdf-ff72c3da5a639b249f | p.46] 4.1.2. of Brewin et al. (2021) and the recent review of Fichot (Under Review). 1351
[pdf-9102a3eccaa446045a | p.46] Four key priorities were identiﬁed following presentations and discussions at 1352
[pdf-92fc87a15be90a2807 | p.46] the workshop. These are summarised in Table 6 and include: 1) temporal coverage 1353
[pdf-d4cd79f9f2f482163b | p.46] of the coastal ocean; 2) understanding the relationship between CDOM and DOC; 1354
[pdf-f423c0e2f044e34b76 | p.46] 3) identiﬁcation of sources and reactivity; and 4) vertical measurements. 1355
[pdf-6b2d094f97157089b0 | p.46] 3.4.2. DOC priority 1: Spatial and temporal coverage of the coastal ocean 1356
[pdf-bc2948fd54b3f87d8d | p.46] Challenges: The remote sensing of DOC in the surface ocean is facilitated 1357
[pdf-c2df89137522343815 | p.46] by the optical detection of CDOM (the coloured component of dissolved matter), 1358
[pdf-bd8058fdcd1421d7a7 | p.46] particularly in the coastal ocean, where DOC and CDOM can be tightly correlated 1359
[pdf-333672b7be08942c47 | p.46] (Ferrari et al., 1996; Vodacek et al., 1997; Bowers et al., 2004; Fichot and Benner, 1360
[pdf-9a25b2c28caee714a6 | p.46] 2012; Tehrani et al., 2013). In such cases, the detection of DOC from space relies 1361
[pdf-d18a723ca39a8c69f5 | p.46] on the optical detection of CDOM absorption coeﬃcients, ag(λ), from remote- 1362
[pdf-0ce69b63a6274ef924 | p.46] sensing reﬂectance, followed by the estimation of DOC from ag(λ). However, as 1363
[pdf-083b90bd7386ebbc68 | p.46] coastal regions are highly dynamic and heterogenous, quantifying DOC stocks and 1364
[pdf-5678c6845822e1f8ac | p.46] ﬂuxes require satellite optical monitoring systems with high temporal and spatial 1365
[pdf-e4953fb678d74f2a79 | p.46] coverage, and accurate atmospheric correction (e.g., separating the contribution of 1366
[pdf-1e119c2cb91498627e | p.46] Rayleigh scattering in the atmosphere is particularly important for DOC retrievals; 1367
[pdf-d1c50ebe3ae3c2aa40 | p.46] Juhls et al., 2019), both of which are challenging. High latitudes, where high 1368
[pdf-b7f678429c0b87da21 | p.46] loads of DOC are transported from rivers into the sea (e.g., Arctic rivers, Baltic) 1369
[pdf-9ccdccb12d9ebd25d3 | p.46] are diﬃcult to view using passive ocean colour satellites in winter months. 1370
[pdf-a256d648082c124c11 | p.46] Gaps: At present, accurate estimates of DOC stocks and ﬂuxes in coastal 1371
[pdf-830f8a26f4fdaa75fa | p.46] 46

## p.47 — Page 47

[pdf-c5bf4e842728c4b969 | p.47] environments are severely limited by the temporal coverage of existing ocean- 1372
[pdf-e0588c9bc3ef74863f | p.47] color satellites. Current satellites oﬀer revisit times of about ﬁve times per week, 1373
[pdf-03ea30dc31d18a0424 | p.47] at best (though this depends on latitude and time of year). More appropriate 1374
[pdf-4dc425d07a40766bc5 | p.47] revisit times for nearshore coastal waters would need to be an order of magnitude 1375
[pdf-d12d9849225a66253b | p.47] higher (e.g., ideally 3-5 times per day) to adequately capture the dynamics of 1376
[pdf-86bd1ab18bdc9d1510 | p.47] DOC and facilitate the accurate estimation of DOC ﬂuxes across the boundaries 1377
[pdf-bbba1da45546e1fe0a | p.47] of coastal systems. This is especially important for the nearshore regions of the 1378
[pdf-20e51f70dd18ce0b59 | p.47] coastal ocean which can be strongly inﬂuenced by tides, currents, and rivers. 1379
[pdf-dfbf01e89810550389 | p.47] Opportunities: With the advent of geostationary ocean-colour satellites, such 1380
[pdf-0c3520911b90a0b1b1 | p.47] as GOCI and the upcoming hyperspectral NASA GLIMR, capable of imaging 1381
[pdf-489cff3639dee39ba6 | p.47] multiple times daily, there are exciting opportunities to address these challenges 1382
[pdf-437660829ce1b539ce | p.47] and gaps at regional scales (e.g., see Huang et al., 2017). NASA’s GLIMR 1383
[pdf-d9d5581e4a57add84b | p.47] (launch expected in 2027) will help quantify DOC stocks and ﬂuxes in coastal 1384
[pdf-25a14b8c1ab49cddef | p.47] environments of the continental USA and in targeted regions of coastal South 1385
[pdf-5ca92e7986ee294a2f | p.47] America (e.g., Amazon River outﬂow, Orinoco River Outﬂow) by providing 1386
[pdf-87ca27c134063ef289 | p.47] multiple observations per day (hourly), at around 300 m resolution. Reﬂectances 1387
[pdf-fc11c77389e40c2bd2 | p.47] from GLIMR will also be hyperspectral (10 nm resolution) across the UV-NIR 1388
[pdf-8f1cc9c753570bde16 | p.47] range (340 -1040 nm) and will therefore provide the opportunity for improved 1389
[pdf-68b25e0f102af8af8d | p.47] accuracy of DOC concentration retrievals. We recommend continuing eﬀorts 1390
[pdf-a38790c6eb83a06fa8 | p.47] towards deploying additional geostationary and hyperspectral satellites to improve 1391
[pdf-f3b9a1a0331c2c7d40 | p.47] the lack of good temporal coverage in other coastal regions around the world. 1392
[pdf-0c96604b0e3d05a4d3 | p.47] High spatial resolution satellites (such as Sentinel-3 and Sentinel-2/Landsat), and 1393
[pdf-95289c5e0edad3ece4 | p.47] potential future constellations of Cubesats (e.g., SeaHawk/HawkEye; Jeﬀrey et al., 1394
[pdf-4bd6e0a919d5d9e63e | p.47] 2018), may also help in this regard. 1395
[pdf-ccf23bb6b0978ebae8 | p.47] 3.4.3. DOC priority 2: Understanding and constraining the relationship between 1396
[pdf-c9c1205cd9d7198a56 | p.47] CDOM and DOC 1397
[pdf-855a67348b00f3d9ed | p.47] Challenges: Improvements in satellite CDOM absorption retrievals are 1398
[pdf-566ad35f9b3ccc9593 | p.47] needed, with uncertainties in algorithms often higher than other IOPs derived 1399
[pdf-bbacaa253e98ba2789 | p.47] from ocean colour data (Brewin et al., 2015b). The relationships between DOC 1400
[pdf-41de19ecd3915db70a | p.47] and CDOM absorption, commonly used to quantify stocks of DOC in coastal 1401
[pdf-83bdf80b5b26f9a8b3 | p.47] regions, tends to be variable seasonally and across coastal systems (Mannino 1402
[pdf-db15a919f1317609cf | p.47] 47

## p.48 — Page 48

[pdf-bd22fb84dc7d78ad5a | p.48] et al., 2008; Massicotte et al., 2017; Cao et al., 2018). Furthermore, the dynamics 1403
[pdf-d14d61a5e9633149ab | p.48] of CDOM and DOC are largely decoupled in the open ocean (Nelson and Siegel, 1404
[pdf-3ee5c40ec4aefa6218 | p.48] 2013), making the accurate remote sensing of DOC concentration challenging in 1405
[pdf-eebd21b3734f704792 | p.48] much of the open ocean. 1406
[pdf-e65a7cd848b7c82083 | p.48] Gaps: There are gaps in our understanding of the relationship between DOC 1407
[pdf-1017fdfe1f9eb9bf39 | p.48] and CDOM absorption coeﬃcients that need to be addressed, for example, rela- 1408
[pdf-ca0a8ee7c447df6ad7 | p.48] tionships are likely to depend on the type of river system studied, and its optical 1409
[pdf-5ff829f58ff294e506 | p.48] constituents. There are also gaps in our understanding of the various physical 1410
[pdf-3b3ce25709070b583a | p.48] and biogeochemical processes that impact diﬀerently CDOM absorption and 1411
[pdf-7c2060500f9bcc982e | p.48] DOC, depending on DOC quality (e.g., Miller and Moran, 1997; Tzortziou et al., 1412
[pdf-01848e6c244eb7a806 | p.48] 2007; Helms et al., 2008). This will improve our understanding of regional and 1413
[pdf-924cb3daaefcb46424 | p.48] seasonal variability in the relationship among these variables, and consequently 1414
[pdf-134ad4efeac1bb1841 | p.48] improve DOC estimates from space. Additionally, there is a lack satellite UV and 1415
[pdf-e5f326a808409091a8 | p.48] hyperspectral data for resolving DOC and its composition. 1416
[pdf-b825b5e7d64f814409 | p.48] Opportunities: We recommend the community work towards improving this 1417
[pdf-9f6c586665b0d37653 | p.48] understanding through a combination of the following four eﬀorts. 1418
[pdf-246e81ab50f9132d52 | p.48] • Utilise the spectral slope of CDOM absorption, S 275−295, to constrain the 1419
[pdf-a8785fb4249d5cd935 | p.48] variability between CDOM and DOC in the ocean and improve empirical 1420
[pdf-8befb93a72b7847cf2 | p.48] algorithms. In river-inﬂuenced coastal systems, S275−295 has been shown 1421
[pdf-090a2187a93162ec32 | p.48] to be a useful parameter to constrain the variability between CDOM and 1422
[pdf-697952c966b187cf0c | p.48] DOC (Fichot and Benner, 2011; Cao et al., 2018). It has also been shown 1423
[pdf-ccc34fdf6c3489cf32 | p.48] that this parameter can be retrieved empirically with reasonable accuracy 1424
[pdf-4e73d33d5999b26680 | p.48] from ocean colour, therefore providing a means to improve DOC retrievals 1425
[pdf-ee27dd935c7619c6c5 | p.48] (Mannino et al., 2008; Fichot et al., 2013, 2014; Cao et al., 2018). Future 1426
[pdf-cea87e5b104fbfa51f | p.48] studies could look into developing similar approaches for other regions 1427
[pdf-c42df80842382b6927 | p.48] of the ocean. Retrievals of S275−295 requires very accurate atmospheric 1428
[pdf-c40c2e508c2f67339f | p.48] correction, which is challenging in coastal waters. 1429
[pdf-7b31ae595a09c388ae | p.48] • Develop mechanistic models of the processes regulating the relationship 1430
[pdf-1c03cd51281c4bb173 | p.48] between CDOM and DOC, by integrating new insight on the eﬀects of pho- 1431
[pdf-169e91f0acb9990cae | p.48] tobleaching. Recent eﬀorts have quantiﬁed and included in biogeochemical 1432
[pdf-16c0d51fe021f5f274 | p.48] 48

## p.49 — Page 49

[pdf-532ee516b1dd15e37d | p.49] models (e.g., Clark et al., 2019) the eﬀects of photobleaching on CDOM 1433
[pdf-6f3ac14b39c53c43e1 | p.49] absorption coeﬃcient spectra, which in turn, may improve our ability to 1434
[pdf-ecc26ccffbed091437 | p.49] constrain the relationship between CDOM and DOC (Swan et al., 2013; 1435
[pdf-7b1b4e105bee74e195 | p.49] Zhu et al., 2020). Similar eﬀorts should be conducted for understanding 1436
[pdf-9059ca846566293d5f | p.49] other processes such as the marine biological net production of DOC. A 1437
[pdf-86f76a7481a3eed712 | p.49] quantitative appreciation of these processes is also critical to understand 1438
[pdf-b637eaf2a8cc1eb480 | p.49] the inﬂuence of climate-driven change on the relationship between CDOM 1439
[pdf-96499e6d5c87f0c89b | p.49] and DOC. 1440
[pdf-3190e556717eeaf01f | p.49] • Harness opportunities to acquire high-quality ﬁeld measurements of DOC 1441
[pdf-d710b2a427a08718cc | p.49] and CDOM absorption across diﬀerent seasons and marine environments. 1442
[pdf-4586587b6ab2e375f3 | p.49] This could be achieved by tapping into ﬁeld campaigns that collect IOPs 1443
[pdf-d61868ce3ec2e8f6be | p.49] and apparent optical properties (AOPs) for satellite validation, and perform 1444
[pdf-1f624ec91bb350321d | p.49] additional concurrent sampling for DOC. Many ﬁeld datasets include mea- 1445
[pdf-77c8d6a4148b289719 | p.49] surements of CDOM absorption coeﬃcients but lack DOC measurements. 1446
[pdf-aa31a2d190ee819e6b | p.49] It should be noted, however, that while many labs have the capability to 1447
[pdf-30566c7d8c81f5feb3 | p.49] measure CDOM, much fewer labs can measure DOC. Coordinated eﬀorts 1448
[pdf-cf2c2c3b5d7d99d9e6 | p.49] should therefore be considered to ensure that CDOM and DOC are mea- 1449
[pdf-73a34ec63f435b0361 | p.49] sured together as often as possible. This could be aided by the development 1450
[pdf-fbd3ecf7ceefdca357 | p.49] of semi-automative methods to measure DOC, that could be used alongside 1451
[pdf-5e5ad08f129d5a9e4f | p.49] similar techniques for measuring CDOM absorption (e.g., Dall’Olmo et al., 1452
[pdf-f9d543402ca9220b37 | p.49] 2017), which could facilitate the development of improved satellite DOC 1453
[pdf-0b02438c8edde061d5 | p.49] algorithms. 1454
[pdf-f7ceaeabdcf15bc5e8 | p.49] • Harnessing new satellite sensors for CDOM and DOC retrievals. For exam- 1455
[pdf-4977d04189ecda119f | p.49] ple, consideration in the allocation and characteristics of spectral wavebands 1456
[pdf-46a1d368f5a5804205 | p.49] for DOC studies has also gone into the development of NASA’s PACE mis- 1457
[pdf-7bcc3e1066ff0ccd52 | p.49] sion (Werdell et al., 2019). Harnessing optical water type frameworks for 1458
[pdf-97d5693dcc91c607ae | p.49] algorithm selection, may also lead to better separation of NAP-CDOM 1459
[pdf-aaf620964f198c30d2 | p.49] absorption. Within the ESA project Sentinel-5-P for Ocean Colour Prod- 1460
[pdf-a89beb9bcb2dff8b21 | p.49] ucts (S5POC), Kd at three wavelengths (UV-AB, UV-A and short blue) 1461
[pdf-8a1424e771a353ef11 | p.49] were developed (Oelker et al., 2022), which could help provide insight on 1462
[pdf-f022c3dc0708bf13b7 | p.49] 49

## p.50 — Page 50

[pdf-859a031a09a428f274 | p.50] the sources of CDOM. Additionally, there is potential to exploit the high 1463
[pdf-cc4cfcfebc1e94f408 | p.50] spectral resolution of TROPOMI (e.g., the ﬁlling of the Fraunhofer lines by 1464
