[pdf-8ce185db3fd96e8ba4 | p.50] 3.4.4. DOC priority 3: Identiﬁcation of source and reactivity 1467
[pdf-e12b470be32c1959ac | p.50] Challenges: To quantify the cycling, fate, and impacts of DOC in the ocean, 1468
[pdf-28fc3a1fa4499c0bae | p.50] requires identifying speciﬁc pools of DOC of diﬀerent sources and reactivity. 1469
[pdf-cb6a8add0522dfb7a1 | p.50] This is particularly true for the coastal ocean. There is likely to be large gradients 1470
[pdf-b124478c5d0b9854a7 | p.50] in the sources and reactivity of DOC as we transition from inland waters to coasts 1471
[pdf-943defbb090e2362eb | p.50] and the open ocean. 1472
[pdf-3521e36d02f6ed4392 | p.50] Gaps: Although ﬂuorescence excitation-emission matrix methods have been 1473
[pdf-2630318180af1f0fb1 | p.50] used as an in-situ optical indicator of dissolved organic matter (DOM) origin and 1474
[pdf-4a0669d28da838622a | p.50] reactivity (Mopper and Schultz, 1993; Kowalczuk et al., 2013), there has been 1475
[pdf-699cc55f930d3854c7 | p.50] few studies assessing whether the DOM ﬂuoresced signal can be detected from 1476
[pdf-0da4b22cd4c4473a31 | p.50] remote-sensing reﬂectance. 1477
[pdf-c094ff1f24e6a1a7b4 | p.50] Opportunities: We recommend the community puts eﬀorts towards assess- 1478
[pdf-a7b3a79be7bf332927 | p.50] ing whether the ﬂuorescence of DOC and CDOM, originating from speciﬁc 1479
[pdf-af14eb928cfa52ef4e | p.50] sources (e.g., riverine, eﬄuent), can have a measurable inﬂuence on remote- 1480
[pdf-339a5461037b7c6029 | p.50] sensing reﬂectance. Recent and upcoming hyperspectral sensors (e.g., TROPOMI, 1481
[pdf-a3c2fb5a68e35601ce | p.50] GLIMR, PRISMA, PACE, see Table 2) have (or will have) improved signal-to- 1482
[pdf-009d1173e48994e514 | p.50] noise ratio, as well as enhanced spectral information in the UV-visible range, 1483
[pdf-d8890054b9c7884d8e | p.50] and adequate spatial resolution, that could facilitate detection of the ﬂuorescence 1484
[pdf-9ad8e9d88fc9b62326 | p.50] signature of certain pools of DOC and CDOM (Wolanin et al., 2015; Oelker et al., 1485
[pdf-3f11f2a4ab1dc5d9ab | p.50] 2022; Harringmeyer et al., 2021). Such eﬀorts can be facilitated with radiative 1486
[pdf-51ef5105a5da7dcd0c | p.50] transfer simulations (e.g., Hydrolight, www.hydrolight.info, and SCIATRAN, 1487
[pdf-3113b07fa4ef16cf47 | p.50] https://www.iup.uni-bremen.de/sciatran/). However, ﬂuorescence signature of 1488
[pdf-648d7cb707c510337d | p.50] DOC is currently not well understood, and we require a better quantitative knowl- 1489
[pdf-68c11f4ec15f4d2130 | p.50] edge of the ﬂuorescence quantum yield matrix of DOC and CDOM and how it 1490
[pdf-18029939e4453fcf8d | p.50] varies with speciﬁc DOM sources (Wünsch et al., 2015). 1491
[pdf-10923fd43f8e9106ca | p.50] Active remote-sensing approaches based on laser-induced ﬂuorescence could 1492
[pdf-3dbf12ec71a462ac9c | p.50] also potentially facilitate the sourcing of DOM in the surface ocean. Airborne 1493
[pdf-a8249cac50222a72a8 | p.50] 50

## p.51 — Page 51

[pdf-ca7de3eb0a04abf973 | p.51] laser-based measurements of DOM have been used in the past, but these only used 1494
[pdf-3a1b3fb39255900d31 | p.51] a single excitation-emission wavelength pair and were used to speciﬁcally measure 1495
[pdf-7380a4ad97a4531f11 | p.51] DOC (Hoge et al., 1993; Vodacek, 1989). The use of multiple, carefully chosen 1496
[pdf-ffb2eeacd1622249d7 | p.51] excitation-emission wavelength combinations could potentially help identify 1497
[pdf-3aafda1befe62c8c02 | p.51] speciﬁc pools of DOM with unique ﬂuorescence signatures. 1498
[pdf-c92c8d473c3f2d2b2b | p.51] 3.4.5. DOC priority 4: Vertical measurements 1499
[pdf-8b4cf9b42f988272fd | p.51] Challenges: The remote sensing of CDOM and DOC is limited to surface 1500
[pdf-3f6724d88ea83346ad | p.51] measurements. Accurately extrapolating these measurements to depth requires 1501
[pdf-fbfee44361278e805a | p.51] understanding of vertical variability. At present, depth variability is generally 1502
[pdf-1116fcb6166c1b4d3c | p.51] assumed or estimated using empirical or statistical approaches (e.g., neural net- 1503
[pdf-e4d563eb91314589c2 | p.51] works) trained with ﬁeld observations (Mannino et al., 2016). 1504
[pdf-6a20adc73b782ed95d | p.51] Gaps: Approaches that extrapolate surface DOC and CDOM to depth require 1505
[pdf-f1b9b8c73b218a7366 | p.51] extensive in-situ datasets (vertical proﬁles) of DOC and CDOM, representative of 1506
[pdf-885b26312046c4f5d9 | p.51] a wide range of conditions. Though eﬀorts have been made in this regard (Nelson 1507
[pdf-642a83ab99800c5571 | p.51] and Siegel, 2013; Hansell, 2013), gaps exist for many regions and seasons. 1508
[pdf-9c57dec5cba396f77c | p.51] Opportunities: In-situ measurements from autonomous platforms like BGC- 1509
[pdf-86ca11bf470dcee645 | p.51] Argo equipped with DOM-ﬂuorescence sensors can provide valuable informa- 1510
[pdf-3482f0a8e1c3cbd69b | p.51] tion about the depth-dependency of DOM in the ocean (Claustre et al., 2020). 1511
[pdf-66a4399f26caac0a83 | p.51] BGC-Argo radiometric measurements in the UV can also be used to get CDOM 1512
[pdf-7920d5000405bd5ef7 | p.51] absorption proxies (Organelli et al., 2017; Organelli and Claustre, 2019). Re- 1513
[pdf-f3e18b9a7ae3b3612b | p.51] cently, projects such as AEOLUS COLOR (CDOM-proxy retrieval from aeOLus 1514
[pdf-656e2f087957fbeeec | p.51] ObseRvations), have focused on developing UV-lidar-based techniques to retrieve 1515
[pdf-a61266d85371bca305 | p.51] sub-surface information about CDOM in the ocean (Dionisi et al., 2021). The 1516
[pdf-a9f68ebb20988e4b6c | p.51] ESA AEOLUS mission is a UV-lidar (355 nm) mission originally designed for the 1517
[pdf-aaacc25966e9054fd3 | p.51] retrieval of atmospheric properties, but the UV capabilities of this active sensor 1518
[pdf-9ec62569d99dc9d0a8 | p.51] provides an opportunity to retrieve in-water properties of CDOM. We recommend 1519
[pdf-ac783c2338777a762c | p.51] that the community continue to explore original ideas to improve the detection 1520
[pdf-e70b2c1661df5aa211 | p.51] of CDOM and DOC below the surface. There are also opportunities to harness 1521
[pdf-09f24b40ad2f132430 | p.51] mechanistic modelling approaches (physical and biogeochemical modelling) to 1522
[pdf-00b6bc0a17f8047748 | p.51] improve estimation of DOC dynamics at depth (Mannino et al., 2016). 1523
[pdf-2502f844653441770a | p.51] 51

## p.52 — Page 52

[pdf-5ea9b5a15f7e7c3ef6 | p.52] 3.5. Inorganic carbon and ﬂuxes at the ocean interface (IC) 1524
[pdf-4791c6bb133b4c2e82 | p.52] Unlike organic carbon, consisting primarily of organic compounds such as 1525
[pdf-f442ec7e35b3a613d2 | p.52] lipids, proteins and nucleic acids, inorganic carbon consists of simple compounds 1526
[pdf-b32f279cb7f3539dc5 | p.52] such as carbon dioxide, bicarbonate, carbonate and carbonic acid. Inorganic 1527
[pdf-9865969158878ff2e0 | p.52] carbon in the ocean can be partitioned into dissolved (DIC) and particulate 1528
[pdf-78b75c83c9437dd10c | p.52] (PIC) form. Although these two could be treated separately in a review of this 1529
[pdf-07a68bec4061566f97 | p.52] nature, they are intimately linked, considering DIC can be transferred to PIC 1530
[pdf-ec41d22cdb723101c2 | p.52] through biological (e.g., planktonic ﬁxation and osmoregulation) or abiotic (e.g., 1531
[pdf-18e0527c15e5e17b55 | p.52] aragonite) formation of calcium carbonate (CaCO3), and PIC to DIC through 1532
[pdf-06f7117cee8c06780e | p.52] the dissolution of CaCO3. These processes impact the CO2 concentration of the 1533
[pdf-922f79c116e4e35360 | p.52] water, its alkalinity and pH. 1534
[pdf-7ae9c743688adc6b20 | p.52] Relative to DIC, PIC is a small pool of carbon at around 0.03 Gt C (Hopkins 1535
[pdf-fd6e6e483971c1fc81 | p.52] et al., 2019), but annual production is considered highly variable and estimated 1536
[pdf-2cbb8323247f4d013f | p.52] to be of the order 0.8-1.4 Gt C y−1 (Feely et al., 2004). PIC is present in the 1537
[pdf-4b398c1d043722e992 | p.52] form of particulate CaCO3, with coccolithophores, pteropods, foraminifera and 1538
[pdf-2987f7ebd4e22c24fa | p.52] PIC-containing sediments, thought to be the main sources of PIC in the ocean 1539
[pdf-af82b181357697ab96 | p.52] (Schiebel, 2002; Feely et al., 2004; Buitenhuis et al., 2019). Despite its biological 1540
[pdf-ac9deceb7317086236 | p.52] growth the formation of PIC has the net-eﬀect of shifting the carbonate chemistry 1541
[pdf-a7e9ff7ff06a40261d | p.52] towards higher CO2 in the water and decreasing its pH (Zeebe and Wolf-Gladrow, 1542
[pdf-21b8a35f9b85d86b13 | p.52] 2001; Rost and Riebesell, 2004; Zeebe, 2012). The reader is referred to the recent 1543
[pdf-249d8218c3a2a95d78 | p.52] review of Neukermans et al. (2023), for a more detailed description of our current 1544
[pdf-4fef42cdb5f33d707e | p.52] understanding of the inﬂuence of PIC production on carbon cycling. 1545
[pdf-1985595fd7e27953c2 | p.52] In contrast, DIC constitutes the largest pool of carbon in the ocean, at around 1546
[pdf-258bf193147990bdfc | p.52] 38,000 Gt C (Hedges, 1992), and connects carbon in the ocean with the atmo- 1547
[pdf-a6d05f321546140878 | p.52] sphere and with the land. CO2 dissolves in seawater and reacts with water to 1548
[pdf-9d91f96972bd5c7fd8 | p.52] form carbonic acid (H2CO3). Carbonic acid is unstable and dissociates into bi- 1549
[pdf-ae4bbb9eefc6a11f2d | p.52] carbonate (HCO− 3), carbonate (CO2− 3 ) and protons (H+). The equilibrium among 1550
[pdf-f7799d61a7856fe265 | p.52] these forms controls ocean pH. From a biological viewpoint the gaseous quantity 1551
[pdf-f07358ba28db7a55f8 | p.52] of CO2 in seawater, pCO2, is modulated by photosynthesis (PP) and respiration 1552
[pdf-1b906cdf5ccbd3af13 | p.52] (mineralization) which is captured within net community production estimates. 1553
[pdf-353f220bb72ebbcdf2 | p.52] The ﬂux or movement of CO2 between ocean and atmosphere is often de- 1554
[pdf-b5bd55188a1f76996c | p.52] 52

## p.53 — Page 53

[pdf-32ed2a3ae7f04d0dcb | p.53] scribed using a formation ﬁrst described by Liss and Slater (1974), which can be 1555
[pdf-190e17bb8e45afb619 | p.53] expressed as Flux= kK0(pCO2,w −pCO2,a) (Wanninkhof, 2014); where k is the 1556
[pdf-1f88f44c4c02e65d55 | p.53] gas transfer velocity (equivalent to the inverse of the resistance to gas transfer), K0 1557
[pdf-c3d0418393ac53ea4a | p.53] is the constant of solubility of gas, and (pCO2,w−pCO2,a) is the diﬀerence between 1558
[pdf-95515d2763ba5cb8e8 | p.53] the CO2 partial pressures in the ocean and the atmosphere (∆pCO2), respectively 1559
[pdf-bf2356e5afbdd8f6fd | p.53] (see Woolf et al., 2016, for discussion on how best to derive ∆pCO2). Ocean 1560
[pdf-9ab2e443f47b14b3cd | p.53] temperature, and to a less extent salinity, is a strong modulator of the solubility of 1561
[pdf-3957017e54d62e9ab9 | p.53] CO2 in seawater (Takahashi et al., 2009) and is thus an important parameter for 1562
[pdf-87602ef2a5c316c466 | p.53] inﬂuencing oceanic pCO2 variability. k is often parameterised as a function of 1563
[pdf-4289e4b0270b4f29bf | p.53] wind speed and temperature (e.g., Schmidt number; Wanninkhof, 2014). 1564
[pdf-5d995d495719a2b665 | p.53] 3.5.1. State of the art in inorganic carbon and air-sea ﬂuxes 1565
[pdf-6d18fceb9868e5c013 | p.53] Methods to remotely sense PIC have focused on individual or multi-spectral 1566
[pdf-6fb717cc1c2eb4a28c | p.53] band optical detection of coccolithophores (Gordon et al., 2001; Balch et al., 2005; 1567
[pdf-0c5c573994f73f9241 | p.53] Mitchell et al., 2017), with some using time series to improve data consistency 1568
[pdf-322d7977c17a660ab6 | p.53] (Shutler et al., 2010). Due to their unique optical signature (when the plankton 1569
[pdf-8e2a720f08f2f84554 | p.53] dies coccoliths are detached causing the water to appear spectrally white), coccol- 1570
[pdf-8a93bc69daddab8abc | p.53] ithophore blooms have been mapped via satellite ocean colour since the launch of 1571
[pdf-c54ec10d790ad95620 | p.53] NASA’s CZCS satellite sensor (Holligan et al., 1983; Brown and Yoder, 1994) and 1572
[pdf-547b6da02751be9dd3 | p.53] the Advanced Very High Resolution Radiometer (AVHRR) in 1978 (Groom and 1573
[pdf-043374f15ea1191bfb | p.53] Holligan, 1987; Smyth et al., 2004; Loveday and Smyth, 2018). The challenges of 1574
[pdf-5cf912e44ebb94bd1f | p.53] detection include: detecting coccolithophores and their associated PIC at low con- 1575
[pdf-e6b9836ab44218a1a2 | p.53] centrations (or prior to their coccoliths becoming detached), during bloom events, 1576
[pdf-ccb7b90bb1911ca1d4 | p.53] in the presence of bubbles (e.g., in the Southern Ocean; Randolph et al., 2014), 1577
[pdf-1d895e457b39e5813d | p.53] and to remove the eﬀects of suspended particulates that exhibit similar spectral 1578
[pdf-d135a63decce01567f | p.53] properties in shelf seas (Shutler et al., 2010). Laboratory and ﬁeld observations 1579
[pdf-c4de943d124593c024 | p.53] (Voss et al., 1998; Balch et al., 1999, 1996; Smyth et al., 2002) have informed 1580
[pdf-4cc40306919fd5db26 | p.53] PIC algorithm development for determining calcite concentrations by relating 1581
[pdf-3623c95935f7ad61e4 | p.53] coccolithophore abundance and morphology to PIC concentrations. Currently 1582
[pdf-461854b9a78db3d87e | p.53] NASA Ocean Biology Distributed Active Archive Centre (DAAC) distributes 1583
[pdf-1e54e8e5f944dcc735 | p.53] a PIC concentration product that merges Balch et al. (2005) and Gordon et al. 1584
[pdf-bd2a83960b1288f45c | p.53] (2001), and there is also a developmental PIC product available (Mitchell et al., 1585
[pdf-048da87f07c4f35c23 | p.53] 53

## p.54 — Page 54

[pdf-3d2b0a1dc2f3e8d5e0 | p.54] 2017). 1586
[pdf-b877d8795bdbee29a8 | p.54] DIC and other key carbonate system variables (e.g., total alkalinity (TA), 1587
[pdf-abe0d1ae33935b8a68 | p.54] pH, and pCO2) are more challenging to determine from satellite observations 1588
[pdf-d6a4115e90874ef8de | p.54] as they do not have a unique spectral signature. However, alkalinity is strongly 1589
[pdf-033144776afac3ba27 | p.54] conservative with salinity so this has led to the development of many regional 1590
[pdf-fe0475eeb92fa82d95 | p.54] relationships to predict TA from salinity (e.g., Cai et al., 2010; Lefévre et al., 1591
[pdf-f0b48eab8e06258ab0 | p.54] 2010) and DIC from salinity and temperature (e.g. Lee et al., 2006), as well as 1592
[pdf-fcdd8874f9db216ac5 | p.54] global relationships using a suite of physical and chemical variables (e.g., Sasse 1593
[pdf-e6562d88e760de2234 | p.54] et al., 2013) and their application to satellite remote sensing has been identiﬁed 1594
[pdf-d4e590cdca558d2191 | p.54] (Land et al., 2015). For example, total alkalinity has been estimated using the 1595
[pdf-dfc4511633386c7cfe | p.54] strong relation with sea surface salinity (SSS) which in the last decade has been 1596
[pdf-f68696bfe0e355a529 | p.54] measured by diﬀerent satellites, such as ESA’s Soil Moisture and Ocean Salinity 1597
[pdf-010d6b803526ac4622 | p.54] satellite (SMOS; Reul et al., 2012), NASA/Comision Nacional de Actividades 1598
[pdf-d447c3d690b1cda719 | p.54] Espaciales (CONAE) Aquarius (Lagerloef et al., 2013), and NASA’s Soil Moisture 1599
[pdf-52cb00821fd61e47cf | p.54] Active Passive satellite (SMAP; Tang et al., 2017). More recently, eﬀorts to 1600
[pdf-1d7c1587f87bb95ad7 | p.54] combine physical and optical satellite ocean observations with climatological 1601
[pdf-0239c2cdc0c1c9a934 | p.54] and re-analysis data products has opened the door to remote estimation of the 1602
[pdf-f7216155c39f2751ee | p.54] complete marine carbonate system via regional and global relationships as well as 1603
[pdf-5d1c4ed5bc3ced5ab1 | p.54] new machine learning methods and carbonate system calculation packages (e.g., 1604
[pdf-ecdaab39c985ac76bd | p.54] Land et al., 2019; Gregor and Gruber, 2021). 1605
[pdf-ce7ea094971acff491 | p.54] Large scale air/sea ﬂux estimates typically make use of the Surface Ocean 1606
[pdf-539a6aa4d1c60a2873 | p.54] CO2 ATlas (SOCAT, https://www.socat.info/index.php/data-access/; Bakker et al., 1607
[pdf-70b16b3e9f0e4d4890 | p.54] 2016) and/or global climatologies of surface seawater pCO2 using data interpo- 1608
[pdf-3f01d9f97a6267c7e7 | p.54] lation/extrapolation and neural network techniques (e.g., Takahashi et al., 2009; 1609
[pdf-18718c208f35a59453 | p.54] Rödenbeck et al., 2013; Landschützer et al., 2020) to produce spatially and tem- 1610
[pdf-856526cab1f8258b05 | p.54] porally complete ﬁelds. These pCO2 ﬁelds can be coupled with satellite retrievals 1611
[pdf-5f24c387c994bd03e2 | p.54] of SST, wind speed, and other variables, to calculate the air-sea CO2 ﬂux (e.g., as 1612
[pdf-925dadce4f51c61b6c | p.54] demonstrated with the FluxEngine toolbox; Shutler et al., 2016). A key parameter 1613
[pdf-70398af0e0c953ba4b | p.54] for the calculation of the air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes is the xCO2 fraction in air. Global cov- 1614
[pdf-f77b5b6317b8950307 | p.54] erage of atmospheric CO2 estimates is available from multiple satellite missions 1615
[pdf-74f739ef8dadf13fba | p.54] (e.g., Greenhouse gases Observing SATellite (GOSAT) 2009-present, Orbiting 1616
[pdf-8bb429b569ce974515 | p.54] 54

## p.55 — Page 55

[pdf-79ede3364d0f5592ce | p.55] Carbon Observatory-2 (OCO-2) 2014-present, and OCO-3 2019-present). Satel- 1617
[pdf-2dc5916c72b0bd62cd | p.55] lite observations have been combined with model output to estimate pCO2 and 1618
[pdf-a2ae7d3cd0efcfac24 | p.55] air-sea ﬂux (e.g., Arrigo et al., 2010) and estimates of pCO2 and air-sea ﬂux have 1619
[pdf-7b7678e192ca8cc7f5 | p.55] been achieved solely from satellite observations (e.g., Ono et al., 2004; Borges 1620
[pdf-ea15c363b2701f34d9 | p.55] et al., 2009; Lohrenz et al., 2018). It is also possible to calculate seawater pCO2 1621
[pdf-93186295a1ae6995b5 | p.55] from observations of TA and DIC and using marine carbonate system calculations 1622
[pdf-4387a6b493a2c8b09d | p.55] (e.g., Humphreys et al., 2022). For a more in-depth review of status of using 1623
[pdf-bdb07d749b5f6b159c | p.55] satellite remote sensing for determining inorganic carbon and ﬂuxes at the ocean 1624
[pdf-df3d14a6a45122917f | p.55] interface, the reader is referred to Shutler et al. (Submitted). 1625
[pdf-61f969929ed1d27a24 | p.55] Modelling studies can also help inform satellite approaches. They have been 1626
[pdf-87eea6d067de738f70 | p.55] used to evaluate the drivers of the marine carbonate system (e.g., Lauderdale 1627
[pdf-fa968f1e503486fc16 | p.55] et al., 2016) and examine potential impacts of extreme and compound events 1628
[pdf-ecd04add5aa97f8be7 | p.55] (e.g., Salisbury and Jönsson, 2018; Burger et al., 2020; Gruber et al., 2021). 1629
[pdf-31c46b4e3a39f8b0d0 | p.55] Seawater pCO2 and air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes can also be estimated using dynamic ocean 1630
[pdf-79342972ded8ce144c | p.55] biogeochemical models (Hauck et al., 2020) and data-assimilation-based models 1631
[pdf-8c57a09f468fd804e9 | p.55] (e.g., Verdy and Mazloﬀ, 2017). Estimating the Circulation and Climate of the 1632
[pdf-ea8cab98675b5a3694 | p.55] Ocean Darwin model (ECCO-Darwin) (Carroll et al., 2020, 2022) is one such 1633
[pdf-725b5d568e02e06adf | p.55] example which is initialised with a suite of physical variables, biogeochemical 1634
[pdf-9725cbd515520413cf | p.55] properties and also TA and DIC from datasets such as Global Ocean Data Analysis 1635
[pdf-b35591896b0dd10889 | p.55] Project (GLODAP). It assimilates a combination of physical and biogeochemical 1636
[pdf-28e7d7708636a00083 | p.55] data to produce physically conserved properties. As such models continue to 1637
[pdf-56fdaf2a322a9c3d33 | p.55] evolve, it will be increasingly possible to use them to assess regional and global 1638
[pdf-a7d5780736326ee1a0 | p.55] scale carbon inventories as well as ﬂuxes and evaluate them with satellite-based 1639
[pdf-ca31c378743cbe7405 | p.55] products. 1640
[pdf-26db40187d085e6acb | p.55] At the workshop, four priorities were identiﬁed in relation to the detection of 1641
[pdf-d6c6be6301b7f15c6c | p.55] inorganic carbon and the air-sea ﬂux of CO2 from space (summarised in Table 1642
[pdf-9633711ac33bc72100 | p.55] 7), including: 1) in-situ data; 2) satellite retrievals and mapping uncertainty; 3) 1643
[pdf-f4aca29e3c150f3800 | p.55] models and data integration; and 4) mechanistic understanding of gas transfer. 1644
[pdf-11d752ef1c2f6ec221 | p.55] 3.5.2. IC priority 1: In-situ data 1645
[pdf-0831be99f601af9fd2 | p.55] Challenges: Considering many components of inorganic carbon are not di- 1646
[pdf-877a06b6f2ce094ac9 | p.55] rectly observable from space, there is a strong reliance on in-situ data. Integrating 1647
[pdf-c255a736c4c9356bf6 | p.55] 55

## p.56 — Page 56

[pdf-6bb5373af3efdb4619 | p.56] in-situ data products with satellite data is challenging, owing to large diﬀerences 1648
[pdf-f6034c3f1ebd53ca91 | p.56] in spatial and temporal resolution. Furthermore, it can be challenging to integrate 1649
[pdf-a48a16f086e7343e8e | p.56] in-situ datasets from diﬀerent sources and collaborators, without community 1650
[pdf-bca2636d3b504194f6 | p.56] consensus on best practices and consistent use of traceable reference materials 1651
[pdf-ad3054d000439f555e | p.56] and consistent standards. 1652
[pdf-057d320030460dfdb5 | p.56] Gaps: Improved spatial and temporal coverage of ﬁeld observations in key 1653
[pdf-859239f168b0d884ee | p.56] regions and times, not only at the surface but also the full water column, is 1654
[pdf-91489f4af62ae686d9 | p.56] an essential requirement for the development, validation and use of satellite- 1655
[pdf-4c7ab8e3ad56ce898d | p.56] based IC approaches. Although there are some existing programs to monitor 1656
[pdf-491562b2a261dc72b3 | p.56] pCO2 from ships (e.g., SOCAT), air-sea CO2 ﬂux assessments are spatially and 1657
[pdf-9479dfaaa349e915ee | p.56] temporally limited by the extent and number of the in-situ data that underpin 1658
[pdf-eedc0596d8a3f2f830 | p.56] them. Additionally, our understanding of long-term changes in pCO2 and ﬂuxes, 1659
[pdf-2834509aae41b9d5b4 | p.56] in key ocean regions (e.g., the Southern Ocean), is limited by a lack of in-situ 1660
[pdf-18aa8952959d6e2b1f | p.56] data time-series stations (Sutton et al., 2019). At present, there is no dedicated 1661
[pdf-5c496f162949df6725 | p.56] framework for sustained, long-term monitoring of seawater pCO2 (particularly in 1662
[pdf-1170d44bf08f23e6ae | p.56] South Ocean which contributes around 40 % of the anthropogenic carbon uptake) 1663
[pdf-febd55486f8718cc8b | p.56] which is concerning as without these satellite methods are limited, though some 1664
[pdf-659580968acbf1f76b | p.56] satellite products like wind may still reveal insights into pCO2 dynamics. 1665
[pdf-42efe98fcc27f049f4 | p.56] There are also gaps in our ability to assure consistent quality of these in-situ 1666
[pdf-2d096ef993edfe13bd | p.56] observations. For example, TA and DIC observations require a certiﬁed reference 1667
[pdf-9b3ff1e540346305d2 | p.56] material (Dickson, 2010), that needs to be sustained into the future (at present 1668
[pdf-e62bdd35dae5c65dbd | p.56] there is only one laboratory able to produce it). Community-wide agreement on 1669
[pdf-d854f77d37ffa08e4c | p.56] best practices and approaches is needed for measurements that enable accurate 1670
[pdf-b5acf82c03e4fed843 | p.56] estimation of air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes. 1671
[pdf-2ef8b619baa3e947b9 | p.56] Opportunities There are opportunities to improve the spatial and temporal 1672
[pdf-1864589be1e16220b6 | p.56] resolution of in-situ data through autonomous platforms, such as BGC-Argo ﬂoats 1673
[pdf-29c94cda0620b58bf4 | p.56] (Williams et al., 2017; Bittig et al., 2018; Claustre et al., 2020) and autonomous 1674
[pdf-a22a438e5f178d7f5e | p.56] surface vehicles or sail drones (Sabine et al., 2020; Chiodi et al., 2021; Sutton et al., 1675
[pdf-b71f9387688067cc1d | p.56] 2021). Furthermore, as technology and instrumentation continues to advance, 1676
[pdf-ca3faf129ff5e0fe74 | p.56] there are opportunities to develop and integrate new sensors on these platforms, 1677
[pdf-b044ad9589911ec28b | p.56] such as exploiting polarimetry to detect PIC (Bishop et al., 2022). There may be 1678
[pdf-8817dfe6b127c2f740 | p.56] 56

[pdf-0bd5ec81e80c59b3ff | p.57] opportunities to extend recent eﬀorts to develop Fiducial Reference Measurements 1679
[pdf-f0ef4693d34736ef8e | p.57] (FRM) for satellite products (e.g., Le Menn et al., 2019; Banks et al., 2020; 1680
[pdf-a4b1e5e7f16f3fe93f | p.57] Mertikas et al., 2020) to in-situ measurements of inorganic carbon. This could 1681

[pdf-6bae9d371f82126a6b | p.57] 3.5.3. IC priority 2: Satellite retrievals and mapping uncertainty 1685
[pdf-2645d3f62c098f832a | p.57] Challenges: Estimating some components of the inorganic carbon cycle 1686
[pdf-b171be68c3c3f30bc1 | p.57] in optically-complex water is challenging. For example, current PIC satellite 1687
[pdf-95b45ee9ad2e7ff848 | p.57] products are global and are not as accurate in environments where other highly 1688
[pdf-2a9d04f191a4831617 | p.57] scattering materials are present (e.g., coastal shelf seas, but see Shutler et al., 1689
[pdf-5c8d72df6e361f2131 | p.57] 2010, who used of machine learning and computer vision approaches), and can 1690
[pdf-6c75f1d22c1078bafc | p.57] be ﬂagged as clouds. For all inorganic products (including TA and, ∆CO2) there 1691
[pdf-935db1fbb77dd1f54c | p.57] are also trade-oﬀs related to retaining the use of satellite algorithms based on 1692
[pdf-96cdb8d151a1e21265 | p.57] theoretical understanding, and harnessing new powerful empirical (black box) 1693
[pdf-0290e47f8f7f802732 | p.57] approaches, such as machine learning. 1694
[pdf-00e52b48daaa5e9990 | p.57] Gaps: The lack of pixel-by-pixel uncertainty estimates in the satellite prod- 1695
[pdf-69bd550fc98c7e3fb8 | p.57] ucts, for all components of the inorganic carbon cycle and carbonate system, is a 1696
[pdf-cb81f11516b3cdb871 | p.57] major gap that needs to be addressed. There is a crucial lack of coincident in-situ 1697
[pdf-fc712d1a104f56411f | p.57] observations of PIC concentrations and other highly scattering materials, along 1698
[pdf-33894231d9809dff38 | p.57] with full spectral measurements of speciﬁc inherent optical properties for PIC, 1699
[pdf-551cd119195916283d | p.57] needed to improve PIC concentration estimates in optically complex water. 1700
[pdf-d444b3dc0b8652f43f | p.57] Opportunities: Plans for improved spatial, spectral and temporal resolution 1701
[pdf-cd78238bae2b78b84b | p.57] of satellite sensors will likely lead to improvements in IC satellite products. 1702
[pdf-f49501203f79a9bf81 | p.57] For example, in optically complex waters, hyperspectral satellite data may help 1703
[pdf-1bba76fc7ccec2bb47 | p.57] diﬀerentiate among particles that scatter light with high eﬃciency, and lead to 1704
[pdf-a0ba9226f006285f15 | p.57] improved PIC products. Information on light polarisation (e.g. from PACE) may 1705
[pdf-f1bf0eaf9d3b19bf25 | p.57] also be useful for improving PIC algorithms. There may be opportunities to 1706
[pdf-2c87555b7839390802 | p.57] harness and build on recent techniques used to map uncertainty in satellite organic 1707
[pdf-ca1bb8344c0324a367 | p.57] carbon products (e.g., Evers-King et al., 2017; Martínez-Vicente et al., 2017; 1708
[pdf-4ba428abfceacc4ae4 | p.57] Brewin et al., 2017a; IOCCG, 2019) for the mapping of uncertainty in satellite 1709
[pdf-2a8de44cb02979eb5f | p.57] 57

[pdf-4f6b12f462b161558f | p.58] inorganic carbon products and ﬂux estimates. 1710
[pdf-cbdac6d05a8cf03e5b | p.58] 3.5.4. IC priority 3: Models and data integration 1711
[pdf-7bd3b7b5f9dfc13ac6 | p.58] Challenges: Bridging the diﬀerences in spatial and temporal scales in data 1712
[pdf-824d7f59a91c3ee523 | p.58] products and models, and diﬀerences in units (e.g. what is measured versus 1713
[pdf-743cc345e659b14f9d | p.58] what is represented in the models), is a major challenge in producing accurate 1714
[pdf-d04d65159c960539cb | p.58] inorganic carbon and ﬂux products. There are also challenges in extrapolating 1715
[pdf-50d6d6bd4af51a3179 | p.58] pCO2 observations to the surface and horizontally (see Woolf et al., 2016). 1716
[pdf-58241bea2776729fdf | p.58] Gaps: Closer collaboration between data generators and modellers is required 1717
[pdf-ac3b9a8c0034d604b1 | p.58] to improve the development of satellite-based inorganic carbon products for 1718
[pdf-4512f7e4b040d244d0 | p.58] integration into Earth System Models (Cronin et al., 2022). 1719
[pdf-bda54126f0491fb109 | p.58] Opportunities: Enhanced computer processing power (e.g., quantum com- 1720
[pdf-26af0b925a9d9c0228 | p.58] puting), and the development of new statistical tools for big data (e.g., machine 1721
[pdf-27e2076bf341d3c2ff | p.58] learning), oﬀer opportunities to improve model and data integration. There are 1722
[pdf-f1db9f083f26a3f2e0 | p.58] opportunities to improve model products by reconciling model carbon budgets 1723
[pdf-4765df2a4b35ce2d7a | p.58] with both satellite and in-situ observations, for example, by constraining the dif- 1724
[pdf-48ec38f4ee05a8c941 | p.58] ferent terms within the budget. Increases in the amount of data produced from a 1725
[pdf-d0cef815ae9e1b8ed1 | p.58] range of sources (models, satellites, ships, autonomous platforms, etc.) mean that 1726
[pdf-537c1cba127f79bfda | p.58] improved links among biogeochemical, physical, optical and biological data could 1727
[pdf-5f0f0f76fa2bdd4d54 | p.58] help improve data products (e.g., Bittig et al., 2018). Additionally, assimilation 1728
[pdf-4b92731a2b22978d76 | p.58] of these large dataset into models could improve reanalysis products, providing 1729
[pdf-bafa40b9a8a9969457 | p.58] accurate, high resolution pCO2, DIC and TA estimations on local, regional and 1730
[pdf-6101d17cf9a6ba6009 | p.58] global scales (Verdy and Mazloﬀ, 2017; Rosso et al., 2017; Carroll et al., 2020, 1731
[pdf-a0394ab5ace54ffdb8 | p.58] 2022). 1732
[pdf-1fb076b15620f1d650 | p.58] There is a key opportunity to pursue a full and routine integration of in-situ, 1733
[pdf-3984d22d0e67943b5a | p.58] model, and satellite observations to enable routine assessment of the surface water 1734
[pdf-efec53483c3ae8c392 | p.58] pCO2, air-sea exchange and the net integrated air-sea ﬂux (or ocean sink) of 1735
[pdf-f90a9f401b05130512 | p.58] carbon. This has been highlighted previously and is needed to support policy 1736
[pdf-ef042e88bb39a3f40d | p.58] decisions for reducing emissions (Shutler et al., 2019). 1737

## p.59 — Page 59

[pdf-d3bbb30ffd57823caa | p.59] within global assessments of the oceanic sink of CO2 (Woolf et al., 2019). Despite 1740
[pdf-7e166f982692e7bd31 | p.59] signiﬁcant progress in our ability to measure gas exchange, our mechanistic 1741
[pdf-80f4bf60b8947cba34 | p.59] understanding of gas transfer is incomplete (see Yang et al., 2022b). 1742
[pdf-5ec38ede360a456a7c | p.59] Gaps: There is a need to move away from wind speed as a proxy for air-sea 1743
[pdf-62513da8a330a078d7 | p.59] transfer (Shutler et al., 2019) as many other processes control the transfer includ- 1744
[pdf-a3371328935dff2627 | p.59] ing wave breaking, surfactants and bubbles and new advances in understanding 1745
[pdf-88ba5c6172d251861b | p.59] are now being made (e.g. Bell et al., 2017; Blomquist et al., 2017; Pereira et al., 1746
[pdf-e190607212fc9eeb5c | p.59] 2018). The carbon dynamics and air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes within mixed sea ice regions 1747
[pdf-a8777ba06923839898 | p.59] provides further complexities and are poorly understood (see Gupta et al., 2020; 1748
[pdf-7cac2d58b85bd17f7b | p.59] Watts et al., 2022) and these regions are expected to grow with a warming climate 1749
[pdf-a5fa88740a36aa4e4e | p.59] which illustrates a major gap in understanding. 1750
[pdf-32cac5a8bb1f5adcf7 | p.59] There are large uncertainties surrounding the inﬂuence of near surface tem- 1751
[pdf-9b51eca1663e697f4d | p.59] perature gradients on air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes (see Watson et al., 2020; Dong et al., 1752
[pdf-87a48a96c25958babf | p.59] 2022), and the role of wave breaking, bubbles and turbulence (see Bell et al., 1753
[pdf-04549c92601e954cf1 | p.59] 2017; Blomquist et al., 2017). 1754
[pdf-1467edafcab58132ec | p.59] Opportunities: State-of-the-art ﬂux measurement techniques, such as eddy 1755
[pdf-5502f3490ea8ca1a29 | p.59] covariance (see Dong et al., 2021), need to be established as FRM. There are 1756
[pdf-b3d9cc15bfb29b9572 | p.59] then opportunities to exploit these techniques on novel platforms and to use novel 1757
[pdf-26de405530fdd77000 | p.59] autonomous technologies to improve understanding of air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes. The 1758
[pdf-d6d50b567b3c1c9d6a | p.59] novel tools should be applied in a range of environments (e.g., low winds, high 1759
[pdf-63ad66b784c22a4409 | p.59] winds, marginal ice zones) to understand speciﬁc processes. For example, the 1760
[pdf-45692028416c9e88de | p.59] inﬂuence of near surface temperature gradients on air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes is currently 1761
[pdf-adc6e6684c2a49c471 | p.59] only theoretical and needs to be quantiﬁed/veriﬁed by direct observations. Im- 1762
[pdf-7c32db3813a14057a7 | p.59] provements in wind speed products could aid in better gas transfer (Taboada et al., 1763
[pdf-0aa7ff717b8c2476bd | p.59] 2019; Russell et al., 2021), although satellite-derived gas transfer estimates could 1764
[pdf-fae4038db570df0c82 | p.59] also be improved if measures other than wind speed are exploited that provide 1765
[pdf-a7f38d571b6204c935 | p.59] more direct observations of surface structure and turbulence (e.g., sea state or sea 1766
[pdf-0cabf7be94af520f3d | p.59] surface roughness using radar backscattering observations, see Goddijn-Murphy 1767
[pdf-b2bb7e3037fcdae67c | p.59] et al., 2013). 1768

## p.60 — Page 60

[pdf-1c9be97eb68d824b68 | p.60] 4. Cross-cutting activities 1769
[pdf-b28091c185350ecbc4 | p.60] 4.1. Blue Carbon (BC) 1770
[pdf-46085e757fe461514c | p.60] Tidal marshes, mangroves, macroalgae and seagrass beds, collectively referred 1771
[pdf-1568eea64611426574 | p.60] to as BC ecosystems, are some of the most carbon-dense habitats on Earth. Despite 1772
[pdf-915aaaac57b1197a14 | p.60] occupying only 0.2 % of the ocean surface, they are thought to contribute around 1773
[pdf-367a2bb3497e97f878 | p.60] 50 % of carbon burial in marine sediments, with a global stock size in the region 1774
[pdf-1b3aa3db127ca098aa | p.60] of 10 to 24 Gt C (Duarte et al., 2013). In addition to providing many essential 1775
[pdf-6fb74351da58f57bc9 | p.60] services, such as coastal storm and sea level protection, water quality regulation, 1776
[pdf-a44f9d3289aaa892f0 | p.60] wildlife habitat, biodiversity, shoreline stabilization, and food security, they are 1777
[pdf-6ae9c44abe38f4f004 | p.60] highly productive ecosystems that have the capacity to sequester vast amounts of 1778
[pdf-aa76dbc238c37872f4 | p.60] carbon and store it in their biomass and their soils (Mcleod et al., 2011). However, 1779
[pdf-d38ade1589bdc47f55 | p.60] their carbon sequestration capacity, carbon storage, and carbon export, depend 1780
[pdf-8c45b1af55bc3542f9 | p.60] on many critical processes, including inundation dynamics, sea level rise, air- 1781
[pdf-422378bf483d32fd29 | p.60] and water pollution, changes in salinity regimes, and rising temperatures. All 1782
[pdf-5bdc8f837f3c5904d9 | p.60] of which are sensitive to human impacts and climate change (Macreadie et al., 1783
[pdf-08a22ec77d377208b5 | p.60] 2019) with coastal ecosystems being a highly active interface between human and 1784
[pdf-404a6aeadc0c4a73cc | p.60] natural infrastructures and a complex mix of natural and anthropogenic processes. 1785
[pdf-4c52c29ea782270731 | p.60] The role that blue carbon habitats play in regional and global carbon budgets 1786
[pdf-5557aead81044a87e3 | p.60] and ﬂuxes is a big focus in carbon research (Mcleod et al., 2011). One of the 1787
[pdf-9891413d5050bd53e5 | p.60] biggest unknowns and largest sources of uncertainty in quantifying the role these 1788
[pdf-48c0c2ff7e9cc5dc67 | p.60] systems play in global carbon budgets and ﬂuxes, is mapping the spatial extent 1789
[pdf-50f8b17765911d458a | p.60] of BC and how it is changing. Satellites can play a major role in this, but an 1790
[pdf-7544ee8ad7a8078cfa | p.60] important distinction compared to green carbon (carbon that is contained in living 1791
[pdf-070067ac7d2ef09837 | p.60] vegetation and soil of terrestrial forest ecosystems; Mackey et al., 2008), is that 1792
[pdf-fd75f17b2864b22b7d | p.60] the carbon is primarily stored below rather than above ground. 1793
[pdf-0393af90209c9dabe7 | p.60] 4.1.1. State of the art in Blue Carbon 1794
[pdf-3feefbcd2454d5245f | p.60] Remote sensing technologies are increasingly used for studying BC ecosys- 1795
[pdf-033b9f0b8cb20739af | p.60] tems, owing to their synoptic capabilities, repeatability, accuracy and low cost 1796
[pdf-43108c81e4c4c143ea | p.60] (Hossain et al., 2015; Pham et al., 2019b; Campbell et al., 2022). Various tech- 1797
[pdf-bea69a3b79e06e0606 | p.60] niques have been utilised for this purpose, including spectral optical imagery, 1798
[pdf-d9193d9809f1c0e448 | p.60] 60

## p.61 — Page 61

[pdf-0c47a2e0d3c6fce843 | p.61] synthetic aperture radar (SAR), lidar and aerial photogrammetry (Pham et al., 1799
[pdf-fb02376b3da1d6dc42 | p.61] 2019a; Lamb et al., 2021). Of these technologies, high spatial resolution, multi- 1800
[pdf-d47b88ca26119de245 | p.61] spectral and hyper-spectral optical imagery are used more commonly, with the 1801
[pdf-8f0b0ef1d14bd08a54 | p.61] Landsat time-series thought to be the most widely-used dataset for studying 1802
[pdf-f0883d5cda1cbfc459 | p.61] changes in BC remotely over the past decade (Giri et al., 2011; Pham et al., 2019a; 1803
[pdf-910db07a8d28c5ff80 | p.61] Yang et al., 2022c). 1804
[pdf-5696e954da2fd656f9 | p.61] In recent years, there has been an increasing use of high resolution Sentinel-2 1805
[pdf-9e63e786625259a9c5 | p.61] and Landsat-8/9 imagery for mapping coastal BC, such as tidal marshes (e.g., 1806
[pdf-71bc3eaf0304529ad6 | p.61] Sun et al., 2021; Cao and Tzortziou, 2021) and mangroves (e.g., Castillo et al., 1807
[pdf-a06570894ff57cef4a | p.61] 2017). High frequency and high spatial resolution commercial satellites are 1808
[pdf-2711480c2717a6b1a5 | p.61] also increasingly being used for BC research. For example, the PlanetScope 1809
[pdf-839b0eb7f816a05f86 | p.61] constellation, DigitalGlobe’s WorldView-2, and Planet’s RapidEye satellites, are 1810
[pdf-b65d23b474eb7179a5 | p.61] oﬀering new insights into seagrass mapping (Wicaksono and Lazuardi, 2018; 1811
[pdf-84f53e0e475e47fc1d | p.61] Traganos and Reinartz, 2018; Coﬀer et al., 2020). Despite being challenged 1812
[pdf-671f34f09ef822eb37 | p.61] by the optical complexity of nearshore coastal waters, and accurate nearshore 1813
[pdf-6921eafed9563ec729 | p.61] atmospheric correction (Ibrahim et al., 2018; Tzortziou et al., 2018), submerged 1814
[pdf-d076435282950719be | p.61] aquatic vegetation habitats are now being studied remotely. For example, Huber 1815
[pdf-e9ce3927dca418399e | p.61] et al. (2021) used Sentinel-2 data, together with machine learning techniques 1816
[pdf-7d8dc25c1cad42227e | p.61] and advanced data processing, to map and monitor submerged aquatic vegetation 1817
[pdf-976c43d2a9913538b0 | p.61] habitats, including kelp forests, eelgrass meadows and rockweed beds, in Denmark 1818
[pdf-ec681c5249e3a9a5ec | p.61] and Sweden. Optical satellite remote sensing has been increasingly used for 1819
[pdf-391bce4526581c5fd9 | p.61] mapping benthic and pelagic macroalgae (e.g., Gower et al., 2006; Hu, 2009; 1820
[pdf-f5c2ec99dca389b2c8 | p.61] Cavanaugh et al., 2010; Hu et al., 2017; Wang et al., 2018; Schroeder et al., 2019; 1821
[pdf-a366702b187e38d0d1 | p.61] Wang and Hu, 2021), and has highlighted that macroalgae blooms are increasing 1822
[pdf-2652e0f5cff3c584d7 | p.61] in severity and frequency (Gower et al., 2013; Smetacek and Zingone, 2013; Qi 1823
[pdf-4c05e5f48dad90e99f | p.61] et al., 2016, 2017; Wang et al., 2019), with implications for carbon ﬁxation and 1824
[pdf-359252f405c7138a40 | p.61] sequestration (Paraguay-Delgado et al., 2020; Hu et al., 2021). 1825
[pdf-12a46ecdcc00f1276d | p.61] International eﬀorts have focused on translating science into policy, man- 1826
[pdf-c110ffe7c2172b45ca | p.61] agement and ﬁnance tools for conservation and restoration of blue carbon 1827
[pdf-f69af7e7b6b7b33f2b | p.61] ecosystems, for example, through the Blue Carbon Initiative (https://www. 1828
[pdf-97c488b461e1104096 | p.61] thebluecarboninitiative.org). Large scale mapping of ecosystem extent, change, 1829
[pdf-35ba6772ba3498813a | p.61] 61

## p.62 — Page 62

[pdf-3b542bcbc807521363 | p.62] and attributes such as carbon, is essential for blue carbon prioritisation and im- 1830
[pdf-7576ce30cceb0ccf7e | p.62] plementation at global to local scales, and remote sensing plays a key role in 1831
[pdf-899e3d9dd78db6c042 | p.62] this. For example, Goldberg et al. (2020) used satellite observations to help map 1832
[pdf-a50bd03ad2f2f17606 | p.62] mangrove coverage and change, and understand anthropogenic drivers of loss. 1833
[pdf-dc4615e661ae957b08 | p.62] The Global Mangrove Watch global mangrove forest baseline (taken as the year 1834
[pdf-c5914a06ed19fe4664 | p.62] 2010) was recently updated (v2.5) and has resulted in an additional of 2,660 km2, 1835
[pdf-6fd272e07f69fb6095 | p.62] yielding a revised global mangrove extent of 140,260 km2 (Bunting et al., 2022). 1836
[pdf-11251e01713ea9f5c9 | p.62] However, this needs to be built upon for BC as diﬀerent species will have diﬀerent 1837
[pdf-419b2edd1af081241c | p.62] below-ground biomass. Therefore, the carbon trapping eﬃciency and carbon 1838
[pdf-219fc688cb2900ee5c | p.62] uptake needs to be measured and used to calibrate maps of habitat extent. The 1839
[pdf-cb169ef5d7240b3389 | p.62] development of similar tools and baselines for seagrass, salt marsh, and kelp 1840
[pdf-74ad206b4b43ea41cb | p.62] ecosystems is needed. For a recent review on the topic of remote sensing of BC, 1841
[pdf-97f0b9a33c7d2b053b | p.62] the reader is referred to Pham et al. (2019a). 1842
[pdf-ad50419342405e08f3 | p.62] At the workshop, three priorities were identiﬁed in relation to the remote 1843
[pdf-83472c9283c78e460b | p.62] sensing of BC, these are summarised in Table 8 and include: 1) satellite sensors; 1844
[pdf-a116a9458057707270 | p.62] 2) algorithms, retrievals and model integration; and 3) data access and accounting. 1845
[pdf-1647d853b1b0071204 | p.62] 4.1.2. BC priority 1: Satellite sensors 1846
[pdf-b2284994a4a491d7c6 | p.62] Challenges: Owing to the high temporal variability and heterogeneity of 1847
[pdf-e4bc766e72bcfd67d7 | p.62] many BC ecosystems (tidal or otherwise), there is a requirement for monitoring 1848
[pdf-5c13236bfff9b95892 | p.62] at high temporal (hourly) and spatial (tidal) scales. This is challenging with the 1849
[pdf-7793126bea9b0bb773 | p.62] current ﬂeet of Earth Observing satellites. 1850
[pdf-b71fb0ad1ed3e69792 | p.62] Gaps: Although Landsat has proven vital for the long-term monitoring of 1851
[pdf-f5477bf766ea412e25 | p.62] some BC ecosystems (e.g., Ha et al., 2021), there is a lack of long-term satellite 1852
[pdf-7a8293f5f3f13cf804 | p.62] datasets for change detection in many BC ecosystems. 1853
[pdf-0674bb157623c3cb6d | p.62] Opportunities: New sensors and techniques are leading to signiﬁcant ad- 1854
[pdf-4577aa560c5fb8c777 | p.62] vancements in the spatial and temporal characterization and monitoring of BC 1855
[pdf-04f3f0c9fc8476d1c8 | p.62] ecosystems. New hyperspectral observations (e.g., PACE, GLIMR, PRISMA, 1856
[pdf-837904c4df9a905835 | p.62] DLR Earth Sensing Imaging Spectrometer (DESIS), Environmental Mapping 1857
[pdf-cfdbbbb51ea72b913e | p.62] and Analysis Program (EnMAP); NASA’s Surface Biology and Geology (SBG); 1858
[pdf-bd58eb4bb0bfbb388c | p.62] CHIME) at high to medium resolution and global scale, have the potential to 1859
[pdf-894595efdc7724bbf8 | p.62] distinguish diﬀerences among mangrove, seagrass, salt marsh species, and esti- 1860
[pdf-09debf94d83da6e973 | p.62] 62

## p.63 — Page 63

[pdf-c155887003c834ff67 | p.63] mate satellite products relevant to carbon quality. High spatial resolution (3-5 m) 1861
[pdf-d97dcbf643bb093108 | p.63] imagery from constellations of satellite sensors (e.g., PlanetScope) provides 1862
[pdf-9a9c38a21799bcd549 | p.63] an unprecedented dataset to study vegetation characteristics in BC ecosystems 1863
[pdf-6d6f1a0927b047ce9c | p.63] (Warwick-Champion et al., 2022). Multiple images per day from new geosta- 1864
[pdf-10635c0d77e4c58808 | p.63] tionary satellite instruments (e.g., GLIMR), will allow to capture tidal dynamics 1865
[pdf-d6c4c93b8fc0696a73 | p.63] in BC ecosystems, and monitor them (e.g., seagrass meadows) under optimum 1866
[pdf-62a42e4da073c78067 | p.63] conditions. Additionally, there is scope to build on eﬀorts to develop satellite 1867
[pdf-37ef0455987a56ac8c | p.63] climate records (e.g., through ESA’s CCI) with a focus on BC, to help develop 1868
[pdf-d03342a0628e5a799d | p.63] the long-term data records needed. 1869
[pdf-aa79489621d50946bf | p.63] 4.1.3. BC priority 2: Algorithms, retrievals and model integration 1870
[pdf-79fbf86a91fab33eb3 | p.63] Challenges: Considering many BC remote sensing approaches are regional, 1871
[pdf-a2ae2c2c1fb95c5f09 | p.63] they are not easily applied (or have been tested) at global scale. Owing to the 1872
[pdf-86223d3ebb4d8d3613 | p.63] complexity of some of the techniques, uncertainty estimation for carbon ﬂuxes in 1873
[pdf-25d838fe5a0e43e760 | p.63] BC ecosystems is particularly challenging. Regarding the detection of subaquatic 1874
[pdf-9423d6d133bbdf9a7a | p.63] vegetation (and some other BC ecosystems), there are large uncertainties in 1875
[pdf-75d7f26c2c3777b2db | p.63] the impact of the atmosphere and water depth on the signal. Considering large 1876
[pdf-4b5f975036155d9682 | p.63] quantities of carbon are stored in the sediments of BC habitats, there are challenges 1877
[pdf-8b6a5e15882652acf4 | p.63] to develop direct or indirect satellite techniques to monitor the dynamics of 1878
[pdf-b8db46bf13eaebc696 | p.63] sediment carbon. The lack of models that link carbon storage and cycling in 1879
[pdf-981cac4fce4cbd25ab | p.63] terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems, further challenges our understanding of carbon 1880
[pdf-2a8393824af0b53d72 | p.63] ﬂuxes and stocks in BC habitats. Sub-pixel variability poses a challenge when 1881
[pdf-5f6c5b95be36636ed3 | p.63] monitoring macroalgae using courser resolution satellite data. 1882
[pdf-414980b4555d16de0f | p.63] Gaps: A major gap to improving algorithms and methods, is the limited 1883
[pdf-f771f110fe9c27f45a | p.63] availability of in-situ data for development and validation. For example, the lack 1884
[pdf-00cb24cf85364497e6 | p.63] of measurements on rates (e.g., Sargassum carbon ﬁxation and sequestration 1885
[pdf-a58939e8c189b11717 | p.63] eﬃciency) severely limits our ability to quantify large scale BC budgets (e.g., for 1886
[pdf-2291b3be8228f2a629 | p.63] pelagic macroalgae, see Hu et al., 2021). The lack of basic ecosystem mapping 1887
[pdf-81c65a6b752d61e0b2 | p.63] and change detection for seagrasses and kelp forests, limits our ability to extrap- 1888
[pdf-f72e3d547cc3ceb95f | p.63] olate these measurements to large scales using remote sensing. The lack of BC 1889
[pdf-8408c273010ed1c782 | p.63] ecosystem models limits our ability to quantify full BC carbon budgets (including 1890
[pdf-a3a6dcc2b9d15e93f7 | p.63] soil) globally. 1891
[pdf-a423b1c92902e84076 | p.63] 63

## p.64 — Page 64

[pdf-97920663b505d55889 | p.64] Opportunities: With improvements in computation power and statistical 1892
[pdf-bc58810fb0f37077af | p.64] analysis of big data (e.g., techniques like machine learning) there is scope to 1893
[pdf-722bf1df6846c8ba8a | p.64] improve satellite algorithms and methods of BC carbon quantiﬁcation (e.g., Huber 1894
[pdf-3209ee32bcd61f5397 | p.64] et al., 2021). Additionally, fusion of hyperspectral optical and SAR data provides 1895
[pdf-5eec29321a87f0e58a | p.64] a promising approach for characterization of tidal wetland interfaces, including 1896
[pdf-54265f2a5b6389ce23 | p.64] wetland vegetation characteristics, inundation regimes, and their impact on carbon 1897
[pdf-dc156ec28b6993d2d8 | p.64] ﬂuxes. New in-situ monitoring techniques (e.g., drones) are becoming useful to 1898
[pdf-f2601e04ea233edec2 | p.64] bridge the scales between satellites and in-situ BC monitoring (e.g., Duﬀy et al., 1899
[pdf-beb1335ea83100d9a0 | p.64] 2018). 1900
[pdf-39fdfa621fed79d27a | p.64] 4.1.4. BC priority 3: Satellite data access and blue carbon accounting 1901
[pdf-e17914fbe10006d998 | p.64] Challenges: Existing products and approaches are not easily accessible by 1902
[pdf-05a721cda2876093fc | p.64] users who have limited remote sensing expertise. With the increasing use of com- 1903
[pdf-13178dddcef06b9dbd | p.64] mercial satellites, there are challenges to ensure cost-eﬀective monitoring using 1904
[pdf-9d2d1a07c248480984 | p.64] remote sensing techniques to track the progress of rehabilitation and restoration 1905
[pdf-479c622e78544bcc94 | p.64] of blue carbon ecosystems. 1906
[pdf-370ff01fed9ca64631 | p.64] Gaps: There are a lack of products suited to project development and carbon 1907
[pdf-17ec5090398acf831d | p.64] accounting. The remote-sensing science community must work directly with 1908
[pdf-4a652bc2fc3f0709f3 | p.64] policymakers, conservationists, and others, to ensure advances in such products 1909
[pdf-4c22b11e350209256f | p.64] are tailored to applications and that the tools developed are available broadly 1910
[pdf-fa1fc7d0d8a6adb6a8 | p.64] and equitably. Products are also now needed on global scales, at higher spatial 1911
[pdf-0e8ba2a4dc479d9d56 | p.64] and temporal resolutions, and in a broader range of ecosystems, to support BC 1912
[pdf-a3c1fd210dc93f7ded | p.64] integration into national carbon accounts and to expand the application of carbon 1913
[pdf-2c947e2d933ada67e3 | p.64] ﬁnancing. 1914
[pdf-1c8b1cb3ce98998ef0 | p.64] Opportunities: There is increasing momentum towards eﬀorts to develop BC 1915
[pdf-94ce5aea857b5f498a | p.64] habitat mapping portals that are user friendly, for example, see Huber et al. (2021). 1916
[pdf-c710c8aa46a1c80473 | p.64] These developments are needed to support blue carbon based conservation and 1917
[pdf-d0059b5bb7926b673b | p.64] restoration and have been instrumental in the recent development of blue carbon 1918
[pdf-bd6557e265a2dcb139 | p.64] policy and ﬁnancing by supporting prioritisation, assessment, and monitoring. 1919
[pdf-dc903a3ca8bfffce6b | p.64] 4.2. Extreme Events (EEs) 1920
[pdf-05e84d10d4b477549e | p.64] 64

## p.65 — Page 65

[pdf-6f935b59023d22ce72 | p.65] EEs can be deﬁned as events that occur in the upper or lower end of the range 1921
[pdf-21e55073d0b0f603f2 | p.65] of historical measurements (Katz and Brown, 1992). Such events occur in the 1922
[pdf-6699e3887dcd128238 | p.65] atmosphere (e.g., tropical cyclones, dust storms), ocean (e.g., marine heatwaves, 1923
[pdf-2200a1c0b125c49d2a | p.65] tsunami’s), and on land (e.g., volcanic eruption, extreme bushﬁres), aﬀecting 1924
[pdf-4024c80ce87cdd78de | p.65] marine carbon cycling at multiple spatial-temporal scales (Bates et al., 1998; 1925
[pdf-df3ea1ed71df0351e3 | p.65] Jickells et al., 2005; Gruber et al., 2021). With continued global warming in the 1926
[pdf-804408819eb92e270c | p.65] coming decades, many EEs are expected to intensify, occur more frequently, last 1927
[pdf-cb8778196f95c9117c | p.65] longer and extend over larger regions (Huang et al., 2015; Diﬀenbaugh et al., 2017; 1928
[pdf-a66ca6f0be4eebd346 | p.65] Frölicher et al., 2018). Extreme events and their eﬀects on marine ecosystems and 1929
[pdf-66afb79c38ba20eb0d | p.65] carbon cycling can be observed, to some extent, by various methods, including: 1930
[pdf-ce532c536181c6fe62 | p.65] ships, buoys, autonomous platforms and satellite sensors (e.g., Di Biagio et al., 1931
[pdf-9c9ba136628785ff30 | p.65] 2020; Hayashida et al., 2020; Le Grix et al., 2021; Wang et al., 2022). Here, we 1932
[pdf-e72b2a71ddc5246a1a | p.65] ﬁrst provide a broad overview of the current state of the art in the topic, before 1933
[pdf-54124f06707937242f | p.65] highlighting the priorities identiﬁed at the workshop. 1934
[pdf-951ff826877f6c5cd1 | p.65] 4.2.1. State of the art in Extreme Events 1935
[pdf-4e386306572408e4c7 | p.65] Extremely high temperatures and droughts due to global warming are expected 1936
[pdf-ee6eed83552398be0c | p.65] to result in more frequent and intense wildﬁres and dust storm events in some 1937
[pdf-ab971d097ce40acee7 | p.65] regions (Huang et al., 2015; Abatzoglou et al., 2019; Harris and Lucas, 2019). 1938
[pdf-d20484fbe66767c013 | p.65] Aerosols emitted from wildﬁre and dust storms can signiﬁcantly impact marine 1939
[pdf-993e60cd83e92a5ad0 | p.65] biogeochemistry through wet and dry deposition (Gao et al., 2019), by supplying 1940
[pdf-f33007b58df8b631ff | p.65] soluble nutrients (Schlosser et al., 2017; Barkley et al., 2019), especially essential 1941
[pdf-8f23ae13f9bc3096ee | p.65] trace metals such as iron (Jickells et al., 2005; Mahowald et al., 2005, 2011) 1942
[pdf-e91be7af6d0589613c | p.65] which can also enhance the export of carbon from the photic zone to depth 1943
[pdf-b2ed72f22c0d6a95bb | p.65] (Pabortsava et al., 2017). The record-breaking Australian wildﬁre that occurred 1944
[pdf-887053fc6cccc873c4 | p.65] between September 2019 and March 2020 was evaluated using a combination of 1945
[pdf-947f89a3793690ea6d | p.65] satellite, BGC-Argo ﬂoat, in-situ atmospheric sampling and primary productivity 1946
[pdf-4f8f825edf805be324 | p.65] estimation (Li et al., 2021; Tang et al., 2021; Wang et al., 2022). The wildﬁre 1947
[pdf-aec6ec31db85c5a7a3 | p.65] released aerosols that contained essential nutrients such as iron for growth of 1948
[pdf-7b5e298e18798417cc | p.65] marine phytoplankton. These aerosols were transported by westerly winds over 1949
[pdf-d5d8fae66f5abca4c2 | p.65] the South Paciﬁc Ocean and the deposition resulted in widespread phytoplankton 1950
[pdf-b78e3afcf0f2030396 | p.65] blooms. Severe dust storms, observable from space, in arid or semi-arid regions 1951
[pdf-1a215c7bdc3693ea2c | p.65] 65

## p.66 — Page 66

[pdf-487c3c01fb42af809d | p.66] can also transport aerosols to coastal and open ocean waters increasing ocean 1952
[pdf-4cc84402b83cd486f6 | p.66] primary productivity (Gabric et al., 2010; Chen et al., 2016; Yoon et al., 2017). 1953
[pdf-d439627ae33277121f | p.66] Volcanic eruptions can also fertilise the ocean. The solubility and bioavailabil- 1954
[pdf-18a101b01abfa79132 | p.66] ity of volcanic ash is thought to be much higher than mineral dust (Achterberg 1955
[pdf-d4fe1a3f6c9c431492 | p.66] et al., 2013; Lindenthal et al., 2013), and can act as the source of nutrients and/or 1956
[pdf-a296ae625efce2fc6a | p.66] organic carbon for microbial plankton, and inﬂuence aggregation processes (Wein- 1957
[pdf-9b4497abb40304b624 | p.66] bauer et al., 2017). The ﬁrst multi-platform observation (using SeaWiFS images 1958
[pdf-12c1ae4e71f2395fa2 | p.66] and in-situ data) of the impact of a volcano eruption was provided by Uematsu 1959
[pdf-4e12de0d7e621b38cc | p.66] et al. (2004), who observed the enhancement of primary productivity caused 1960
[pdf-dd837421a51de3291b | p.66] by the additional atmospheric deposition from the Miyake-jima Volcano in the 1961
[pdf-c5184e69a1a471b8f5 | p.66] nutrient-deﬁcient region south of the Kuroshio. Lin et al. (2011) observed ab- 1962
[pdf-9bd9ee07f3e09bc316 | p.66] normally high phytoplankton biomass from satellite and elevated concentrations 1963
[pdf-3402c24fc8e61b68c1 | p.66] of limiting nutrients, from laboratory experiments, caused by aerosol released 1964
[pdf-616a8a34d60ef096c4 | p.66] by the Anatahan Volcano in 2003. The eruption of K¯ılauea volcano triggered a 1965
[pdf-f822075be6b4ff68b8 | p.66] diatom-dominated phytoplankton bloom near Hawaii (Wilson et al., 2019). More 1966
[pdf-fa2d3962115d706494 | p.66] recently, the eruption of Hunga Tonga–Hunga Ha’apai ejected about 400,000 1967
[pdf-e80885da8d212351a1 | p.66] tonnes of SO2, threw ash high into the stratosphere, and caused a catastrophic 1968
[pdf-b80fc99f2bc23e0c63 | p.66] tsunami on Tonga’s nearby islands (Witze, 2022). Detailed observations on its 1969
[pdf-837d6cfeeabc8d0717 | p.66] biochemical eﬀects have yet to be reported. 1970
[pdf-a6eef6f71444a92930 | p.66] Using satellite data with in-situ observations, and proﬁling ﬂoats, recent re- 1971
[pdf-f4ae2624aa7f7b7dae | p.66] search showed remarkable changes during marine heatwaves (MHWs) in the 1972
[pdf-def85e1fcc4ea458bc | p.66] oceanic carbon system (Long et al., 2021; Gruber et al., 2021; Burger et al., Ac- 1973
[pdf-1e8be74ecbef936a28 | p.66] cepted) and phytoplankton structures (Yang et al., 2018; Le Grix et al., 2021), that 1974
[pdf-9a4a5d028f319da715 | p.66] are linked to background nutrient concentrations (Hayashida et al., 2020). MHWs 1975
[pdf-4be8d93b91d7915956 | p.66] (and cold spells) are deﬁned as prolonged periods of anomalously high (low) 1976
[pdf-b5e20633b4521261be | p.66] ocean temperatures (Hobday et al., 2016), which can have devastating impacts on 1977
[pdf-9c4a2918ccc3915298 | p.66] marine organisms and socio-economics systems (Cavole et al., 2016; Wernberg 1978
[pdf-adaa40ddde75d1b40d | p.66] et al., 2016; Couch et al., 2017; Frölicher and Laufkötter, 2018; Hughes et al., 1979
[pdf-a9f0453cfbaae56de6 | p.66] 2018; Smale et al., 2019; Cheung et al., 2021). MHWs and cold spells are caused 1980
[pdf-73c62986e3eaae5d70 | p.66] by a combination of local oceanic and atmospheric processes, and modulated 1981
[pdf-3c16c34f2379d69b45 | p.66] by large-scale climate variability and change (Holbrook et al., 2019; Vogt et al., 1982
[pdf-9f0a3fadd1b0312798 | p.66] 66

## p.67 — Page 67

[pdf-393d8ef70baee78d0d | p.67] 2022). As a consequence of long-term ocean warming, MHWs have become 1983
[pdf-92f3675a5fd94bcb08 | p.67] longer-lasting and more frequent, and have impacted increasingly large areas 1984
[pdf-e7de29b3315cce7a90 | p.67] (Frölicher et al., 2018; Oliver et al., 2018). Satellite and autonomous platforms 1985
[pdf-8e3944bd522b17503c | p.67] have been used to study MHWs in many regions, including: the Mediterranean 1986
[pdf-5fd5267b67c6f4898a | p.67] Sea (Olita et al., 2007; Bensoussan et al., 2010), the East China Sea (Tan and 1987
[pdf-3097879df1dddb5208 | p.67] Cai, 2018), NE Paciﬁc (Bif et al., 2019), the Atlantic (Rodrigues et al., 2019), 1988
[pdf-4b11c0ca1fc0f7ab62 | p.67] Western Australia (Pearce and Feng, 2013) and the Tasman Sea (Oliver et al., 1989
[pdf-09b22aa57665d30a36 | p.67] 2017; Salinger et al., 2019). 1990
[pdf-4c98450d9071fbe02b | p.67] Tropical cyclones (called hurricanes or typhoons in diﬀerent regions) are 1991
[pdf-ebeb6e7a940dde8ffd | p.67] deﬁned as non-frontal, synoptic scale, low-pressure systems over tropical or sub- 1992
[pdf-3ef590ddd22987b646 | p.67] tropical waters with organized convection (Lander and Holland, 1993). They 1993
[pdf-86be0f1660e7d7abc6 | p.67] can bring deep nutrients up into the photic zone and lead to changes in the 1994
[pdf-4494dc2d8b74cf5902 | p.67] local carbon system by cooling the sea surface (Li et al., 2009; Chen et al., 1995
[pdf-dd63998f4d9ed63322 | p.67] 2017; Osburn et al., 2019). Satellite data are often used for studying tropical 1996
[pdf-d638ccf7648af597d5 | p.67] cyclones, however, it is diﬃcult to obtain clear images shortly after typhoons due 1997
[pdf-9cb53d7d47527edbf0 | p.67] to extensive cloud cover (Naik et al., 2008; Hung et al., 2010; Zang et al., 2020). 1998
[pdf-024df0f4009cc107df | p.67] Combining satellite observations with Argo ﬂoat and biogeochemical models is 1999
[pdf-56ca685a9a55eb7fc7 | p.67] increasingly being used to understand biological impacts of tropical cyclones 2000
[pdf-348cb431ba43a21a2a | p.67] (Shang et al., 2008; Chai et al., 2021). D’Sa et al. (2018) have reported intense 2001
[pdf-7ba0e8d3ccf762bf2b | p.67] changes in dissolved organic matter dynamics after Hurricane Harvey in 2017 2002
[pdf-14e7c2719d7e3ef9b7 | p.67] and then reported changes in particulate and dissolved organic matter dynamics 2003
[pdf-eb8290cb90327209b6 | p.67] and ﬂuxes after Hurricane Michael in 2018 (D’Sa et al., 2019), highlighting 2004
[pdf-695b1d7f7a9122ea46 | p.67] the importance of using multiple satellite data with diﬀerent resolutions as well 2005
[pdf-46d2942894b08dbf6e | p.67] as hydrodynamic models. Using the constellation of Landsat-8 and Sentinel- 2006
[pdf-47c81c0efc1512a43c | p.67] 2A/2B sensors, Cao and Tzortziou (2021) showed strong carbon export from 2007
[pdf-032e2f06392da686d4 | p.67] the Blackwater National Wildlife Refuge marsh into the Chesapeake Bay and 2008
[pdf-bcadc7f88a337a24ca | p.67] increase in estuarine DOC concentrations by more than a factor of two after the 2009
[pdf-edfaaa9eb3b117b44d | p.67] passage of Hurricane Matthew compared to pre-hurricane levels under similar 2010
[pdf-d6c1f89693ba93ad63 | p.67] tidal conditions. 2011
[pdf-f6d7a2c2e8f9869fae | p.67] The impacts of marine compound events, deﬁned as extremes in diﬀerent 2012
[pdf-98a7adbeb0cc4192d5 | p.67] hazards that occur simultaneously or in close spatial-temporal sequence, are being 2013
[pdf-ef5025cf6e1b9d9e3d | p.67] 67

[pdf-3085cebe00663612a2 | p.68] increasingly studied (Gruber et al., 2021). The dual or even triple compound 2014
[pdf-1e214b5ecf24858ae0 | p.68] extremes such as ocean warming, deoxygenation and acidiﬁcation, could lead 2015
[pdf-e305e2e9dda4f31839 | p.68] to particularly high biological and ecological impacts (Gruber, 2011; Zscheis- 2016
[pdf-993843570411d6cf09 | p.68] chler et al., 2018; Le Grix et al., 2021; Burger et al., Accepted). The increasing 2017
[pdf-e60a73535eb363df7d | p.68] prevalence of extreme Harmful Algae Blooms (HAB) have been linked with ex- 2018
[pdf-c563571e0b49086ac8 | p.68] treme events, and satellites play a major role in their monitoring and management 2019
[pdf-054cba772e71a13845 | p.68] (IOCCG, 2021). Although EEs have emerged as a topic of great interest over the 2020
[pdf-6b6c97a73527735291 | p.68] past decade, our understanding of their impacts on the marine ecosystems and 2021
[pdf-e492259ab08a5c3fba | p.68] ocean carbon cycle remains limited. 2022
[pdf-8fc1576502f4994f37 | p.68] At the workshop, three priorities (summarised in Table 9) were identiﬁed in 2023
[pdf-3b11bbffaf40a8ce1b | p.68] relation to understanding impacts of EEs on the ocean carbon cycle: 1) in-situ 2024
[pdf-ff9b714bec12bb41ad | p.68] data; 2) satellite sensing technology; and 3) model synergy and transdisciplinary 2025
[pdf-e9693e0e02b823666d | p.68] research. 2026
[pdf-58394acf53b66c121a | p.68] 4.2.2. EEs priority 1: In-situ data 2027
[pdf-7f7ccac17b33c2d1f0 | p.68] Challenges: In-situ observations are essential to monitor EEs, especially 2028
[pdf-a5a067ba8b7a5c8389 | p.68] considering some EEs are hard to monitor from space (e.g., clouds with tropical 2029
[pdf-b0d67aca0c6d61bf15 | p.68] cyclones or volcanic eruptions) and require ground truthing, owing to challenges 2030
[pdf-1d37c3669b30be8206 | p.68] around satellite retrievals (e.g., atmospheric aerosols with dust events and volcanic 2031
[pdf-2de84848c8ce8e9773 | p.68] eruptions). In some cases, EEs can be close to the valid range of measurements 2032
[pdf-379cb306bebca7bd50 | p.68] retrieved by satellites. Considering the temporal scales of EEs, their sporadic 2033
[pdf-a5e1a0b4eb2944627f | p.68] occurrence, and hazardous environments, they are extremely challenging and 2034
[pdf-1c6cb88982bb8b7091 | p.68] sometimes dangerous to monitor in-situ using ship-based techniques. 2035
[pdf-ba01f5468a32f554d3 | p.68] Gaps: At present there are major gaps in the availability of in-situ observations 2036
[pdf-f3fd88c95f474152c7 | p.68] of EEs. This severely limits our understanding of their impact on the ocean 2037
[pdf-71a8d10f685668ca08 | p.68] carbon cycle. Gaps are even greater in subsurface waters. Long time-series 2038
[pdf-aca0851ac5c18bcd70 | p.68] measurements with high frequency resolution are also essential to provide robust 2039
[pdf-e6c69439d137660b4c | p.68] baselines against which extremes can be detected and attributed. 2040
[pdf-7e5c2c2e2004a70ffa | p.68] Opportunities: With an expanding network of autonomous in-situ platforms 2041
[pdf-20ab6f41d1d130ba84 | p.68] (Chai et al., 2020), we are becoming better positioned to monitor EEs. It will be 2042
[pdf-696361137f50417319 | p.68] important that these networks of autonomous in-situ platforms have fast response 2043
[pdf-99d8655884cebd3d19 | p.68] protocols that can be implemented soon after an extreme event takes place, so 2044
[pdf-538bcd785351dcd064 | p.68] 68

[pdf-604a1b51d965b44c6a | p.69] valuable data are collected and not missed. It is also essential that funding 2045
[pdf-1ec91ad3cdfb9f2fea | p.69] continues, at the international level, to support these expanding networks of 2046
[pdf-2bdbfdc088f66af76d | p.69] autonomous platforms. 2047
[pdf-47efa47d56e5d5263d | p.69] 4.2.3. EEs priority 2: Satellite sensing technology 2048
[pdf-3e31e2d7b809153985 | p.69] Challenges: Monitoring EEs from space requires suitable temporal and spatial 2049
[pdf-2c6b24023f1421003a | p.69] coverage to track the event, which varies depending on the nature and location 2050
[pdf-4d10f3a0c2d7b84d2e | p.69] of the event. Some events require high temporal and spatial coverage, which 2051
[pdf-ffc994503fa02ccf51 | p.69] challenges current remote sensing systems. Other challenges exist, for example, 2052
[pdf-3d0baab91b4c0204dd | p.69] dealing with cloud coverage during tropical cyclones, or retrievals in the presence 2053
[pdf-6724f5d1d63c209755 | p.69] of complex aerosols (e.g., volcanic eruptions). 2054
[pdf-44ca06e424bbe3d1b0 | p.69] Gaps: High temporal and spatial resolution data are required for monitoring 2055
[pdf-263d2e1ee3496f63f7 | p.69] some EEs. There are gaps in satellite data for some EEs (e.g., clouds). Algorithms 2056
[pdf-42909c227abd078979 | p.69] for satellite retrievals during some EEs (e.g., volcanic eruptions) require detailed 2057
[pdf-511d831c431fec9bd9 | p.69] knowledge on the optical properties of the aerosols present. Long time-series 2058
[pdf-b936102e7be4e7000c | p.69] remote sensing data are needed for baselines against which extremes can be 2059
[pdf-ba7d917f68d0213f8d | p.69] monitored. 2060
[pdf-220a818498e86689f9 | p.69] Opportunities: Synergistic use of diﬀerent long-term, high-frequency and 2061
[pdf-46561981ff4d198a49 | p.69] high-resolution, remote sensing data may allow better insight into extreme events 2062
[pdf-fe9b87819fc7eb56d1 | p.69] and their development. For example, combining ocean colour products from 2063
[pdf-a7910b6f9bf342a63d | p.69] ESA’s OC-CCI (e.g., Sathyendranath et al., 2019a) and the National Oceanic and 2064
[pdf-f308a146bfdbbde573 | p.69] Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) Climate Data Record Programme (e.g., 2065
[pdf-7639d61ad4a3956618 | p.69] Bates et al., 2016). The increased spectral, spatial and temporal resolution of 2066
[pdf-c5685beeab83a7cdf9 | p.69] the satellite sensors and platforms would help to improve understanding of the 2067
[pdf-954b80c6e53bb2669e | p.69] response of phytoplankton community (Losa et al., 2017) and their diel cycles to 2068
[pdf-8d2745693db2e238c6 | p.69] extreme events, and HAB detection, for example, with NASA’s PACE mission 2069
[pdf-de748707e74f000c06 | p.69] (Werdell et al., 2019) and the Korean geostationary GOCI satellite platform (Choi 2070
[pdf-d07e56ddeb6e42a69e | p.69] et al., 2012). There are opportunities to derive indicators of EEs for determining 2071
[pdf-7ff1d359b212c0d56c | p.69] good environmental status of our seas and oceans, for example, for use in the 2072
[pdf-07549c2d292484d0ee | p.69] EU Marine Strategy Framework Directive and the Oslo and Paris (OSPAR) 2073
[pdf-e33090fff9ffefc4a9 | p.69] Conventions EEs and pollution monitoring. 2074
