[pdf-21b25d7a35cf8542b5 | p.70] 4.2.4. EEs priority 3: Model synergy and transdisciplinary research 2075
[pdf-b8750cf7797339be24 | p.70] Challenges: Owing to gaps in observational platforms (both satellite and 2076
[pdf-6760b7f3da95e83d20 | p.70] in-situ observations) and the transdisciplinary nature of EEs, there is a need to 2077
[pdf-9591a97435e5194936 | p.70] utilise Earth System Models (ESMs) for understanding EEs and projecting future 2078
[pdf-0de938dc6e53faae9b | p.70] scenarios, and to bring together communities from multiple ﬁelds. 2079
[pdf-8e70db8a37b17ec628 | p.70] Gaps: Reliable projections of extreme events require higher spatial resolution 2080
[pdf-6bcab156918320e4f5 | p.70] ESMs, with improved representation of marine ecosystems. ESMs ideally need 2081
[pdf-dd6903930d9c7f5979 | p.70] to include prognostic representations of EEs processes, and improvements are 2082
[pdf-79b92fa9505af68361 | p.70] needed in coupling with land via aerosol emissions and deposition due to ﬁres 2083
[pdf-4bbb25fe0665be88b7 | p.70] or due to dust. Transdisciplinary research on the impact of extremes on marine 2084
[pdf-27256a7d53074799ca | p.70] organisms and ecosystem services is needed to close knowledge gaps. 2085
[pdf-6161dae546f278f0c7 | p.70] Opportunities: With enhancements in computation power and improvements 2086
[pdf-1b5fadac1a81b7cd41 | p.70] in ESMs and data assimilation techniques, there is likely to be an increasing use 2087
[pdf-eb04ec065fb7c88e18 | p.70] of ESMs for understanding EEs, and especially marine compound events. To 2088
[pdf-2db1eef050df22bdc2 | p.70] promote cross-disciplinary research, support is needed for collaborative projects 2089
[pdf-abd7c434117d1493fe | p.70] and digital platforms, to make data digestible to non-experts (e.g., Giovanni 2090
[pdf-9479af91a8a1762af8 | p.70] (https://giovanni.gsfc.nasa.gov/giovanni/), MyOcean https://marine.copernicus. 2091
[pdf-c9914828215348fcc1 | p.70] eu/access-data/myocean-viewer)). 2092
[pdf-77ba99335bd24d0169 | p.70] 4.3. Carbon Budget Closure (CBC) 2093
[pdf-7496ba6ccbd6340e9d | p.70] Quantifying the ocean carbon budget and understanding how it is responding 2094
[pdf-7902ce1e60bf0f9751 | p.70] to anthropogenic forcing is a major goal in climate research. It is widely accepted 2095
[pdf-ab52e12755c3da2564 | p.70] that the ocean has absorbed around a quarter of CO2 emissions released anthro- 2096
[pdf-e8e8f9a23433afc4bf | p.70] pogenically, and that the ocean uptake of carbon has increased in proportion to 2097
[pdf-65586331ab5e699f8f | p.70] increasing CO2 emissions (Aricò et al., 2021). Yet, our understanding of the pools 2098
[pdf-d44204dd49f501ca70 | p.70] of carbon in the ocean, the processes that modulate them, and how they interact 2099
[pdf-b2e7872146512fee07 | p.70] with the land and atmosphere, is not satisfactory enough to make conﬁdent predic- 2100
[pdf-62c9c473b222d844ba | p.70] tions of how the ocean carbon budget is changing. Improving our understanding 2101
[pdf-ae2263aaa5ecb10e7f | p.70] requires a holistic and integrated approach to ocean carbon cycle research, with 2102
[pdf-d3f139869557271a6c | p.70] monitoring systems capable of ﬁlling the gaps in our understanding (Aricò et al., 2103
[pdf-a8760088b942e34a84 | p.70] 2021). Satellites can play a major role in this (Shutler et al., 2019). 2104
[pdf-0761c5851b688d5271 | p.70] 70

## p.71 — Page 71

[pdf-2fe9211a286e2e95bb | p.71] 4.3.1. State of the art in Carbon Budget Closure 2105
[pdf-d977025b5820f8705a | p.71] Each year, the international Global Carbon project produces a budget of 2106
[pdf-a216626f3aa21331fc | p.71] the Earth’s carbon cycle (https://www.globalcarbonproject.org/about/index.htm), 2107
[pdf-49901f1cfdf8f505ee | p.71] based on a combination of models and observations. In a recent report (Friedling- 2108
[pdf-b4b53e59f725130596 | p.71] stein et al., 2022), for the year 2020, and for a total anthropogenic CO2 emission of 2109
[pdf-6aa643e74affd4d025 | p.71] 10.2 Gt C y−1 (±0.8 Gt C y−1), the oceans were found to absorb 3.0 Gt C y−1 (±0.4 2110
[pdf-eec8d639a375b6e418 | p.71] Gt C y−1), similar to that of the land at 2.9 Gt C y−1 (±1.0 Gt C y−1). Building on 2111
[pdf-6a7c142e18f29a0e96 | p.71] earlier reports (e.g., Hauck et al., 2020), this latest report highlighted an increasing 2112
[pdf-ad6be8bcd6701aa94a | p.71] divergence, in the order of 1.0 Gt C y−1, between diﬀerent methods, on the strength 2113
[pdf-089dde86ec263eb55f | p.71] of the ocean sink over the last decade (Friedlingstein et al., 2022), with models 2114
[pdf-925ef9ff79573441cd | p.71] reporting a smaller sink than observation-based data-products (acknowledging 2115
[pdf-5efb7d78bc6b8cf533 | p.71] that observation-based data-products are heavily extrapolated). Results from 2116
[pdf-9dfffc503c672e0c49 | p.71] this report suggest our ability to predict the ocean sink could be deteriorating. 2117
[pdf-c2c306d433ed63454a | p.71] Understanding the causes of this discrepancy is undoubtedly a major challenge. 2118
[pdf-a5134e369ee27e4570 | p.71] Possible causes include: uncertainty in the river ﬂux adjustment that needs to be 2119
[pdf-121a68654d0e9566ec | p.71] added to the data-products in order to account for diﬀerent ﬂux components being 2120
[pdf-1a9557b607bd1c096d | p.71] represented in models and data-products; data sparsity; methodological issues in 2121
[pdf-92d2df6853adbd1d2e | p.71] the mapping of methods used in data-products; underestimation of wind speeds in 2122
[pdf-799ff59557ec46be05 | p.71] the climate reanalyses (Verezemskaya et al., 2017), model physics biases; possible 2123
[pdf-57a9303cc8ed860313 | p.71] issues in air-sea gas exchange calculations; and underestimation of the role of 2124
[pdf-81cefee50b95d77b54 | p.71] biology in air-sea gas exchange. Or possibly some compound eﬀects of these 2125
[pdf-e7982e9cf896a3592f | p.71] causes. 2126
[pdf-3617d0fa5f1cc6ef33 | p.71] It is clear satellite data can help in addressing this issue. For example, through 2127
[pdf-e6e3bec0c2fc0606c2 | p.71] assimilation of physical data (temperature, salinity, altimeter) into high resolution 2128
[pdf-e97b63bd573a887389 | p.71] physical models, to improve model physics (e.g., Verdy and Mazloﬀ, 2017; Carroll 2129
[pdf-f2cd8bc4a6f3e72e6e | p.71] et al., 2020) or ocean colour data assimilation to improve the representation of 2130
[pdf-768aa40b4d64aa06d3 | p.71] biology (e.g., Gregg, 2001, 2008; Rousseaux and Gregg, 2015; Gregg et al., 2017; 2131
[pdf-334c38c2384c61f3e0 | p.71] Ciavatta et al., 2018; Skákala et al., 2018). A recent budget analysis using ECCO- 2132
[pdf-757a09b7efaa821649 | p.71] Darwin successfully managed to close the global carbon budget "gap” between 2133
[pdf-a6f7eb1ac1369b2b60 | p.71] observation-based products and biogeochemical models (see Carroll et al., 2022). 2134
[pdf-c6798e1de606d6d1d3 | p.71] Other ways satellites could help include: by improving observation-based data- 2135
[pdf-beba56663fbdd8510e | p.71] 71

## p.72 — Page 72

[pdf-111ebbc27ab0985821 | p.72] products (e.g. using direct SST skin measurements Watson et al., 2020), through 2136
[pdf-9334286582b8f8fff5 | p.72] improved estimates or river-induced carbon outgassing and deposition in the 2137
[pdf-85c5bb92ea3a7b7920 | p.72] sediments, and even through better understanding of the way ocean biology is 2138
[pdf-c8a22705b0e482a151 | p.72] responding to climate (Kulk et al., 2020; Li et al., 2021; Tang et al., 2021; Wang 2139
[pdf-52cc50f72a8d33cfb3 | p.72] et al., 2022). On this latter point, whereas it is accepted that biology is critical 2140
[pdf-0657b2172e057d17bb | p.72] to maintaining the surface to depth gradient of DIC (estimated to be responsible 2141
[pdf-8d6a0513b94767e19a | p.72] for around 70 % of it; Sarmiento and Gruber, 2006), the role of biology in ocean 2142
[pdf-05d08e2861351c8125 | p.72] anthropogenic CO2 update has been thought to be minor, based on a lack of 2143
[pdf-97426ed874e65053b3 | p.72] evidence that the biological carbon pump has changed over the recent (industrial) 2144
[pdf-4dce27c7b1cff25bc2 | p.72] period, or that any change is suﬃcient to impact anthropogenic CO2 uptake. An 2145
[pdf-c1e2aac1b111b4823c | p.72] assumption that is now being challenged. It has been shown in ocean models 2146
[pdf-deedaf6d217d9fd4a5 | p.72] that with a future reduced buﬀer factor, the CO2 uptake may increase during 2147
[pdf-cb6fcb04b3127ea195 | p.72] the phytoplankton growth season (Hauck and Völker, 2015). This ‘seasonal 2148
[pdf-6a3dc0e3a5e6d6064b | p.72] ocean carbon cycle feedback’ leads to an increase of ocean carbon uptake by 8 % 2149
[pdf-856ad79a5f8345a11e | p.72] globally in a high-emission scenario RCP8.5 by 2100 (Fassbender et al., 2022). 2150
[pdf-5f660806707d38b89a | p.72] Increasing amplitudes of the seasonal cycle of pCO2 can already be determined 2151
[pdf-05fff7f130169008f0 | p.72] in pCO2-based data-products (Landschützer et al., 2018). 2152
[pdf-13e2196ddcc24f8e2d | p.72] Satellite ocean carbon products have expanded in recent years (CEOS, 2014; 2153
[pdf-248f20f4f361e7aa05 | p.72] Brewin et al., 2021), to the point where some satellite-based carbon budgets maybe 2154
[pdf-56472ce3fd93000f2b | p.72] feasible in the surface mixed layer. For example, we are now in a position to use 2155
[pdf-de8c4586eb7692de00 | p.72] satellite data to improve our understanding of how organic carbon is partitioned 2156
[pdf-9804e898f00e1abc40 | p.72] into particulate carbon and dissolved carbon (DOC), how particulate carbon (PC) 2157
[pdf-2cf801a11f9ec66753 | p.72] is partitioned into organic (POC) and inorganic (PIC) contributions (PC = PIC 2158
[pdf-7c8337b9c752c94b39 | p.72] + POC), how POC is partitioned into algal (C-phyto) and non-algal portions, 2159
[pdf-696f22e2281310f10b | p.72] and the relationships between phytoplankton carbon (C-phyto) and PP (and net 2160
[pdf-579032ed3a47d3686c | p.72] community production), which can give information on turnover times for marine 2161
[pdf-11c0062c438fff1f86 | p.72] phytoplankton. Considering the continuous ocean-colour record started in 1997, 2162
[pdf-9653bba2690917c439 | p.72] we can begin to develop an understanding how these budgets are changing. This 2163
[pdf-46d04a4a0b79f08c7e | p.72] could be extremely useful for evaluating models. 2164
[pdf-b1c4f8c138380ad924 | p.72] Notwithstanding the potential and use of satellite-based carbon budgets, many 2165
[pdf-7e1f9d3d6b743ab7f7 | p.72] carbon pools and ﬂuxes are still not amenable from satellite remote sensing, 2166
[pdf-aa2a854f17ae140977 | p.72] 72

## p.73 — Page 73

[pdf-2aefaad75841cc8f47 | p.73] that satellite ocean observations are limited to the surface ocean, to cloud-free 2167
[pdf-7f456eb54baf097aa0 | p.73] conditions and low to moderate sun-zenith angles (for some systems), have diﬃ- 2168
[pdf-dd8f9426cad3523870 | p.73] culties in coastal regions, and in spatial and temporal resolution. Thus to quantify 2169
[pdf-988b679395c38de5dd | p.73] ocean carbon budgets, an integrated approach is required, combining satellite 2170
[pdf-d0d88b5e9ab6fa6df8 | p.73] data with other observations (in situ) and with models. A nice demonstration 2171
[pdf-b8fa37ee65d7d77a09 | p.73] of this is a recent study by Nowicki et al. (2022), who assimilated satellite and 2172
[pdf-dd4e05c122455ef389 | p.73] in-situ data into an ensemble numerical model of the ocean’s biological carbon 2173
[pdf-36a5559475dd7725c6 | p.73] pump, to quantify global and regional carbon export and sequestration, and the 2174
[pdf-7a290389e6f2e10df2 | p.73] contributions from three key pathways to export: gravitational sinking of particles, 2175
[pdf-c8199461fba5cf875f | p.73] vertical migration of organisms, and physical mixing of organic material. Their 2176
[pdf-cb267d40139957a131 | p.73] analysis demonstrated large regional variations in the export of organic carbon, 2177
[pdf-e0b3b5e4c3cffe88b3 | p.73] the pathways that control export, and the sequestration timescales of the export. 2178
[pdf-ee27eb000cfba3248e | p.73] It also suggested ocean carbon storage will weaken as the oceans stratify, and the 2179
[pdf-0ea28c5c0ef607d92d | p.73] subtropical gyres expand due to anthropogenic climate change. It is, perhaps, that 2180
[pdf-f1f320bf20095dd9be | p.73] mechanisms thought to be understood decades ago about the ocean biological 2181
[pdf-faaa7510a3e06adf67 | p.73] carbon pump have already evolved with climate change. 2182
[pdf-6e14721e1b6e8fc7da | p.73] Three priorities were identiﬁed at the workshop in relation to carbon budget 2183
[pdf-e6319665a42771cdf8 | p.73] closure (CBC). These are summarised in Table 10 and include: 1) in-situ data; 2184
[pdf-3d6a6250ba258d6f65 | p.73] 2) satellite algorithms, budgets and uncertainties; and 3) model and satellite 2185
[pdf-5accbf29f9c9fb98a6 | p.73] integration. 2186
[pdf-b2a572a5c3944733ec | p.73] 4.3.2. CBC priority 1: In-situ data 2187
[pdf-68f398638c06ff66ef | p.73] Challenges: As emphasised throughout previous sections, in-situ data are 2188
[pdf-4728640f89d2b0a14f | p.73] central to algorithm development and validation of ocean carbon products. Some 2189
[pdf-2b2bbb38f46c264f45 | p.73] carbon pools and ﬂuxes are easier to measure in situ than others. Consequently, 2190
[pdf-20fc30fdc6c72146a6 | p.73] the quality, quantity and spatial distribution of in-situ measurements vary de- 2191
[pdf-e6de29599a2d5d2442 | p.73] pending on the pool or ﬂux being studied. This makes it challenging for budget 2192
[pdf-4a1c015c2e999ae325 | p.73] computations. 2193
[pdf-466dad03cc3140e71e | p.73] Gaps: Very few, if any, datasets exist (or are accessible) on concurrent and co- 2194
[pdf-2a104c288e85932ce2 | p.73] located in-situ measurements of all the key pools and ﬂuxes required to evaluate 2195
[pdf-6d722a1d7521b95df1 | p.73] satellite or model budgets. Some remote regions that are thought to play a critical 2196
[pdf-efd419e09de2e983a3 | p.73] role in global budgets, such as the Southern Ocean, are severely under-sampled. 2197
[pdf-a0616ca6d5391ff793 | p.73] 73

## p.74 — Page 74

[pdf-fb4f9eb6b275d2fade | p.74] There are gaps in some key measurements in many regions (e.g., for organic 2198
[pdf-2a01426185c6b060bf | p.74] carbon budgets, photosynthesis irradiance parameters, see Bouman et al., 2018; 2199
[pdf-18f6421894a5ef9ec0 | p.74] Sathyendranath et al., 2020). 2200
[pdf-ed36ad9f74b9384c1b | p.74] Opportunities: As technology develops, improved methods are being devel- 2201
[pdf-bd887a582f37881de1 | p.74] oped to measure pools and ﬂuxes of carbon in the ocean. Some of these methods 2202
[pdf-63268dc8ad57e0e827 | p.74] (e.g., Williams et al., 2017; Estapa et al., 2017; Bresnahan et al., 2017; Sutton 2203
[pdf-e1be0fdc886c1691ad | p.74] et al., 2021; Bishop et al., 2022) have the potential to be (or have already been) 2204
[pdf-451b90d486ea2c7d86 | p.74] integrated into networks of autonomous platforms, such as gliders and BGC-Argo 2205
[pdf-8265adbfa25b8cf877 | p.74] ﬂoats. New methods are also being developed to quantify carbon pools and 2206
[pdf-702e1be756920feec9 | p.74] ﬂuxes from standard biogeochemical measurements on autonomous platforms 2207
[pdf-3af8d7d1b244202b8a | p.74] (e.g., Dall’Olmo et al., 2016; Claustre et al., 2020; Giering et al., 2020; Claustre 2208
[pdf-c4387425089442d7ff | p.74] et al., 2021; Johnson and Bif, 2021). As in-situ data grow with time, it is feasible 2209
[pdf-b3521428f268f3724f | p.74] to quantify properties of carbon budgets from in-situ compilations that can be 2210
[pdf-db734069947b21b194 | p.74] used to check and constrain satellite or model budgets. For example, empirical 2211
[pdf-be60db4d3c803fdbf0 | p.74] relationships among POC, C-phyto, and Chl-a (Sathyendranath et al., 2009), have 2212
[pdf-2371a579b1636a2e43 | p.74] proven useful in model evaluations of emergent carbon budgets (de Mora et al., 2213
[pdf-771d00f932ac5b06fa | p.74] 2016). 2214
[pdf-295cbddb3962d09ac7 | p.74] 4.3.3. CBC priority 2: Satellite algorithms, budgets and uncertainties 2215
[pdf-55b1277766e439c344 | p.74] Challenges: When closing the ocean carbon budget, it is critical that there is 2216
[pdf-34a3cbabe6bd614404 | p.74] coherence in the satellite data ﬁelds we input into the diﬀerent satellite algorithms, 2217
[pdf-5f377f651eefcb92f1 | p.74] and that uncertainties are available for model propagation. Additionally, and as 2218
[pdf-bc7df60f9ccf792382 | p.74] identiﬁed in previous sections, some of the pools and ﬂuxes of carbon require 2219
[pdf-4dd59cb2df3977ac67 | p.74] satellite data with higher spatial, temporal, and spectral resolution. There is a 2220
[pdf-470da1a250bbc5a650 | p.74] need for consistency in algorithms used to quantify budgets (see Sathyendranath 2221
[pdf-441742a6bf40f10bec | p.74] et al., 2020), and these algorithms must respect properties of the ecosystem known 2222
[pdf-6718d71ec677c52f6e | p.74] from in-situ data. 2223
[pdf-52a6f2f908da0c8fea | p.74] In the context of quantifying the ocean carbon budget, the pools and ﬂuxes 2224
[pdf-4ddeb4aca33a713174 | p.74] have to ﬁt together in a consistent way. Therefore, it is important to not only 2225
[pdf-9031f4c15e4cf564e6 | p.74] consider the uncertainties in individual products, but to analyse uncertainties in 2226
[pdf-c4776c69197eba92a1 | p.74] multiple products to identify any discrepancies. This requires that we analyse 2227
[pdf-c80d3fa325c388bde7 | p.74] each of the products in relation to all the other products and see whether they 2228
[pdf-92f3256dea623df420 | p.74] 74

## p.75 — Page 75

[pdf-670b4b884d3d7cbfc2 | p.75] hold together in a coherent fashion. These checks can also help to constrain those 2229
[pdf-bd5d132d2cb2d6bb52 | p.75] components which are impossible to observe or that are more uncertain. 2230
[pdf-9348a0058bd7a498a7 | p.75] Gaps: Many satellite carbon products lack associated estimates of uncertainty. 2231
[pdf-b440a311101c7fbba1 | p.75] The uncertainties for individual products are also needed when combining mul- 2232
[pdf-7fb12474f8d767e528 | p.75] tiple products to assess carbon budgets. Considering the importance of model 2233
[pdf-12570f33a3d303c177 | p.75] parameters in satellite algorithms, more work is needed to improve estimates of 2234
[pdf-69c56ee99636a0dbda | p.75] uncertainties in model parameters and look towards dynamic, rather than static, 2235
[pdf-5bde16a3b9065b756e | p.75] assignment of parameters in carbon algorithms. From an Earth system perspective, 2236
[pdf-4c6e7c92b29a769d26 | p.75] increasing emphasis needs to be placed on harmonising satellite carbon products 2237
[pdf-b531aac40a6043d297 | p.75] across diﬀerent planetary domains, and evaluating the impact of using diﬀerent 2238
[pdf-3743bdecb4a702fc41 | p.75] input climate data records. 2239
[pdf-097785300e94d78d77 | p.75] Opportunities: With the development of consistent and stable climate data 2240
[pdf-8efdc57a8f8f8674ea | p.75] records, with associated estimates of uncertainty (e.g., ESA CCI), we are now 2241
[pdf-56c8468c474f4f8838 | p.75] in a good position to utilise coherent satellite data ﬁelds as input to ocean car- 2242
[pdf-c5a7c1ae8c96008155 | p.75] bon algorithms. The development of new satellite sensors, with higher spatial, 2243
[pdf-41e13ae307bb542f75 | p.75] temporal and spectral resolution, will lead to improved satellite algorithms and 2244
[pdf-fd6710b1feb068c9ac | p.75] more conﬁdent carbon budgets. New approaches and statistical techniques (e.g., 2245
[pdf-0d6f72e4225864ba9b | p.75] machine learning) are becoming available, and oﬀer potential to get at pools and 2246
[pdf-12cfc51bb7746b1878 | p.75] ﬂuxes of carbon from satellite that were previously not feasible to monitor from 2247
[pdf-561420609a9e4f7141 | p.75] space. 2248
[pdf-bcc679f77a94f5c379 | p.75] 4.3.4. CBC priority 3: Model and satellite integration 2249
[pdf-80c9124b7152b12bb7 | p.75] Challenges: A major challenge in bringing satellite observations together 2250
[pdf-79b73d98e14d15e1cf | p.75] with models, is dealing with the contrasting spatial scales in the two types of 2251
[pdf-7ce40b5fa1d46f551c | p.75] datasets. Quantifying carbon budgets through data integration also requires 2252
[pdf-4e7118d5e6484ec013 | p.75] appreciation of the diﬀerent temporal scales that the pools and ﬂuxes operate 2253
[pdf-08acacdefcca188a5e | p.75] on. This is particularly true from an Earth system approach, considering the 2254
[pdf-86ea281ce2675382b0 | p.75] timescales of carbon cycling diﬀer among the ocean, land and atmosphere. 2255
[pdf-83bd27af60dbd60dec | p.75] Gaps: Successful integration of satellite carbon products with models requires 2256
[pdf-255a6d85a52cab2fec | p.75] accurate uncertainties in the satellite observations and model simulations. These 2257
[pdf-5c1ecdba31316956ed | p.75] are often not available. Greater emphasis is needed on model diversity, which 2258
[pdf-f1acdb030664cef2e4 | p.75] should help increase conﬁdence in carbon budgets and improve understanding. 2259
[pdf-ca02a4422973333693 | p.75] 75

## p.76 — Page 76

[pdf-4272773a6848a285af | p.76] Opportunities: There are opportunities to harness new developments in data 2260
[pdf-c31a5b3be2711f7d6c | p.76] assimilation to help constrain carbon budgets, through the use of new satellite 2261
[pdf-33475fd5a4229d9698 | p.76] biological products (e.g. community structure, Ciavatta et al., 2018; Skákala et al., 2262
[pdf-d7f7af505e2667a873 | p.76] 2018) and advancements in optical modules for autonomous platforms (Terzi´c 2263
[pdf-13dc07e40c736d5828 | p.76] et al., 2019, 2021), or through combined physical and biological data assimilation 2264
[pdf-191681e9a7a3800721 | p.76] (Song et al., 2016; IOCCG, 2020). There is scope to harness developments in ma- 2265
[pdf-ccfe2a7df671bf194c | p.76] chine learning to help combine data and models, for example, bridging diﬀerent 2266
[pdf-420ad8c8e3c11df010 | p.76] spatial scales in the satellite and model products. Future enhancements in com- 2267
[pdf-7a6a260d7552458044 | p.76] putation power (e.g., quantum computing) should lead to better representations 2268
[pdf-aac343f71f2d2c9071 | p.76] of spatial scales in models (e.g., sub-mesoscale processes), improving carbon 2269
[pdf-676a1e2297ff8c757c | p.76] budgets. 2270
[pdf-adb4be985ec310b837 | p.76] 5. Common themes 2271
[pdf-2f7089cc41e53bcfb7 | p.76] Figure 2 shows a word cloud produced using all the priorities identiﬁed across 2272
[pdf-605f9f704568ef032f | p.76] the nine themes of the workshop. It illustrates the dominant themes and subthemes 2273
[pdf-32409bee435d51755a | p.76] emerging from all priorities identiﬁed. Commonalities among the nine themes of 2274
[pdf-afb600d6c04d44e034 | p.76] the workshop, include: 2275
[pdf-ddc5082c17beed19c0 | p.76] • In-situ data. It is strikingly clear from this analysis the importance of 2276
[pdf-6e4319b93a18fa06a1 | p.76] in-situ data, for algorithm development and validation, for extrapolation 2277
[pdf-c7139dd9d171faf1ef | p.76] of surface satellite ﬁelds to depth, for parametrisation and validation of 2278
[pdf-52865d4484ce151236 | p.76] ESMs, and for constraining estimates of the carbon budget. It is critical 2279
[pdf-40919aa4a6c07f6109 | p.76] that the international community continues investing in the collection of 2280
[pdf-fee5eed996e6c9a823 | p.76] in-situ data, in better data protocols and standards, community-agreed upon 2281
[pdf-ce836494d0b579cc39 | p.76] data structure and metadata, more intercomparison and intercalibration 2282
[pdf-98d8da89dee4bae061 | p.76] exercises, the development of new in-situ methods for measurement of 2283
[pdf-5497b26109175be30d | p.76] carbon, and in the expanding networks of autonomous observations, that 2284
[pdf-5c6a05b54d2cb8fc30 | p.76] have the potential to radically improve the spatial and temporal coverage of 2285
[pdf-1edf36f4a391e56f1b | p.76] in-situ data. There are clear challenges with respect to compiling large in- 2286
[pdf-0a8059474499e0d199 | p.76] situ datasets from diﬀerent sources, using diﬀerent methods and protocols, 2287
[pdf-61e245438343414981 | p.76] for algorithm development and validation, that need to be addressed. It is 2288
[pdf-f05ba58250c8e6de5f | p.76] 76

## p.77 — Page 77

[pdf-14639dd71b76d0f459 | p.77] important that the in-situ, satellite and modelling community communicates 2289
[pdf-3c4646fd3666e5b080 | p.77] prior to collecting data, to ensure the data collected will be useful for the 2290
[pdf-aff9d2eb80f9f8ec70 | p.77] entire community. 2291
[pdf-46a4e2ea4adf07cb89 | p.77] • Satellite algorithm retrievals. For all pools and ﬂuxes of carbon, contin- 2292
[pdf-f56bb388b2a095a83d | p.77] ued development of satellite algorithms and retrieval techniques is critical 2293
[pdf-b11d5d4ee7ca1bf1e4 | p.77] to maximise the use of satellite data in carbon research. New satellites 2294
[pdf-7c97fb07816acd4641 | p.77] are being launched in the near future, with new capabilities and improved 2295
[pdf-7f0c42309d705d776b | p.77] spatial, temporal and spectral resolution (see Table 2). Micro- and nano- 2296
[pdf-278ea656afa17bea4d | p.77] satellites (CubeSats; Schueler and Holmes, 2016; Vanhellemont, 2019) 2297
[pdf-901c50577b298c018f | p.77] have potential to be launched cheaply into low Earth orbit, in large swarms 2298
[pdf-7edce806bccd6fefba | p.77] improving spatial and temporal coverage. New advanced statistical methods 2299
[pdf-e35bf4d11c51fabc46 | p.77] are emerging (e.g., advancements in artiﬁcial intelligence). New satellite 2300
[pdf-7a5b91068c0589db77 | p.77] data records are appearing, that will provide the much-needed coherence for 2301
[pdf-70e4b6944a28943391 | p.77] input to multiple satellite carbon algorithms for budget calculations. Over 2302
[pdf-3c9f48e2a826d4f83e | p.77] the coming decades existing missions like Sentinel-3 OLCI, Sentinel-2 MSI 2303
[pdf-fc9ecb368709f166c9 | p.77] and VIIRS, will provide better carbon products with real operational usage. 2304
[pdf-298e2f3d2123807b03 | p.77] Our community needs to be positioned to harness these opportunities. Satel- 2305
[pdf-d6870290cead2215ac | p.77] lite retrievals of carbon products critically rely on accurate atmospheric 2306
[pdf-92d89a73048035abc2 | p.77] correction, and there are challenges around developing new atmospheric 2307
[pdf-2982c33a53a38933a4 | p.77] correction schemes for emerging sensors (Table 2). Additionally, contin- 2308
[pdf-006e6772ad70230405 | p.77] ued investment is required into basic and mechanistic understanding of 2309
[pdf-57d186e75df56f59de | p.77] the retrieval process, and improvements in retrievals in coastal and shelf 2310
[pdf-7bb55f0a41296efbf9 | p.77] sea environments and other optically complex waters, which is crucial for 2311
[pdf-b5fc1e600a53a7a19b | p.77] monitoring trends in satellite-based carbon products (e.g., Sathyendranath 2312
[pdf-293f4c363d68aba6e7 | p.77] et al., 2017b). 2313
[pdf-aa297f145cfc6e5812 | p.77] • Uncertainty in data. There is a clear requirement across all themes to 2314
[pdf-163d5d626fdf7b49cc | p.77] provide uncertainty estimates with satellite, in-situ and model products. 2315
[pdf-c799759a04feecbf00 | p.77] Continued investment in methods to quantify uncertainty is vital for quanti- 2316
[pdf-3304225e3f2531df53 | p.77] fying carbon budgets and change (IOCCG, 2019; McKinna et al., 2019). 2317
[pdf-aa2ba4cb6db24741e9 | p.77] 77

## p.78 — Page 78

[pdf-9652632fdda5b15874 | p.78] • Vertical distributions. One of the major limitations of satellites, is that 2318
[pdf-7d7faf5f033e834942 | p.78] they only view the surface layer of the ocean. Sub-surface measurements 2319
[pdf-ce875a762f037c9079 | p.78] are required to extrapolate the surface ﬁelds to depth. Synergy among 2320
[pdf-353f246440805868b5 | p.78] satellite surface passive ﬁelds, satellite active-based sensors (e.g. lidar) 2321
[pdf-08794a1088266c1771 | p.78] that can penetrate further into the water column (Jamet et al., 2019), and 2322
[pdf-a4fffd0a39633d1d44 | p.78] the expanding networks of autonomous and in-situ observations, that are 2323
[pdf-a82353d880e0cde9e8 | p.78] viewing the subsurface with ever-increasing coverage, for example, the 2324
[pdf-9daaed8ebcbc3985bd | p.78] global network of BGC-Argo ﬂoats (Roemmich et al., 2019; Claustre et al., 2325
[pdf-7cbbf1d9f907685c42 | p.78] 2020) and Bio-GO-SHIP (https://biogoship.org), is a clear focus for future 2326
[pdf-ce3ecf803a45b7445b | p.78] ocean carbon research. 2327
[pdf-757aca644f1e709cb0 | p.78] • Ocean models. Many components of the ocean carbon cycle are not di- 2328
[pdf-dd7ba0c2f60a68752f | p.78] rectly observable through satellite, and some are even inherently diﬃcult 2329
[pdf-f774762215d4ec91e9 | p.78] or expensive to measure in situ. To target these hidden pools and ﬂuxes 2330
[pdf-7de47df796562f5464 | p.78] we must turn to models. Models can also help tackle the low temporal 2331
[pdf-8bd27940970cedfe94 | p.78] and spatial resolution of in situ data and issues around gaps in satellite 2332
[pdf-2390849540f51c6dbe | p.78] data. Exploring synergy between satellite observations and models is clear 2333
[pdf-d2db03c79c52c0f33d | p.78] priority for future ocean carbon research (IOCCG, 2020). New develop- 2334
[pdf-7f0a157fb8b135337e | p.78] ments in data assimilation may help (not only satellites, but growing data 2335
[pdf-4ea7953ad0a0fc80b7 | p.78] sources from autonomous platforms), and integration of radiative transfer 2336
[pdf-4cf82230620846bf42 | p.78] into models, such that the models themselves become capable of simulat- 2337
[pdf-70a05fd60573e71b52 | p.78] ing ﬁelds of electromagnetic energy (e.g., Jones et al., 2016; Gregg and 2338
[pdf-4e26ad1b1b80eab38e | p.78] Rousseaux, 2017; Dutkiewicz et al., 2018, 2019; Terzi´c et al., 2019, 2021). 2339
[pdf-4de3f307b6678dee69 | p.78] We must continue to identify processes poorly represented in models, that 2340
[pdf-8671d63eb5a94c15af | p.78] can be subsequently improved in future model design. Observing System 2341
[pdf-55b26f0906438528d0 | p.78] Simulation Experiments (OSSE) can be used to evaluate the impact of 2342
[pdf-d4f28f5f4e802afede | p.78] under sampled observing systems on obtained results, or evaluate the value 2343
[pdf-333ca666a68a78a0d4 | p.78] of new observing systems design for optimal sampling strategies. 2344
[pdf-9c105b09c1ef60dfc7 | p.78] • Integration of data. It is challenging to ﬁnd an optimal way of combining 2345
[pdf-48a5f84a8931166a61 | p.78] satellites, models and in-situ observations, to produce best-quality data 2346
[pdf-c6a0533bf998e15995 | p.78] products. Integrated carbon products are required for near real-time fore- 2347
[pdf-a7db2af165e8f3c369 | p.78] 78

## p.79 — Page 79

[pdf-db285d28479828411f | p.79] casting of the biogeochemical ocean carbon cycle. Additionally, they are 2348
[pdf-8e2c5437d960cfa16b | p.79] required for regional or global impact assessments, to assess the multiple 2349
[pdf-3814a366266a6027ba | p.79] stressors (e.g., temperature change, ocean acidiﬁcation) acting upon the 2350
[pdf-25ca1fac2186137560 | p.79] marine ecosystem, and subsequent downstream eﬀects on the carbon cycle 2351
[pdf-9d37e680605bf94f60 | p.79] (e.g., natural food web, ﬁsheries, etc.). Continued eﬀorts are required to 2352
[pdf-dd5e80a040460df812 | p.79] develop methods and strategies to bridge the spatial and temporal scales 2353
[pdf-5cbc624fbdb3d3fc15 | p.79] of the diﬀerent datasets (Cronin et al., 2022), and statistical methods like 2354
[pdf-463786ece74ef42b12 | p.79] machine learning may help in this regard. 2355
[pdf-3cbc7bd87aef9db858 | p.79] • Fundamental Understanding. Continued investment is required into im- 2356
[pdf-816ce8db5cb627f83a | p.79] proving our fundamental understanding of the ocean carbon cycle, and on 2357
[pdf-06615a60289e53fdb0 | p.79] the interaction between pools of carbon and light. The latter is critical for 2358
[pdf-07e305a31475e2036b | p.79] the development of satellite carbon products. For example, there remains 2359
[pdf-c76f4a501c5ae8b211 | p.79] fundamental gaps in our understanding of controls on carbon cycling in the 2360
[pdf-8cf7fda35c7dba35ff | p.79] ocean by viruses and other microbes (Middelboe and Lyck, 2002; Worden 2361
[pdf-c804cfa7d5160af031 | p.79] et al., 2015). 2362
[pdf-5dbc9fe05b97636639 | p.79] 6. Emerging concerns and broader thoughts 2363
[pdf-44d198dddc536b33a6 | p.79] In addition to the common themes, during workshop discussions, other emerg- 2364
[pdf-649b1bb51e4bf6b60f | p.79] ing concerns and broader thoughts materialised, including: 2365
[pdf-fbcbfdb38a8c524c15 | p.79] • Bringing carbon communities together. Considering the need to take a 2366
[pdf-d7026a6b18fa01915f | p.79] holistic, integrated approach to ocean carbon science (Aricò et al., 2021; 2367
[pdf-ab0713e37b20076cbc | p.79] Cronin et al., 2022), there is a strong requirement to bring diﬀerent com- 2368
[pdf-07c06a476c4497af7f | p.79] munities together working on diﬀerent aspects of the ocean carbon cycle, 2369
[pdf-9224b7f7666bb7af0b | p.79] that can often operate in a disparate fashion, including those working in 2370
[pdf-bd5a3c1bffb6d4d6f2 | p.79] diﬀerent zones of the ocean (e.g., pelagic, mesopelagic, bathypelagic and 2371
[pdf-3597dd5a9add35afce | p.79] abyssopelagic), on the inorganic and organic sides, ﬁeld and laboratory sci- 2372
[pdf-4de9384a028e8f0b10 | p.79] entists, remote sensing scientists and modellers. Furthermore, and taking an 2373
[pdf-e014250854d1ebc813 | p.79] Earth system view, this should also be extended to those working on carbon 2374
[pdf-4762fe17062f928909 | p.79] in other planetary domains (Campbell et al., 2022). We need to improve 2375
[pdf-275763075cf8c8eb58 | p.79] our understanding of the connectivity between coastal and open-ocean 2376
[pdf-4dd9ac8cce86f1eceb | p.79] 79

## p.80 — Page 80

[pdf-1ce4c78f6c957dc032 | p.80] ecosystems, for example, the potential impact of (large) rivers on oceanic 2377
[pdf-a5361d476c71653380 | p.80] carbon dynamics. A good example is the Observing Air-Sea Interactions 2378
[pdf-e95c7806255902b34e | p.80] Strategy (OASIS), a UN Ocean Decade-endorsed program that has brought 2379
[pdf-1c0014c1381a468c0b | p.80] together the carbon community to consolidate three interlinked grand ideas 2380
[pdf-b29fcb596331891cbe | p.80] centred around: the building of a global in-situ air-sea observing network; 2381
[pdf-179d2cbe579c92aa8c | p.80] the creation of a high temporal and spatial resolution satellite network for 2382
[pdf-d5e055b2fbdb6b8731 | p.80] measuring air–sea ﬂuxes; leading to improved models and understanding 2383
[pdf-184249a4ce8dd07a4f | p.80] of air–sea interaction processes (Cronin et al., 2022). 2384
[pdf-72a61440568c470388 | p.80] • The need to maximise use of limited resources. Current funding levels 2385
[pdf-9b55a8fb71464cb33f | p.80] make it challenging to support adequate monitoring of core ocean carbon 2386
[pdf-e0777d839d74d3f6bb | p.80] variables in addition to supporting innovative blue skies science. Increasing 2387
[pdf-09c92d49c9f7a432f4 | p.80] overall funding and separating the funding pots for the two activities could 2388
[pdf-e46688b6e875277ee4 | p.80] help to maximise monitoring and achieve key priorities for blue skies 2389
[pdf-4781a5d9f430e6d701 | p.80] research. 2390
[pdf-0edbac0d67a46039d6 | p.80] • Improved distribution of satellite and model carbon products. Al- 2391
[pdf-3557e754c12a099b33 | p.80] though satellite-based carbon products are becoming available, more em- 2392
[pdf-09c41a37f2d12576f5 | p.80] phasis is needed to integrate satellite carbon products, as well as model 2393
[pdf-7bbed0ce4f68f1c841 | p.80] products, into operational satellite services to ensure end-user access, and 2394
[pdf-2169aba611ff87ed0a | p.80] make products more user friendly. This requires close dialogue with the 2395
[pdf-837dd7907b85971fad | p.80] user communities. 2396
[pdf-47ba62b841269ee409 | p.80] • Working with satellite carbon experts in diﬀerent planetary domains. 2397
[pdf-93b70721d060ddbb1a | p.80] More emphasis should be placed on harmonising satellite carbon products 2398
[pdf-4ec03a12effee4065f | p.80] across diﬀerent planetary domains (ocean, land, ice and air). This involves 2399
[pdf-3e7e6a434be45ea4e8 | p.80] working closer with scientiﬁc communities working in the diﬀerent spheres 2400
[pdf-fdf63072cc1318092b | p.80] of the planet (Earth System approach). 2401
[pdf-af5567872d3a4c41cd | p.80] • Carbon and environmental footprints of research.. Our communities 2402
[pdf-bdea6864fdea20d19c | p.80] need to start taking more responsibility to monitor and minimise the carbon 2403
[pdf-7bd759de395009c513 | p.80] and environmental footprints of scientiﬁc research, and improve how this is 2404
[pdf-e75f5308786b17eafc | p.80] managed and controlled (e.g., Achten et al., 2013; Shutler, 2020). Greater 2405
[pdf-42b4d75272d4690526 | p.80] 80

## p.81 — Page 81

[pdf-9be37cb101906b9a56 | p.81] stewardship is needed to document and track the carbon and environmental 2406
[pdf-d14834758f5f4809c9 | p.81] footprints of researchers, ideally within a transparent and traceable frame- 2407
[pdf-3441aff6c69bd2569c | p.81] work (e.g., Mariette et al., 2021). The beneﬁts of the priorities identiﬁed 2408
[pdf-4e9f5288370d996289 | p.81] (e.g., launching of new satellites and collection of more in-situ measure- 2409
[pdf-9633c3d3b92dd6c626 | p.81] ments etc.) need to be balanced against their environmental footprint, with 2410
[pdf-c3ac24f10267b8bbd7 | p.81] a view to identify means by which it can be reduced and mitigated. 2411
[pdf-d31bd1150e7b268be1 | p.81] • Carbon and environmental footprints of space technology. There is an 2412
[pdf-b7127d92d30ca8c462 | p.81] increasing number of satellites being launched into space. Although much 2413
[pdf-7ee70f4cad0e0eb192 | p.81] of this growth is for internet services, Earth Observation satellites are also 2414
[pdf-5bcb4f60bd0a2e27df | p.81] increasing in numbers, with increasing amounts of space junk. This raises 2415
[pdf-da7f16df5747b4c750 | p.81] questions on the environmental impacts of satellites and space technologies 2416
[pdf-32035d85972b227ebf | p.81] more generally throughout their complete lifetimes that have previously not 2417
[pdf-8b51faaeb8ff68141a | p.81] been a concern (from construction, to rocket launch and being placed into 2418
[pdf-722f22392c1f8b8b89 | p.81] orbit and use, de-orbiting and removal) (Shutler et al., 2022). 2419
[pdf-6858547fafdd2f1841 | p.81] • Use of satellite products for informing ocean carbon dioxide removal 2420
[pdf-6b2b8ed8195d7cd87e | p.81] (CDR) studies. Satellites will play a role in future monitoring of potential 2421
[pdf-9d9ebb1fec6106ad5c | p.81] implementations of CDR, for understanding the consequences that some 2422
[pdf-3272a9d341917a2d88 | p.81] of these proposed mechanism would have on the marine ecosystem (Boyd 2423
[pdf-658672aefe6229757a | p.81] et al., 2022; National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine, 2424
[pdf-0ec8863bcbd78a631d | p.81] 2022). 2425
[pdf-17f56fa6a38d571d3e | p.81] • Economic valuation of the satellite based information. Quantifying the 2426
[pdf-2f22c0e91030692c9d | p.81] value of satellite based information would be useful for a range of applica- 2427
[pdf-24ca449fb1ae47eb9e | p.81] tions, including climate and carbon management strategies and solutions 2428
[pdf-f45867b8f2e36da0da | p.81] (e.g., CDR), and for understanding environmental footprints. 2429
[pdf-7d1e7e18778e95ebb6 | p.81] • Need to consider how satellites can be used to help monitor cycles of 2430
[pdf-c88d565f00a68648ee | p.81] other important climatically-relevant compounds and elements. For 2431
[pdf-2825111b5825571b5b | p.81] example, methane (CH4) emissions have contributed almost one quarter of 2432
[pdf-6a4bb52a90272e7cf5 | p.81] the cumulative radiative forcings for CO2, CH4, and N2O (nitrous oxide) 2433
[pdf-9dc204d705db3534d9 | p.81] combined since 1750 (Etminan et al., 2016), and absorbs thermal infrared 2434
[pdf-159b26f09288154e28 | p.81] 81

## p.82 — Page 82

[pdf-eed9f58a74dfedad25 | p.82] radiation much more eﬃciently than CO2. 2435
[pdf-a92aea1a151da07dc0 | p.82] • Open Science. It is essential that our community follows a transparent, 2436
[pdf-e92d21088038ea46e3 | p.82] open science approach, promoting data sharing and knowledge transfer, and 2437
[pdf-42654d8be2f577ff0e | p.82] committing to FAIR principles (https://www.go-fair.org/fair-principles/). 2438
[pdf-16b04173d56cd2c48f | p.82] Supporting open-access repositories for publications, data and code, and 2439
[pdf-148f66b7bab70875a0 | p.82] openly available education resources, for the next generations of scientists. 2440
[pdf-f781c159e37eb5a30c | p.82] • Promote diversity and inclusivity. Geosciences are one of the least di- 2441
[pdf-f80651230faab83d42 | p.82] verse branches of STEM. And while it was positive to see the high gender 2442
[pdf-4350937c20fca0fa02 | p.82] diversity at this meeting (Figure 1), more is needed to promote the po- 2443
[pdf-3d4625d6e1567aa889 | p.82] sition of the under-represented minorities in our ﬁeld. There has been a 2444
[pdf-9c934e44a5850d1631 | p.82] disproportionate impact of climate change on historically marginalized and 2445
[pdf-cc3e966087bbc75aea | p.82] under-represented community’s worldwide (IOCCG, 2019). System wide 2446
[pdf-2cb774f06b83c6d779 | p.82] changes need to be implemented, where diversity, inclusion, cohesion, and 2447
[pdf-ca620d3df5fc34388d | p.82] equality across the ocean research (with special emphasis on ﬁeld safety) 2448
[pdf-4981a0e529f31908bc | p.82] are a priority. 2449
[pdf-b8b1bf9c2c5bebcbb0 | p.82] • Prioritise infrastructure in space-based assets for improved observation 2450
[pdf-2bafe864a5afd0604e | p.82] of ocean carbon on multiple scales. It is critical we continue to explore new 2451
[pdf-697f84166936f496a4 | p.82] and innovative ways to remotely monitor the pools and ﬂuxes of carbon in 2452
[pdf-7643d6edf63b807b3a | p.82] the ocean on multiple scales. This requires investment in basic/fundamental 2453
[pdf-3408066497c7999633 | p.82] research on the interactions among light, water and carbon, and working 2454
[pdf-d27a97036e73db5ee0 | p.82] with a wide network of stakeholders to target and address some of the 2455
[pdf-efad0ed42982967ccc | p.82] challenges and gaps highlighted. 2456
[pdf-e25a7201f9c377be82 | p.82] • Harness the power of quantum computing. Our community should be 2457
[pdf-404c64031ca7f5cb99 | p.82] poised to take advantage of developments in quantum computing, which 2458
[pdf-9376f8033279c6d890 | p.82] has the potential to radically change our ability to process and integrate a 2459
[pdf-eb4d7c8ee947f03f53 | p.82] range of diﬀerent data (models, satellite and in situ) not possible with high 2460
[pdf-463ba6d40cf9875af4 | p.82] performance computing. 2461
[pdf-545332bd4648436502 | p.82] 82

## p.83 — Page 83

[pdf-f86c6e4376cf537c44 | p.83] 7. Summary 2462
[pdf-fea455ea7464f9047e | p.83] We organised a workshop on the topic of ocean carbon from space with the 2463
[pdf-ead4283230dcdb7a12 | p.83] aim to produce a collective view of status of the ﬁeld and to deﬁne priorities 2464
[pdf-d85336c81e8dfaf896 | p.83] for the next decade. Leading experts were assembled from around the world, 2465
[pdf-5ae1c56fcf20557a74 | p.83] including those working with remote-sensing data, with ﬁeld data and with 2466
[pdf-3b671f8fe0236ae45b | p.83] models. Inorganic and organic pools of carbon (in dissolved and particulate 2467
[pdf-49131be7782fcd692f | p.83] form) were targeted, as well ﬂuxes between pools and at interfaces. Cross- 2468
[pdf-f0f586559e3551441c | p.83] cutting activities were also discussed, including blue carbon, extreme events and 2469
[pdf-62526aa5f545e34f09 | p.83] carbon budgets. Common priorities should focus on improvements in: in-situ 2470
[pdf-4477fab3490e448629 | p.83] observations, satellite algorithm retrievals, uncertainty quantifying, understanding 2471
[pdf-8e216658801ead6ad5 | p.83] of vertical distributions, collaboration with modellers, ways to bridge spatial and 2472
[pdf-df484c3d86f430c779 | p.83] temporal scales of the diﬀerent data sources, fundamental understanding of the 2473
[pdf-3dd1fe62dc8f4bb38a | p.83] ocean carbon cycle, and on carbon and light interactions. Priorities were also 2474
[pdf-f5ce0324f765333a1b | p.83] reported for the speciﬁc pools and ﬂuxes studied, and we highlight emerging 2475
[pdf-f8e5b8eae21aa95ce6 | p.83] concerns that arose during discussions, around the carbon footprint of research 2476
[pdf-d865c2422241850f2f | p.83] and space technology, the role of satellites in CDR approaches, the economic 2477
[pdf-a1b855e49a135758d8 | p.83] valuation of the satellite based information, to consider how satellites can be used 2478
[pdf-4e71e7ba818f7ed704 | p.83] to help monitor the cycles of other climatically-relevant compounds and elements, 2479
[pdf-18251b0e08012c25bd | p.83] the need to promote diversity and inclusivity, bringing communities working 2480
[pdf-cd9763b7218fb4b45c | p.83] on diﬀerent aspects of ocean carbon together, open science, to explore new and 2481
[pdf-935b1581ab68201c20 | p.83] innovative ways to remotely monitor ocean carbon, and harness developments in 2482
[pdf-c882d667b94c24ac63 | p.83] quantum computing. 2483

## p.157 — Page 157

[pdf-d633a6ac5bb0f48143 | p.157] Table 2: A selection of recently launched or upcoming satellite sensors with applications in ocean carbon research and monitoring.
[pdf-b508cf6cb28447cc31 | p.157] Sensor Description & Reference Pool/ﬂux of carbon
[pdf-7c37f7f2454ca43445 | p.157] Plankton, Aerosol, Cloud, ocean Ecosystem (PACE)
[pdf-a627e23bcd848958ad | p.157] PACE will have a hyperspectral Ocean Color In-
[pdf-81d425b9fc0713a08a | p.157] strument (OCI), measuring in the UV, visible, near infrared, and several shortwave infrared bands. It will also contain two multi-wavelength, multi- angle imaging polarimeters for improved quantiﬁ- cation of atmospheric aerosols and ocean particles (Remer et al., 2019a,b). PACE is scheduled to launch in 2024 (https://pace.gsfc.nasa.gov).
[pdf-97d906951c20ff4bf0 | p.157] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, BC, EEs
[pdf-744dba1e425e26b6ad | p.157] Geosynchronous Littoral Imaging and Monitoring Radiometer (GLIMR)
[pdf-9d5e1c09d9d91162d8 | p.157] GLIMR is a geostationary and hyperspectral ocean colour satellite that will observe coastal oceans in the Gulf of Mexico, portions of the south-eastern US coastline, and the Amazon River plume. It will provide multiple observations (hourly), at around 300 m resolution across the UV-NIR range (340 -1040 nm). GLIMR is expected to be launched in 2027 (https://eospso.nasa.gov/ missions/geosynchronous-littoral-imaging-and- monitoring-radiometer-evi-5).
[pdf-285db6378f5a8bbab5 | p.157] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, BC, EEs
[pdf-3c7c8d0e40b2297b4b | p.157] Environmental Mapping and Analysis Program (EnMAP)
[pdf-5e636fca069fa9cfea | p.157] EnMAP is a German hyperspectral satellite mis- sion measuring at high spatial resolution (30 m)
[pdf-f7e96097ba28918ddd | p.157] from 420-1000 nm in the visible and near-infrared, and from 900 nm to 2450 nm in the shortwave in- frared. It aims to monitor and characterise Earth’s environment on a global scale. It was launched in April 2022 (https://www.enmap.org).
[pdf-b3f0481066c66c1398 | p.157] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, BC, EEs
[pdf-2589430582541ed54a | p.157] FLuorescence EXplorer (FLEX)
[pdf-a2ae6bfcc60d10c71f | p.157] FLEX is a mission designed to accurately measure ﬂuorescence, and provide global maps of vegeta- tion ﬂuorescence that reﬂect photosynthetic activ- ity and plant health and stress, which is impor- tant for understanding of the global carbon cy- cle. FLEX is expected to be launched in 2025 (https://earth.esa.int/eogateway/missions/ﬂex).
[pdf-160e9e987d2aa0b4f3 | p.157] BC, EEs
[pdf-d2b14b56715b4b4571 | p.157] Continued on the next page.

## p.158 — Page 158

[pdf-76f532f0ea1dfc5716 | p.158] Table 2: A selection of recently launched or upcoming satellite sensors with applications in ocean carbon research and monitoring.
[pdf-d2ddfb1ddd60f17c78 | p.158] Sensor Description & Reference Pool/ﬂux of carbon
[pdf-dc58780f8077a060c4 | p.158] Sentinel-4 (S-4) S4 mission consists of an Ultraviolet-Visible-Near- Infrared (UVN) light imaging spectrometer instru- ment embarked to be onboard the Meteosat Third Generation Sounder (MTG-S) satellite. It will pro- vide geostationary data over European waters and planned to be launched in 2023 (https://sentinel. esa.int/web/sentinel/missions/sentinel-4).
[pdf-225f977c1829513f1a | p.158] IC (air-sea gas inter- actions)
[pdf-f8a04821bd0971281a | p.158] Sentinel-5 (S-5) S5 mission consists of a hyperspectral spectrometer
[pdf-d64c4302da44141a55 | p.158] system operating in the UV, visible and shortwave- infrared range. Though focused primarily on re- trieving information on the composition of the at- mosphere, it can retrieve information on ocean colour. Preliminary applications using the precur- sor mission (S-5p, launched in October 2017), has demonstrated retrieval of diﬀuse attenuation (Kd) in the blue and UV regions. Owing to the hyper- spectral nature of the instrument, it also has appli-
[pdf-c25056ce4fab4ce2e7 | p.158] cations in deriving information on the composition of the phytoplankton in the ocean (e.g., Bracher et al., 2017) (https://sentinel.esa.int/web/sentinel/ missions/sentinel-5).
[pdf-a0604ef415bf90abb2 | p.158] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, EEs
[pdf-2f87a90965ecacc313 | p.158] Copernicus Hyperspectral Imaging Mission for the Environment (CHIME)
[pdf-4a74e11bee5e4ff58c | p.158] CHIME will provide routine hyperspectral obser- vations from the visible to shortwave infrared.
[pdf-efc3f87581a4ce8365 | p.158] The mission will complement Copernicus Sentinel- 2 satellite for high resolution optical mapping. Planned to be launched in the second half of
[pdf-4e299c28a62f6353ed | p.158] this decade (https://www.esa.int/ESA_Multimedia/ Images/2020/11/CHIME).
[pdf-cefc4f7520a64c5e02 | p.158] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, BC, EEs

## p.159 — Page 159

[pdf-b4ea60433dda0382b2 | p.159] Table 2: A selection of recently launched or upcoming satellite sensors with applications in ocean carbon research and monitoring.
[pdf-db720fa9eb51adbd85 | p.159] Sensor Description & Reference Pool/ﬂux of carbon
[pdf-c903f02d65b7795175 | p.159] Earth Cloud, Aerosol and Radiation Explorer (Earth- CARE)
[pdf-4d168a925d1b9d8e1d | p.159] EarthCARE will contain an atmospheric lidar, cloud proﬁling radar, a multi-spectral imager, and a broad-band radiometer, with the objective to al- low scientists to study the relationship of clouds, aerosols, oceans and radiation. It is planned for launch in 2023 (https://earth.esa.int/eogateway/ missions/earthcare).
[pdf-9128cf9e9df743d274 | p.159] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, BC, EEs
[pdf-78a61a6471cbc5a069 | p.159] Surface Water and Ocean To- pography Mission (SWOT)
[pdf-712b6f23565041ec02 | p.159] SWOT will contain a wide-swath altimeter that will collect data on ocean heights to study currents and eddies up to ﬁve times smaller than have been pre- viously been detectable. It was launched on 16th
[pdf-2bb39c760f96905005 | p.159] December 2022 (https://swot.jpl.nasa.gov/mission/ overview/).
[pdf-edbf11be798a08841d | p.159] IC, EEs
[pdf-aa545a2cb6b4ee75a3 | p.159] Satélite de Aplicaciones Basadas en la Informa- ción Ambiental del Mar (SABIA-Mar)
[pdf-86f1b7eb0bd31a6e98 | p.159] SABIA-Mar was conceived to observe water colour in the open ocean (global scenario, 800 m resolution) and coastal areas of South America (regional scenario, 200 m resolution) and provide
[pdf-6173bcfb783b0bb9a5 | p.159] information about primary productivity, carbon cy- cle, marine habitats and biodiversity, ﬁsheries re- sources, water quality, coastal hazards, and land cover/land use. The satellite will carry two push- broom radiometers covering a 1496 km swath and measuring in 13 spectral bands from 412 to 1600 nm. SABIA-Mar is scheduled to be launched in 2024 (https://www.argentina.gob.ar/ciencia/conae/ misiones-espaciales/sabia-mar).
[pdf-f456fc5f2371425432 | p.159] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, BC, EEs

## p.160 — Page 160

[pdf-54c61aa4b5faf6aa90 | p.160] Table 2: A selection of recently launched or upcoming satellite sensors with applications in ocean carbon research and monitoring.
[pdf-5e2e9747d9109a4b56 | p.160] Sensor Description & Reference Pool/ﬂux of carbon
[pdf-4fac6d1d9438bb72de | p.160] Surface Biology and Geol- ogy (SBG)
[pdf-7616ff5ba9a0859ca4 | p.160] SGB is being designed to address, via visible to shortwave imaging spectroscopy, terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems and other elements of biodi- versity, geology, volcanoes, the water cycle, and applied topics of social beneﬁt. In the current ar- chitecture considered, the instrument payload will consist of a hyperspectral imager measuring at 30-
[pdf-0878ec2e00eb583239 | p.160] 45 m resolution in >200 spectral bands from 380 to 2250 nm and a thermal infrared imager measuring at 40-60 m resolution in >5 spectral bands from 3 to 5 and 8 to 12 microns, with revisit of 2-16 and 1-7 days, respectively. Launch is scheduled for 2026 (https://sbg.jpl.nasa.gov).
[pdf-a7ac8a90aa7d9b83af | p.160] PP, POC, C-phyto, DOC, IC, BC, EEs
[pdf-68bcd8a2c3a17546ce | p.160] MetOp-SG Multi-Viewing Multi-Channel Multi- Polarisation Imaging (3MI) instrument
[pdf-448a3f8e3b37de0adf | p.160] 3MI is a passive optical radiometer with large swath (2200 km) dedicated primarily to aerosol characterization for applications in climate mon- itoring, atmospheric chemistry, and numerical weather prediction, but with ocean colour capa- bility. It will provide multi-spectral (12 spectral bands from 410 to 2130 nm), multi-polarization (+60 deg., 0 deg., and -0 deg.), and multi-angular
[pdf-f1ced959cccb6aa578 | p.160] (14 directions) views of a Earth target at 4 km reso- lution. The ﬁrst MetOp-SG A-series satellite car- rying 3MI will be launched in 2024, the second in 2031, and the third in 2038 (https://earth.esa.int/ web/eoportal/satellite-missions/m/metop-sg).

## p.161 — Page 161

[pdf-06f31cdda345415e1e | p.161] Table 3: Priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities for satellite estimates of primary production (PP).
[pdf-34cb9a66c1df825649 | p.161] Priority Challenges Gaps Opportunities
[pdf-72d3abbb4d37be3161 | p.161] (1) Parametri- sation of satellite algo- rithms using in-situ data
[pdf-d74967dda72555ee6a | p.161] • Representing the spatial and tem- poral variability of model param- eters.
[pdf-e0caaab38ca91b4227 | p.161] • Continued ﬁnancial support for in-situ observations.
[pdf-1070f982b769ebb6b1 | p.161] • Standard conversion factors and measurement protocols.
[pdf-afe13f9341eb827da0 | p.161] • Diurnal variability in parame- ters and variables assumed (mod- elled).
[pdf-dd06505d2acd3215f6 | p.161] • Spatial and temporal gaps in PP parameters.
[pdf-b6756ac812d3ebe60c | p.161] • Lack of continuous measure- ments.
[pdf-215780fac2ae74fcc3 | p.161] • Better coordination at interna- tional level required.
[pdf-5547cb0f64a359bc74 | p.161] • Spatial biases in estuar- ine/coastal in-situ PP data.
[pdf-4c5fc5e032e53ef23e | p.161] • Active ﬂuorescence-based meth- ods and oxygen optode sensors on novel in-situ platforms.
[pdf-2b3b2d19d0797aaa8b | p.161] • Synergy across in-situ data sources (multi-platform sen- sors).
[pdf-e84667cdf38e3d072a | p.161] • Use of artiﬁcial intelligence tech- niques for mapping model pa- rameters.
[pdf-03dc1dd1e26e12eba9 | p.161] • Commercial partnerships and technological innovation of in- situ sensors and platforms.
[pdf-f9182222190a4942b2 | p.161] • Exploit geostationary platforms to resolve diurnal variability in light and biomass.
[pdf-83dcf4b8e861207566 | p.161] • Formulate priorities for fund- ing (long-term time series, novel measurements).
[pdf-bfefe11a8e45ce755b | p.161] (2) Un- certainty estimation and valida- tion
[pdf-35c3fe50010f05bd7d | p.161] • Validation of satellite-based pri- mary production estimates is challenging.
[pdf-db12e3f9fa979023e1 | p.161] • Uncertainty estimates satellite- based products are not readily provided.
[pdf-0e7a8ae0e43423abcb | p.161] • Gaps in in-situ data for valida- tion.
[pdf-084a84d05eae5246fd | p.161] • Gaps in our understanding of un- certainty in key input variables and parameters.
[pdf-930791a58690c3498d | p.161] • Data gaps in satellite observa- tions.
[pdf-249fd63cc9e6a23e7f | p.161] • Enhanced computational capac- ity to run models for uncertainty estimation.
[pdf-49fd14c8d104fddceb | p.161] • Use of emerging (hyperspectral, geostationary, lidar) sensors.
[pdf-7e8bf4f6671a571b1b | p.161] • Validation opportunities with au- tonomous platforms.
[pdf-dfe5b466ce54eb8b65 | p.161] Continued on the next page.
[pdf-3796866b826c5e9a8b | p.161] 161

## p.162 — Page 162

[pdf-04f0de616757c87418 | p.162] Table 3. Priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities for satellite estimates of primary production (PP). (continued from previous page).
[pdf-8c67d9683b1d06c6d6 | p.162] Priority Challenges Gaps Opportunities
[pdf-087c5652191888ecae | p.162] (3) Link- ing surface satellite mea- surements to vertical distribution
[pdf-a1c8e92babf4644345 | p.162] • Resolving vertical structure of PP, Chl-a, and PAR. • Lack of high spatial-temporal vertical in-situ data
[pdf-265418a864728da00d | p.162] • Need for better physical prod- ucts (e.g., mixed-layer depth) with uncertainties.
[pdf-53a1cc105b0bd69698 | p.162] • Improve (basic) understanding of vertical structure.
[pdf-b95c82c816029cefc8 | p.162] • Beneﬁt from use of novel in-situ platforms.
[pdf-cd70bde3866697fe56 | p.162] • Beneﬁt from future satellite lidar systems.
[pdf-16007a441b7b646fc3 | p.162] (4) Trends • Diﬃculty in assessing direction of change in trends of PP.
[pdf-b207cdf722364a4f88 | p.162] • Dealing with noise in non-linear systems.
[pdf-03785281fca1fad69b | p.162] • Uncertainty estimates of satellite-based PP are not provided.
[pdf-c243c6190380b74b99 | p.162] • Length of satellite record not suf- ﬁcient for climate change stud- ies.
[pdf-c865eeb3c06554fac6 | p.162] • Need for consistent and continu- ous satellite records for climate research.
[pdf-df274ec86d80d7847f | p.162] • Assimilation of satellite data into models.
[pdf-df986c8759d0c78153 | p.162] (5) Fun- damental understand- ing
[pdf-8f5685ff8b361e84ca | p.162] • Better understand relationships among PP, community structure and environment.
[pdf-f81d87d15434436e73 | p.162] • Better understand feedbacks be- tween physics and biology.
[pdf-51481858395ef85147 | p.162] • Understand the fate of PP (i.e., secondary and export produc- tion).
[pdf-83635b16237a347bd2 | p.162] • Better understand the interac- tions of PP in diﬀerent compo- nents of the Earth System.
[pdf-17db73e4a9742ec39c | p.162] • Improved quantiﬁcation of new production and net community production from space.
[pdf-cde8efac1d925c049e | p.162] • Need for higher spatial and temporal resolution products to study diurnal variability.
[pdf-438e8994c6354a2e9e | p.162] • Include inland and coastal wa- ters.
[pdf-eb9449d74c4b7f5a34 | p.162] • Gaps in satellite information on data sets relevant to photochemi- cal reactions.
[pdf-e53f1ded6a2c06302a | p.162] • Better understanding of viral control on PP.
[pdf-f6c50fe06bee9968f1 | p.162] • Unifying the integration of pri- mary production across inter- faces (e.g. land and ocean).
[pdf-159cee59efc2844249 | p.162] • Regional models/algorithms with aim to merge/nest models for larger scale estimates.
[pdf-9cc9e9c4ac66ae47b2 | p.162] • Harness developments in quan- tum computing.
[pdf-a89e6bcb77528c6ef9 | p.162] • Meet challenges of the UN Ocean Decade.
[pdf-5531cdd48c0086efbb | p.162] • Harness novel algorithms and satellites (hyperspectral, lidar and geostationary).
[pdf-5dcd8a664f4e03923a | p.162] • Harness satellite instruments covering the UV spectral range for insight into photodegrada- tion.

[pdf-fbbdc9a975db1aed71 | p.163] Table 4: Priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities for satellite Particulate Organic Carbon (POC) estimates
[pdf-c4e16d65c51af3bc84 | p.163] Priority Challenges Gaps Opportunities
[pdf-4baa4f042f4878f272 | p.163] (1) In situ measurement methodology
[pdf-7961a89e5f9440f68f | p.163] • Inclusion of particles of all sizes to determine total POC.
[pdf-ab283465bad9a919e3 | p.163] • Quantifying contributions of diﬀerently-sized particles and diﬀerent particle types.
[pdf-2e695e7efd4df1176a | p.163] • Dealing with biases due to DOC in ﬁlters.
[pdf-5fe5a9a3d23a533631 | p.163] • Submicrometer and rare large particles under-represented in the standard ﬁltration method.
[pdf-952b11d9f1161c84e9 | p.163] • No capability to measure contri- butions of diﬀerently-sized parti- cles and diﬀerent particle types.
[pdf-d202cfccc1d88538b2 | p.163] • A lack of a certiﬁed reference material for POC.
[pdf-366b799af7a125db82 | p.163] • Advance and standardise meth- ods for improved measurement of total POC.
[pdf-eb2e5ef2c93fd53567 | p.163] • Develop measurement capabili- ties combining particle sizing, particle identiﬁcation, and parti- cle optical properties.
[pdf-26f02f33ff1118ebb0 | p.163] (2) In situ data compila- tion
[pdf-63acebd87692e0a8b0 | p.163] • Quality control and consistency across diverse datasets.
[pdf-2aea2021afa2afe34b | p.163] • Limitations of satellite-in-situ data match-ups (e.g., spatial- temporal scale mismatch, spatial biases).
[pdf-d25ca1febeb55123cf | p.163] • Limitations in documentation of methods in historical datasets.
[pdf-359b2176be413434c5 | p.163] • Best-practice guidelines for data quality control and synthesis ef- forts.
[pdf-75d7d6c0f2bbb1912c | p.163] • Under-sampled environments.
[pdf-b5cfe98fb62836e37d | p.163] • Improve and standardise best practices for documentation, quality control, sharing, and data submission into permanent archives.
[pdf-fe1b98daea25d2e6be | p.163] • Collection of high-quality data along the continuum of diverse environments. (3) Satellite algorithm re- trievals
[pdf-c69036260a823c83e0 | p.163] • Uniﬁed algorithms for reliable retrievals from open ocean to coastal and inland water bodies.
[pdf-db32c938b52197d088 | p.163] • Global algorithms applied to en- vironmental conditions outside the intended scope.
[pdf-80b7d6c6f4c8968cb9 | p.163] • Satellite inter-mission consis- tency.
[pdf-9ad7723fc7afc98f24 | p.163] • Atmospheric-correction tailored to a new ocean colour sensors (e.g. geostationary and hyper- spectral).
[pdf-05c494c1a901729718 | p.163] • Mechanistically-based ﬂags as- sociated with optical water types to ensure appropriate application of algorithms.
[pdf-e7e22faeba65f4a13f | p.163] • Advanced algorithms (e.g., adap- tive based on mechanistic princi- ples) to enable reliable retrievals across diverse environments.
[pdf-a7589085f421eb9435 | p.163] • Opportunities to harness a new suite of empirical satellite sensor-speciﬁc global POC algorithms.
[pdf-d166b1516090be3764 | p.163] • Use of satellite geostationary and hyperspectral data in combi- nation with in-situ data.

[pdf-95f0ae1fb71f9946b7 | p.164] (4) Partition- ing into size

[pdf-b76f73541f0cf14266 | p.164] • Ability to reliably measure in situ various fractions is limited, e.g., separate living vs. non- living POC.

[pdf-00682db2524b0bbe85 | p.164] • Insuﬃcient knowledge of IOPs for optics-based partitioning of POC.

[pdf-4f97c0fdeed71a5038 | p.164] • Emerging techniques to separate living and non-living POC.
[pdf-409f72538306f1c303 | p.164] • Support PSD measurements as part of a suite of basic required measurements.

[pdf-75631790c18c4bc758 | p.165] (5) Vertical proﬁles
[pdf-e533deb1537e9fb3d3 | p.165] • Reconstructing vertical proﬁles using data from space-borne, air- borne, and in-situ sensors.
[pdf-c9cec5af249a07c4e7 | p.165] • Determining relationship(s) be- tween remotely-sensed variables and characteristics of the POC vertical proﬁle.
[pdf-4596fbc0192b9d10c0 | p.165] • Relationships between optical variables and POC (e.g., from sensors on autonomous in-situ platforms).
[pdf-7d6e30bc3da07f88ab | p.165] • Uneven distribution of in-situ proﬁles of POC globally.
[pdf-49c6010103a7a5bd98 | p.165] • Development of POC algorithms for in-situ optical data (e.g., BGC-Argo) along with improve- ments of optical sensor technol- ogy (e.g., polarized scattering sensors for BGC-Argo).
[pdf-d66aff9188a40838e6 | p.165] • Use multiple data (satellite, BGC-Argo) and model streams to reconstruct 3D and 4D POC in the ocean via statistical and data assimilation techniques.
[pdf-97b4f7458902b74011 | p.165] • Advance basic research to determine relationships among remote-sensing reﬂectance and other optical variables and vertical proﬁles of POC characteristics (e.g., PSD).
[pdf-cfd5126e96237fb7c8 | p.165] • Harness lidar-based remote sens- ing.

[pdf-4ff9b584b39f40cef8 | p.165] • Quantifying the vertical ﬂux of POC a major challenge.
[pdf-07543f4695a7b68ae9 | p.165] • Measurements of gravitational sinking of POC are work- intensive and rely on simpliﬁed assumptions.
[pdf-7a70343d32fb79220e | p.165] • Measuring the migrant and mix- ing pumps is demanding.
[pdf-01fe10eef3d5e531cb | p.165] • Sparsity of in-situ data on verti- cal ﬂuxes of POC.
[pdf-616ea57fb837e348cb | p.165] • Interannual variation in vertical ﬂuxes of POC poorly known.
[pdf-6c23c195e7a898070c | p.165] • Gaps in understanding of POC ﬂuxes in shallow and shelf seas.
[pdf-f7c405d8cc8fe29b2d | p.165] • Gaps in understanding on mi- grant and mixing pumps.
[pdf-d15e72bfff07d4caf5 | p.165] • Harness autonomous sensors and emerging observation tech- niques (e.g., “optical sediment traps” on BGC-Argo ﬂoats).
[pdf-a058f8403f5f57710c | p.165] • Harnessing new statistical ap- proaches (e.g., machine learn- ing).
[pdf-bbf779ecac9795a1a8 | p.165] • Constraining prognostic ocean BGC models using observations from remote and in-situ au- tonomous sensors.

[pdf-b82e81e1324610ade8 | p.166] (1) In-situ data • Extremely diﬃcult to measure C- phyo in situ.
[pdf-c62570a9c49b81ed9e | p.166] • Challenges quantifying photoac- climation parameters and their variability at large scales.
[pdf-d38d31102abd9e4ddc | p.166] • Challenges around standardiza- tion of phytoplankton carbon data submission using emerging in-situ techniques.
[pdf-6500cceb1145d1958e | p.166] • Gaps in accurate in-situ C-phyto data.
[pdf-f8c4e3f0e2c31a8374 | p.166] • Gaps in consistent C-phyto sur- face time-series data sets.
[pdf-4d33dcd62a8ede41d6 | p.166] • Gaps in photo-acclimation pa- rameters.
[pdf-c0185c0d0921b7dfd8 | p.166] • The enlargement and explo- ration of data analysis of in situ supersites.
[pdf-9ee1b7ee32ba13a4d2 | p.166] • Empower validation through autonomous mobile platforms (e.g., BGC-Argo ﬂoats and Lagrangian drifters).
[pdf-78b22b4d058fc4aea6 | p.166] • Connecting new genetic level data with phytoplankton carbon properties.

[pdf-04003bf3137058b9e8 | p.166] • Separating the contributions of living and non-living particles to the particle backscattering coeﬃ- cient.
[pdf-17782bfaccb0a7f635 | p.166] • Understanding the inﬂuence of phytoplankton composition and photoacclimation on the rela- tionships among Chl-a, particle backscatter and C-phyto.
[pdf-8b9f37f954014fd60d | p.166] • A gap in our mechanistic under- standing of how optical proper- ties and particle types link to C- phyto.
[pdf-afde20ed5072138485 | p.166] • Uncertainties infrequently re- ported with satellite C-phyto products.
[pdf-65cb7020789058af23 | p.166] • Harness long time-series satel- lite products.
[pdf-6aded68057325f5455 | p.166] • Explore the combined use of satellite data with ecosystem modelling.
[pdf-345f0200819635e03b | p.166] • Combining models of photoac- climation with size-based ap- proaches and models of PP, for consistent carbon pools and ﬂuxes. (3) Vertical structure
[pdf-c02a0172738cfcef2f | p.166] • Challenging to collect, aggre- gate and produce an in-situ dataset that is representative of entire euphotic depth and at global scale.
[pdf-4cf0898cb61612bdd1 | p.166] • Biases towards in-situ C-phyto data collected at surface depths.
[pdf-6b741db99455bfd60e | p.166] • Lack of methods for extrapolat- ing the surface satellite C-phyto products down through the en- tire euphotic zone.
[pdf-d1f2887ff2808987d1 | p.166] • Use autonomous platforms such as BGC-Argo ﬂoats and moor- ings with satellite data and mod- els to reconstruct the 4D views of C-phyto.

## p.167 — Page 167

[pdf-075f1a715592e0914f | p.167] Table 6: Priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities for satellite detection of Dissolved Organic Carbon (DOC).
[pdf-a7442be4f2ddf653fc | p.167] Priority Challenges Gaps Opportunities
[pdf-2d4a0aee7591c3b234 | p.167] (1) Spatial and temporal coverage of the coastal ocean
[pdf-636117b786e6b1c54f | p.167] • Quantifying DOC stocks and ﬂuxes in coastal waters require data with high temporal cover- age.
[pdf-cf106be66dec237dea | p.167] • Atmospheric-correction of ocean colour data in coastal waters.
[pdf-4ad44473a2513cbb44 | p.167] • Viewing high latitudes regions from space in winter months.
[pdf-0f8effa7d4c7122cd9 | p.167] • Estimates of DOC stocks and ﬂuxes in coastal environments limited by the temporal coverage of existing satellites.
[pdf-e03327b1f6c9a5324b | p.167] • Geostationary ocean-colour satellites, capable of imaging multiple times daily.
[pdf-47a570ce7076f742f1 | p.167] • Future satellite ocean-colour constellations may improve temporal coverage.
[pdf-b4ff09a84465db7880 | p.167] (2) Under- standing and constraining the relation- ship between CDOM and DOC
[pdf-79fdfe5be51b49a603 | p.167] • Improved performance of satel- lite CDOM absorption retrievals is required.
[pdf-74edf7ecf3b95028c8 | p.167] • Relationships between DOC and CDOM absorption tends to be variable seasonally and across coastal systems.
[pdf-6af4c6014dbe17cee3 | p.167] • CDOM and DOC are largely de- coupled in the open ocean.
[pdf-d27ac639a59f78f5f2 | p.167] • High sensitivity to atmospheric correction (e.g., eﬀects of Rayleigh scattering).
[pdf-cc1626ffe29b0a2ef5 | p.167] • Gaps in our understanding of the relationship between DOC and CDOM absorption.
[pdf-8e99d744124ceda56c | p.167] • There is a lack satellite UV and hyperspectral data for resolving DOC and its composition.
[pdf-94616dea47e933ccc6 | p.167] • Reliable atmosphere-correction is needed for UV and shortwave visible wavelengths.
[pdf-c0e5e78f707c59f2f6 | p.167] • Utilise the spectral slope of CDOM absorption to constrain the variability between CDOM and DOC.
[pdf-d59cf3ca19afca8672 | p.167] • New insight on the eﬀects of photobleaching may provide op- portunities for mechanistic mod- els of the processes regulating the relationship between CDOM and DOC.
[pdf-57c89511c83da84132 | p.167] • Harness opportunities to acquire high-quality ﬁeld measurements of DOC and CDOM absorption.
[pdf-6a0090b35bcf273df0 | p.167] • Emerging UV and hyperspectral satellites will open opportunities for CDOM and DOC retrievals.
[pdf-50ac9b485b391250e9 | p.167] • Harness optical water type frameworks for algorithms selection and merging for better separation of NAP-CDOM eﬀects.
[pdf-136ee2ba93ffcbbf7e | p.167] Continued on the next page.

[pdf-4111d558c83fbc09c9 | p.168] (3) Identi- ﬁcation of sources and reactivity
[pdf-6339cb49d301da4121 | p.168] • Challenging to identify speciﬁc pools of DOC of diﬀerent sources and reactivity.
[pdf-b37111ea9b57c58a3b | p.168] • Few studies assessing whether the DOM ﬂuoresced signal can be detected from ocean colour.
[pdf-b8e4231a289e0579f7 | p.168] • Whether the ﬂuorescence of DOC and CDOM can have a measurable inﬂuence on remote- sensing reﬂectance.
[pdf-eab438f133df5b6d0f | p.168] • Hyperspectral sensors will pro- vide improved signal-to-noise ra- tio, atmospheric corrections, as well as enhanced spectral infor- mation in the UV-visible range
[pdf-191dd4506fc37c2978 | p.168] • Opportunities with active remote-sensing approaches based on laser-induced ﬂuores- cence.
[pdf-26471e7481fb9b952e | p.168] (4) Vertical measure- ments
[pdf-809a6c4f4f7f0605cb | p.168] • Remote sensing of CDOM and DOC is limited to surface mea- surements.
[pdf-2026c391752d5a93b8 | p.168] • Approaches that extrapolate sur- face DOC and CDOM to depth require extensive in-situ datasets (vertical proﬁles). Gaps exist for many regions and seasons.
[pdf-72b0c9979ac3096f93 | p.168] • Acquiring in-situ measurements from autonomous platforms like BGC-Argo equipped with DOM- ﬂuorescence sensors and radiom- etry.
[pdf-6d6970d97bbce006db | p.168] • Opportunities with UV-lidar- based techniques to retrieve sub-surface information about CDOM.
[pdf-fdde95e48d056247ce | p.168] • Opportunities to harness mod- elling approaches to improve es- timation of DOC dynamics at depth.

[pdf-2b75e24456213e1b35 | p.169] Table 7: Priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities for satellite detection of inorganic carbon (IC) and ﬂuxes at the ocean interface.
[pdf-9527b17e658d89edab | p.169] Priority Challenges Gaps Opportunities
[pdf-03abb13b6abd34c489 | p.169] (1) In-situ data
[pdf-65c3615c1be5abf6d9 | p.169] • Strong reliance on in-situ data, as many components of IC are not directly observable from space.
[pdf-ec42667053857a62da | p.169] • In-situ data of a much coarser spatial and temporal resolution when compared with satellite data.
[pdf-94ec1c54bb8abe4e7e | p.169] • In-situ data products are heavily extrapolated.
[pdf-aace42ec111f0af35e | p.169] • Challenging to integrate in-situ datasets without community con- sensus on best practices and ref- erence materials.
[pdf-ff5b4548fb8c793332 | p.169] • Better spatial and temporal cov- erage of ﬁeld observations re- quired throughout the water col- umn.
[pdf-ffb31ab5be4501285e | p.169] • Limited in-situ data time-series stations in key locations.
[pdf-96a04a6af4b9894f4e | p.169] • Opportunities to improve the spatial and temporal resolu- tion of in-situ data through autonomous platforms.
[pdf-708decee34df76ad24 | p.169] • Opportunities to extend recent eﬀorts to develop FRM to inor- ganic carbon.
[pdf-2430b8342e5f62e938 | p.169] (2) Satellite retrievals and mapping un- certainty
[pdf-f953f8b3b0e6ccee06 | p.169] • Satellite inorganic carbon esti- mates in optically-complex wa- ter are challenging.
[pdf-06b20bd18d83cc0a56 | p.169] • Challenging to retain the theoret- ical understanding of satellite al- gorithms, while harnessing new powerful statistical approaches (e.g. AI).
[pdf-9d131163e5ae273b54 | p.169] • Lack of pixel-by-pixel uncer- tainty estimates in the satellite inorganic products.
[pdf-2e38c4ba2fd12cb900 | p.169] • Lack of coincident in-situ obser- vations of PIC, other highly scat- tering materials, and IOPs, in optically-complex waters.
[pdf-62678bfd6164e76380 | p.169] • New satellite sensors, with im- proved spatial, spectral and tem- poral resolution, may lead to im- provements in IC satellite prod- ucts.
[pdf-f38e2aabb2803b1f87 | p.169] • Opportunities to harness and build on recent techniques used to map uncertainty.

[pdf-a9536c0be25b7a55a2 | p.171] (4) Mech- anistic understand- ing of gas transfer
[pdf-714a96352c0a354615 | p.171] • Mechanistic understanding of gas transfer is challenged by our ability to measure and quantify key processes.
[pdf-03afa5e0c4da1b872d | p.171] • Large uncertainties surrounding the inﬂuence of near surface tem- perature gradients on gas trans- fer.
[pdf-012906f947a6a3951e | p.171] • Large uncertainty surrounding the importance of bubbles for air- sea CO2 ﬂuxes.
[pdf-942f010f5f93f13db3 | p.171] • Carbon dynamics and air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes in mixed sea ice re- gions are poorly understood.
[pdf-3d2446a9a3e1686fd8 | p.171] • Opportunity to establish FRM status and agree best practice for eddy covariance air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes.
[pdf-990307420fa716e590 | p.171] • Opportunities to exploit state-of- the-art techniques on novel plat- forms to improve understanding of air-sea CO2 ﬂuxes in diﬀerent environments such as mixed sea ice regions.

## p.172 — Page 172

[pdf-79064430a5c997167d | p.172] Table 8: Priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities for satellite detection of Blue Carbon (BC).
[pdf-93edde84c023038c39 | p.172] Priority Challenges Gaps Opportunities
[pdf-6d9ccd1434e2242609 | p.172] (1) Satellite sensors
[pdf-c4ddc318f9cf1989fb | p.172] • Requirement for monitoring at high temporal (hourly) and spa- tial (tidal) scales.
[pdf-813842836720d94165 | p.172] • A lack of long-term satellite datasets for change detection in many BC ecosystems.
[pdf-f861fb8edf46fa2f88 | p.172] • New hyperspectral observations will lead to improved BC detec- tion.
[pdf-0ca922c0b1e63cea56 | p.172] • High spatial resolution (3-5 m) imagery becoming available from a constellation of commer- cial satellites.
[pdf-01faa6c8e13f5d1e6c | p.172] • Geostationary satellite instru- ments will meet the require- ments for high temporal (hourly) BC monitoring.
[pdf-09850c9e573cddced5 | p.172] • Scope to build on eﬀorts to de- velop satellite climate records with a focus on BC.
[pdf-8f4ef4e2314684de9a | p.172] (2) Al- gorithms, retrievals and model integration
[pdf-260b256879ee04c6be | p.172] • Many BC approaches are re- gional, diﬃcult to go to global scales.
[pdf-907c0fd5f9434e90b6 | p.172] • Uncertainty estimation for BC ﬂuxes challenging.
[pdf-b7b1d94e270f2fe8a7 | p.172] • Diﬃcult to monitor the dynam- ics of sediment carbon remotely.
[pdf-d7cf51d493b28a3f0a | p.172] • Dealing with sub-pixel variabil- ity of macroalgae when using courser resolution satellite data.
[pdf-41e98c734c2e831159 | p.172] • Limited availability of in-situ data for development and valida- tion of BC satellite algorithms.
[pdf-bd0c5020e6a9d5c5f1 | p.172] • Lack of BC ecosystem models limits our ability to quantify full BC carbon budgets.
[pdf-dcf4de6ed1cf704beb | p.172] • Harness computation power and statistical analysis of big data.
[pdf-e646036b9100ebae2d | p.172] • Fusion of hyper-spectral optical and SAR data for characteriza- tion of tidal wetlands.
[pdf-18a85d763d545cd208 | p.172] • New in-situ monitoring tech- niques (e.g., drones) are use- ful to bridge the scales between satellites and in-situ observa- tions.
[pdf-af75167f63e4f30466 | p.172] (3) Satel- lite data access and blue carbon accounting
[pdf-f0758833e3f4ce4c03 | p.172] • Existing products and ap- proaches are not easily accessi- ble to non-expert users.
[pdf-e67d530e9caede5895 | p.172] • Challenges to ensure cost- eﬀective monitoring using commercial satellites.
[pdf-a350dec2645236daca | p.172] • Lack of products suited to project development and carbon accounting.
[pdf-1b97b8d9d9c12c190a | p.172] • Products needed at global scales, at higher spatial and temporal resolution.
[pdf-d206461a0e14c0b61c | p.172] • Increasing eﬀorts to develop BC habitat mapping portals that are user friendly.

## p.174 — Page 174

[pdf-ec44cf810d6e56bbc2 | p.174] Table 10: Priorities, challenges, gaps and opportunities for using satellite data for Carbon Budget Closure (CBC).
[pdf-df9367ae9a92ddc6ff | p.174] Priority Challenges Gaps Opportunities
[pdf-352208bf3df18baa45 | p.174] (1) In-situ data
[pdf-71502cb622887a395d | p.174] • Quality, quantity and spatial distribution of in-situ measure- ments varies depending on the pool or ﬂux being studied and measurement platform used.
[pdf-8a342eca94b95f32c6 | p.174] • Very few datasets exist on con- current and co-located in-situ measurements of all the key pools and ﬂuxes needed to evalu- ate model budgets.
[pdf-ae3f82469d588b8b79 | p.174] • Remote regions that play a key role in global budgets (e.g., Southern Ocean) are severely under-sampled.
[pdf-55f862ca28afdd73b7 | p.174] • Gaps in key measurements in many regions (e.g., photosynthe- sis irradiance parameters, for or- ganic carbon budgeting).
[pdf-2a238571f4b3df84b2 | p.174] • New in-situ technologies be- ing integrated into networks of autonomous platforms, for im- proved carbon measurements.
[pdf-66928520de31cbd07f | p.174] • Methods being developed to quantity carbon pools and ﬂuxes from routine optical autonomous observations.
[pdf-64147855b8bfc7bff0 | p.174] • Properties of carbon budgets can be interrogated using in-situ compilations to check and con- strain satellite or model budgets.
[pdf-f388313f1d1c497205 | p.174] (2) Satellite algorithms, budgets and uncertainties
[pdf-419f4c649cd7fed3d8 | p.174] • Coherence in the input satellite data ﬁelds for diﬀerent satellite carbon algorithms needed when computing budgets.
[pdf-7448889fc0c8162dc3 | p.174] • Some of the pools and ﬂuxes of carbon require satellite data with higher spatial, temporal and spectral resolution.
[pdf-75e7e6d3a066a8e850 | p.174] • There needs to be consistency in algorithms used to quantify bud- gets, and these algorithms must respect properties of the ecosys- tem we know from in-situ data.
[pdf-6d1aeb42ddb7bd6cb3 | p.174] • Uncertainties in individual prod- ucts are essential to analyse mul- tiple products to compute the budgets.
[pdf-72b35e367fc2a86b3a | p.174] • Products must be evaluated in re- lation to other products, to see whether they hold together in a coherent fashion.
[pdf-6a5f6ca16015f4739f | p.174] • Many satellite carbon products lack associated estimates of un- certainty.
[pdf-fa15b015d05f69b67c | p.174] • More work is needed to im- prove estimates of uncertainties in model parameters.
[pdf-d3ed2e8d12b9eb4be8 | p.174] • More eﬀorts needed towards dy- namic, rather than static, assign- ment of parameters in carbon al- gorithms.
[pdf-5aed4d6b0b9005abce | p.174] • Harmonising satellite carbon products across diﬀerent plane- tary domains (ocean, land, ice and air) is needed.
[pdf-4f979c62b174516b3c | p.174] • Opportunities to harness climate data records.
[pdf-582832876bc1d4dbb1 | p.174] • Opportunities to harness emerg- ing sensors with increased spec- tral, spatial and temporal resolu- tion.
[pdf-6a96d082446c7ef59f | p.174] • New approaches and statistical techniques oﬀer potential to get at pools and ﬂuxes of carbon not seen from space.
[pdf-7e816daf59ec0e4ed2 | p.174] Continued on the next page.
